Diageo · DGE-GB · the new Memo conveyor's card of 19 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: Diageo

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 43,409 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “57th percentile” means the stock beats 57% of them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

F3515th percentile

Memo topic 1, Tailwinds in the operating environment: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external forces acting on Diageo are a moderate, persistent net headwind. The second derivative has improved (destocking has ended, the Scotch tariff is gone, GLP-1 fears have faded, Chinese comparisons are easy), but the first derivative has not: US consumption of Diageo's brands is still falling at about 6%, competitors are cutting prices into gluts of whiskey, cognac and agave, and power is moving to distributors and retailers. The growing pockets (India premium, Guinness, non-alcoholic, ready-to-drink, travel retail) add roughly 2 to 2.5 points of growth and the US spirits pool subtracts 1 to 2, which is why flat FY2027 and low-single-digit growth thereafter is the right external baseline. For the model this means: no market-driven recovery in North America before FY2028; negative-to-flat price/mix; gross margin flat at best; currency and tariffs a combined drag of roughly $95m on FY2027 operating profit. Upside external surprises (a tariff refund, an Indian customs resolution, a faster US real-income recovery) are plausible but should be treated as scenarios, not the base.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0222C-0547C-0730C-0942C-3545C-3958
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

F224th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Competitive intensity: how much and in which direction is it changing?

Judgement: Competitive intensity has risen sharply over the last 12-18 months and will stay high for at least the next six months, mainly through price. Diageo is both the main cause (it is the largest price-cutter) and the main victim (it is the largest share donor). The offset is that its two most leveraged peers cannot afford a long price war.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Price: retail prices fell by more than 1% on 67-71% of Diageo's US SKUs (Deutsche Bank, March and October 2026), against 18-40% at Pernod Ricard, 13-47% at Brown-Forman, 87-92% at Rémy Cointreau and 7% at LVMH; later data show 72% of the industry's top SKUs cut against only 14% of Campari's major SKUs. US spirits price/mix is -4.5% (Morgan Stanley, November 2026). Diageo cut Casamigos recommended retail prices by 10-15% and is testing 18% on Reposado; Morgan Stanley models a 35% average-selling-price fall for Casamigos and a 6% cut for Don Julio in Mexico; Hennessy VS is down 12.7% since early 2023; Patrón has cut.
  • Gluts that feed promotion: barreled American whiskey stocks have tripled since 2012 (+30% in three years); cognac stocks are at about ten years of depletions; agave spot prices have collapsed (₱30/kg to ₱4-5/kg), so cheap tequila floods the lower tiers.
  • Share: Campari's US off-trade value +8.4% against Diageo -3.5% (September 2026); Diageo -6.1% against a market +0.6% (four weeks to 31 October); Diageo losing share in seven of nine US categories and in segments making up 65% of its North America portfolio. Heineken's relaunched Murphy's took 4.4% of the Great Britain stout market from zero at an 80% price index to Guinness. In India, Pernod grew 19% in Q1 FY2027 against Diageo's 3.4%, and Radico Khaitan guides premium volumes up over 30%.
  • Downstream: retailers' buying alliances (Eurelec, Concordis) resist price rises and can delist across countries; US private label grew 7.6% and small brands 23.3% in 2025; Costco's Kirkland is reshaping club purchasing.
  • Moderating factors: Pernod (3.8x net debt to EBITDA) and Rémy (3.2-3.5x) are balance-sheet constrained; Campari refuses "reductive price wars"; Diageo's management stresses surgical, brand-specific resets, which Redburn says reassured competitors.

For the model: no positive price/mix in North America in FY2027 (management describes the price-ladder work as "an investment in margins"); group price/mix flat to slightly negative in FY2027; A&P cannot fall below about 16% of sales without accelerating share loss.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

C+4767th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Input costs, availability and supplier concentration: headwind or tailwind?

Judgement: A mild headwind over the next six to twelve months (energy, glass, freight, Indian grain spirit) turning to a modest tailwind from FY2028 as lower agave costs reach the income statement and supply-chain savings accrue. Input costs are not the swing factor for Diageo's margin; price investment and overheads are.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Glass: 23% of cost of goods sold and 81% of packaging weight; energy is about 35% of glass-making cost; supply is concentrated (O-I in the US, Ardagh in the UK); European glass capacity has fallen 10-15% (Verallia closures) as demand weakened. Verallia reports neutral cost inflation now, but Jefferies warns higher spot energy could feed through next year. Oil was read at $90-110 with Middle East tensions and fell below $100 on 5 November 2026.
  • Agave: contracted prices of ₱11-13/kg against spot ₱4-5/kg and a ₱30/kg peak; Diageo's long-term contracts and owned production lag the benefit, which management says arrives mainly in the outer years. Campari, which buys 100% externally, already captured about 180bps of gross margin; for Diageo the same glut is fuelling the tequila price war, so the cost relief is partly given back in price.
  • Other: United Spirits faces extra neutral alcohol inflation from a poor monsoon and about INR 500m of West Asia costs; US freight is pressured by a truck-driver shortage; barley and grain are hedged. Cost of goods peaked over 40% above 2019.
  • Mitigation: $150m of supply-chain savings (25% in FY2027), lightweighting (15% average on Johnnie Walker and Baileys bottles), biomass at three Scotch distilleries, a 50-supplier partnership summit; aged inventory smooths grain costs because liquid was bought years earlier.

For the model: gross margin roughly flat to -30bps in FY2027 (consensus implies 59.6% against 59.8%), with any input-cost relief absorbed by price investment; a small gross-margin tailwind from agave in FY2028-29.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

F307th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Macro forces: at which level do they bite hardest?

Judgement: The macro forces bite hardest at the company level, because Diageo combines the largest exposure to the squeezed US middle-income consumer with the highest absolute debt in the sector and a hedge book that delays currency effects. At the sector level the dominant force is the discount rate, which has compressed the multiple; at the industry level it is the interplay of wallet pressure with moderation.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Consumer: US household incomes lag cumulative inflation; Mexico's consumer slump drove a double-digit fall in Diageo Mexico in Q1 FY2027; Indian food inflation (8.9%) hurts popular segments; German consumer confidence is weak.
  • Rates: the US ten-year yield is near 5%; the Bank of England is at about 4.00%; the ECB is hiking. Effects: (1) distributors' carrying costs force leaner stock (a structural reset in trade inventory); (2) Diageo's effective interest rate rises to about 4% in FY2027 from 3.9%, and 25% of gross debt must be refinanced by December 2028 (Deutsche Bank estimates -1.9% EPS by year three); (3) staples' valuation (the sector moved from a 50% premium to the market in 2019 to an 11% discount).
  • Currency: the transaction effect dominates (FY2025: -$204m transactional operating profit against +$4m translation). Hedging covers up to 36 months at about 75% of exposure, so moves arrive late. FY2026 had a +$50m operating profit benefit; FY2027 is guided -$25m. With costs in sterling and euros (Scotch, Guinness) and revenues in dollars and emerging-market currencies, a weaker dollar (Deutsche Bank: euro/dollar 1.20 at end-2026, 1.25 at end-2027) and weak emerging-market currencies (the rupee hit a record low in May 2026) create a delayed transactional headwind into FY2028-29. Sensitivity: ±10% in the dollar moves revenue about ±$200m, EBITDA ±$66m and EPS ±2.96c.

For the model: a currency line of -$25m on operating profit in FY2027 and a further transactional headwind in FY2028 if the dollar weakens; interest expense roughly flat in dollars as lower debt offsets higher rates.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–42

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Other material external dynamics

Judgement: Four further dynamics matter, and they net to a slight negative: economic power moving downstream to consolidated distributors and retailers; weather and event timing that distort quarters; a multiple-led sector rally that has already priced part of the recovery; and the weakening of some competitors, which is a modest positive.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Downstream power: Southern Glazer's holds exclusive regional rights and charges a fixed fee per depleted case; RNDC has exited markets; retailers refuse to hold excess stock at 7-9% borrowing costs; Diageo's trade receivables rose $1,036m in H1 FY2026 and its supplier-finance programme covered $1,006m of payables, a sign that working-capital terms are being negotiated hard in both directions.
  • Weather and events: a hot summer cut Guinness in Great Britain and Ireland by a low single digit in Q1 FY2027 (off-trade growth fell from 18.9% in July to -0.2% in October); stout is winter-weighted. The 2026 football World Cup and pre-tariff buying distort the year-on-year comparisons; model quarters with care.
  • Sector rotation: the median European beverage stock outperformed the Stoxx 600 by about 15 points in the six months to mid-November 2026, mostly from multiple expansion (Bernstein: the sector multiple rose 5.2% in relative terms); Diageo rose 22.5% in the six months to 4 November.
  • Weaker competitors: Pernod's India business faces a rejected Delhi licence, a $314m customs back-tax claim and an antitrust inquiry; Rémy is close to its covenant ceiling; Pernod and Brown-Forman abandoned their merger talks in April 2026, and Sazerac has offered $15bn for Brown-Forman.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D4530th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Are new entrants threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement: A moderate and rising threat in unaged spirits and new formats, and a low threat in aged categories. Adjacent entrants (brewers and wine groups in ready-to-drink) are the most dangerous; standard entrants (celebrity and craft brands) are numerous but individually small; orthogonal entrants (THC drinks, functional non-alcoholic drinks) are not yet proven.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Adjacent: Gallo leads prepared cocktails (27%); brewers (AB InBev, Boston Beer) dominate low-priced ready-to-drink, leaving Diageo 3%; Heineken entered stout; retailers' private label (Kirkland, supermarket ready-to-drink).
  • Standard: cheap agave and contract-distilled bourbon lower the cost of launching a brand; smaller, more "authentic" tequila producers erode the majors (expert calls); smaller players grew 23.3% in US spirits in 2025.
  • Orthogonal: THC seltzers (seen by young adults as healthier, but no observable effect in wholesaler data); functional and protein drinks chosen by GLP-1 users.
  • Barriers that still hold: maturation time, three-tier access, advertising restrictions, excise.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

C+5868th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How are suppliers behaving: support or risk?

Judgement: Suppliers are a mild support at present and a manageable risk: weak demand has turned glass and agave into buyers' markets, and Diageo is deepening supplier partnerships, but glass supply is concentrated and energy-sensitive, and climate risk to agriculture is rising.

Reasoning and evidence. Raw materials and packaging represent about 21% and 40% of Diageo's cost structure (Business Foundations; another source gives 31% and 28% of manufacturing cost, with glass 23%, grain 14% and agave 9% of cost of sales); glass is supplied by a concentrated group (O-I, Ardagh, Verallia), with European capacity down 10-15% and furnaces closing; agave suppliers face a glut (spot ₱4-5/kg); Diageo hosted its 50 largest suppliers to bring them earlier into innovation and cost work; supplier finance covers $1,006m of payables (0-150 days, up to 240 days in Greater China), which helps cash but is a lever that can reverse; five regenerative agriculture programmes address water and crop risk.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0125C-0342C-0440C-0828C-3232C-3735C-4340
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F252nd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading End-market demand: structural or cyclical, and how visible?

Judgement: Demand for Diageo's core developed-market spirits is in a decline that is partly structural (moderation, generational change, the end of premiumisation) and partly cyclical (squeezed middle-income wallets, high interest rates), with the rate of decline no longer worsening but not yet turning. Visibility is poor. For modelling, treat the developed-market decline as structural-leaning: the cyclical element does not have two years of visibility, so no cyclical recovery should be built into FY2027-28.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Structural, United States: total US beverage alcohol is at a new low against 2019 (Deutsche Bank, 18 August 2026); the share of US adults who drink is 54%, the lowest in Gallup's 90-year series, and 50% of 18-34 year-olds; only 6% of consumers now see moderate drinking as healthy; moderation is estimated to explain two-thirds of the decline. Premiumisation has stalled: tequila category growth fell to +3% from +10%; Diageo's portfolio is over-indexed to the top of the price ladder (82% of its US tequila sales above $45 against 39% for the market).
  • Cyclical: US consumers spend about 22% more to get 8% less than four years ago; distributors and retailers face 7-9% carrying costs, so they hold structurally less stock. US spirits scanner value was -8.7% year to date in October 2026; state-store value was -7% in August (price/mix -2.5%); September improved only on holiday timing.
  • Disproved or overstated: GLP-1 drugs look like a one-time level effect of about -2% on spirits volumes rather than a compounding decline (Jefferies; Diageo's 60,000-user survey).
  • Elsewhere: global beverage alcohol volume -4% and value -2% in 2025 (ex-Chinese spirits, -1%); India is 39% of global beverage alcohol growth, with an expected 8.3% value compound growth to FY2029; Latin America has recovered from its FY2024 destock; Chinese white spirits look at the trough after a 41.9% volume fall in FY2026; Europe is flat to soft (on-trade still below pre-pandemic; private label in Southern Europe); travel retail is back to double-digit growth.
  • Change in trend: 12 to 18 months ago, distributor destocking compounded weak consumption (North America organic -8.5% in FY2026; Q3 FY2026 US shipments -15.4% against depletions -10%). In the last six months shipments have broadly reconnected with depletions, but Diageo's own US consumption is still falling (scanner -6.1% in the four weeks to 31 October against a market +0.6%).
  • Visibility: scanner data covers under 20% of the alcohol market and misses the on-trade (over half of European volume); second-tier wholesaler stocks are opaque. Management "frequently lacks visibility over distributor inventories" (Redburn).

For the model: assume US category value roughly flat-to-down low-single-digit through FY2028 and Diageo's North America underperforming it until the price-ladder and execution changes show share stabilisation; Europe flat to +2%; India +6-10%; Latin America mid-single-digit; Asia Pacific low-single-digit with easy Chinese comparisons in H1 FY2027. The FY2029 2.5% group target needs share gains, not market growth.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F4218th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Disruption forces: strengthening or disproved, and in which direction for Diageo?

Judgement: The format disruption (ready-to-drink, non-alcoholic, smaller packs) is strengthening and acts on Diageo mainly through mix: more volume, lower gross margin per unit. The "volume collapse" disruption narrative (GLP-1 drugs, cannabis) is being partly disproved. Net, disruption is a moderate negative for margins and a small positive for recruitment where Diageo leads (non-alcoholic beer).

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Ready-to-drink: prepared cocktails were 9% of US spirits volume in 2025 and grew 23.2% in value; Diageo has only 3% share (seventh; Gallo leads with 27%) and carries a 131% price premium to the leaders. Diageo's ready-to-drink grew 25% in volume and 15% in net sales in FY2026 and is about 4% of group sales; reaching fair share would add about 3% to US sales (Deutsche Bank). The catch: cans carry 30-40% margins against about 70% for premium bottled whisky, and they cannibalise full-strength trial among young drinkers.
  • Non-alcoholic: Diageo leads non-alcoholic spirits at four times the next competitor; Guinness 0.0 is the number one non-alcoholic beer in Great Britain, 77% of its shoppers are incremental to the parent brand, and North America sales rose 45% in Q1 FY2027. It is supply-constrained until the Littleconnell brewery is complete in early 2028. Non-alcoholic spirits are under 1% of the portfolio and will not move group earnings.
  • GLP-1 drugs: a one-time level effect of about -2% on spirits volume (Jefferies), smaller than for beer and sugary drinks because spirits carry fewer calories per serve; the residual risk is the health trend among the wealthier cohorts Diageo skews to (Morgan Stanley).
  • Cannabis and THC drinks: wholesaler data show no observable impact in mature legal states; THC drinks are too expensive relative to beer and spirits. Experts see a growing youth threat; it is unproven.
  • Digital: Diageo One (32% of East African net sales value in FY2026) and EDGE 365 improve ordering and forecasting; Rémy Cointreau sells nearly a third of its China sales online, showing Diageo lags in social commerce.

For the model: by simple arithmetic, every percentage point of group sales that moves from a 70% gross-margin bottle to a 35% gross-margin can lowers group gross margin by about 35bps; model ready-to-drink and affordable formats as volume-positive and gross-margin-negative.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F4023rd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Political and regulatory change: how much is under way, and how does it bear on Diageo?

Judgement: A great deal is under way, and it nets to a near-term headwind with medium-term tailwinds in India, while the tail risks (US-Mexico-Canada trade terms, Indian customs) are larger than the tailwinds. The single largest recent change is favourable: the US removed its tariff on Scotch whisky in April 2026.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • US tariffs: the Scotch tariff removal is worth about 1.5% of profit (Bernstein). FY2027 guidance still carries $100m gross and about $70m net of tariffs (about 1.2% of FY2026 EBIT), assuming 10% on EU imports (Guinness, Baileys) and 50% on certain Canadian imports (Crown Royal, the second-largest North American brand). The US Supreme Court invalidated the earlier emergency-powers tariffs on 20 February 2026; Campari has collected a €15m refund of 2025 tariffs. Diageo's files do not mention a refund, so a refund is a possible, unquantified upside the second set should check. Fitch flags up to $600m of impact if the US-Mexico-Canada agreement's tequila exemption were removed.
  • India: the UK-India trade agreement (July 2026) cuts the Scotch tariff from 150% to 75% at once and to 40% by 2035, worth about INR 750m a year to United Spirits; but customs authorities are applying an effective 162.5% duty to bulk Scotch by reclassifying it as a food preparation, and more than 40 Diageo containers are held at ports (November 2026); the government has since said bulk Scotch is covered by the agreement, so a resolution is plausible. State excise: Maharashtra's low-tax category for local grain spirits drove the popular segment -22.7% like-for-like and 35% price rises; Karnataka's April 2026 move to alcohol-strength taxation (slabs cut from 16 to 8) favours premium brands, and Nuvama expects other states to follow.
  • Elsewhere: Chinese anti-extravagance rules hit baijiu (Shui Jing Fang); Brazil's 24% selective tax on spirits (neutral in 2027, a risk in 2028); the European Parliament adopted a text on 12 November 2026 calling for health warnings on all alcohol; Ireland's labelling law is delayed to 2028; Vietnam's Decree 90 tightens marketing; the UK deposit return scheme starts October 2027; India bans direct alcohol advertising.

For the model: tariffs net $70m in FY2027 (with possible refund upside); popular-segment India down double-digit until Maharashtra settles; premium India helped from FY2028 if customs is resolved.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F282nd percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Is a key industry or sector driver accelerating Diageo's demand?

Judgement: No broad driver is accelerating demand. Diageo has several structurally growing pockets (India premium, Guinness and stout, non-alcoholic beer, ready-to-drink, travel retail), but together they only offset the structural decline in US spirits, which is why the company guides flat in FY2027 and low-single-digit growth after.

Reasoning and evidence (the arithmetic is ours, from the files' shares and growth rates).

  • India (consensus FY2027 revenue $1.44bn, about 7.5% of sales) growing 6-10% adds about 0.5-0.75 points to group growth; Prestige and Above is about 90% of India sales and grew 7.1% in Q1.
  • Guinness (about 10% of group sales; 13% compound growth FY2023-26; return on capital about 30%) growing high-single-digit adds about 0.8-1 point; it is the only growing segment of the Great Britain beer market, but capacity-limited until 2028 and weather-sensitive.
  • Ready-to-drink and non-alcoholic (about 4-5% of sales, growing mid-teens) add about 0.5-0.7 points.
  • The US spirits pool (roughly a third of sales) at -3% to -6% subtracts about 1-2 points.
  • The end of destocking is a one-off level lift, mostly already in Q1 FY2027.

For the model: build group organic growth bottom-up from these pockets; growth above 2.5% requires US share stabilisation, which the company itself only targets for FY2028.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F323rd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Customer demand (consumers and the trade) supports a stabilisation thesis but not a growth thesis, and the support is fragile. Consumers are trading down and moving to convenience and moderation; the trade is holding minimal stock and demanding margin. Where Diageo lowered prices outside the US, volume has responded; in the US it has not yet.

Reasoning and evidence. Positive: volumes grew ahead of net sales in Europe and Latin America on affordable packs; Johnnie Walker's three-tier architecture in Latin America lifted volume 28% and gross profit 18%; Guinness grew double-digit globally; Indian Prestige and Above +7.1%; travel retail double-digit (Johnnie Walker Blue Label). Negative: US middle-income cohorts reduce discretionary spirits; Casamigos and Don Julio still show double-digit volume declines after price cuts; Diageo skews to middle and high-income US consumers whose spending on premium spirits is falling (Morgan Stanley); on-trade share of group revenue fell from 32% to 29%; retailers allocate up to 20% of shelf to ready-to-drink; Southern European off-trade is in double-digit decline with private label at half the price.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F3516th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Regulators and politicians are, on balance, hurting near-term earnings (US tariffs, Indian state excise and customs, Brazilian sin tax, tighter marketing rules, health warnings) and helping a few medium-term pools (UK-India agreement, Karnataka's strength-based tax, removal of the US Scotch tariff). The position is fragile because the largest exposures (US trade policy, Indian state policy) can change by decree. Regulation also protects incumbents (franchise laws, advertising bans, appellation rules), which is a quiet positive.

Reasoning and evidence: as set out in C-04. The tail risk worth a scenario is a removal of North American trade exemptions (Fitch: up to $600m of impact), and the upside worth a scenario is an Indian customs resolution plus a US tariff refund.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F4013th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading How large is the realistic addressable market, and what growth does it allow?

Judgement: The market is large relative to Diageo (Diageo has about 9% of global spirits value and about 5% of spirits plus beer), so the market is not a ceiling; but its realistic growth in Diageo's geographic mix is only about 2-3% a year in value over the mid term, so the market alone supports low-single-digit growth and anything more must come from share and mix.

Reasoning and evidence. Global beer is about $193bn of net revenue (2024); global spirits value, implied by Diageo's 9% share, is roughly $170-180bn (our inference). Mid-term value growth: US spirits expected to recover to a normalised 3-4% (below pre-pandemic), Europe flat to low-single-digit, India about 8% (value compound growth from INR 2,664bn in FY2024 to INR 3,976bn by FY2029), Latin America mid-single-digit, China uncertain; long-term industry value growth was 4.5% (2009-19), falling to 3.4% (2019-24). Weighted by Diageo's exposure (roughly 36-39% North America, about 24-28% Europe including Africa, 7-8% India, 9% Latin America, 12-18% Asia Pacific), the market grows about 2-3% (our arithmetic). Diageo averaged about 4% organic growth from 1980 to 2019 by gaining share and mix in a growing market; that combination is weaker now.

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0645C-1028
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D4530th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Where is Diageo's own revenue cycle, and is it turning?

Judgement: Diageo's revenue cycle troughed in FY2026 and is turning, but weakly: the turn comes from the end of destocking (a one-step lift) and easy comparisons, not from consumption growth. Expect flat organic growth in FY2027 with a better second half, and treat Q1's beat as partly phasing.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The trough: organic net sales -2.0% in FY2026 (H1 FY2026 revenue -4.0%); North America -8.5%; Chinese white spirits -20% or worse; the first post-pandemic volume decline.
  • The turn: Q1 FY2027 organic +1.4% (+0.6% excluding EABL), Diageo's best top-line performance in over a year; North America -3.6% against -6.7% expected, helped by distributor inventory "aligning with the prior year". Morgan Stanley estimates underlying US spirits at about -5.8% to -6%; it expects a US distributor stock unwind in Q2 FY2027 and points out that easy comparisons partly reflect the lapping of pre-tariff loading in the prior year.
  • Destock status: US shipments lagged depletions by 5.4 points in Q3 FY2026 and by 2.5 points over FY2026; Jefferies says shipments have now "broadly reconnected" with retail demand; the US wholesaler inventory-to-sales ratio fell from 1.61x (March) to 1.57x (April 2026) against a 1.50x baseline. Latin America's destock (shipments halted in FY2024, 120 days of inventory against a 30-60 day norm) has cleared (+16.2% in Q3 FY2026, +4.8% in Q1 FY2027).
  • Historical analogue: in FY2015 organic sales fell 2% as shipments ran 6% behind depletions; organic growth recovered to 5% by FY2018. The current algorithm assumes a slower recovery (2.5% by FY2029).

For the model: FY2027 organic net sales -1% to +1%, phased weaker in Q2 and stronger in H2 against the -9.4% North America comparison of Q3 FY2026; do not extrapolate the destock end as a growth rate.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

F287th percentile

Layer 1 · External change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Each thematic driver in turn, then all together: how strong are the headwinds and tailwinds?

Judgement: The external picture is a net headwind of moderate strength that is easing only at the margin. Tailwinds are real but narrow (a destock ending, tariff relief on Scotch, India, stout and non-alcoholic beer); headwinds are broad and persistent (US moderation, share donation, price deflation in gluts, downstream power, high discount rates). The environment will not carry Diageo; any investment case has to be self-help.

Theme by theme.

  • Sector theme, spirits destocking ending (Diageo first): a tailwind, but a single step, largely captured in Q1 FY2027.
  • Sector theme, cost programmes and returning buybacks (about 12% EPS compounding for large drinks companies): a tailwind within management's control; for Diageo buybacks are deferred to FY2028.
  • Sector theme, "in spirits, price is not the lever": a headwind confirmed by Diageo's own price cuts.
  • Sector theme, alcohol-free beer's duty advantage: a small tailwind through Guinness 0.0.
  • Industry theme, shoppers resisting price rises: a headwind for a portfolio that relied on 2-3% annual price/mix.
  • Industry theme, leaving low-margin businesses: a tailwind (EABL, African beer, tail brands).

By level. Value chain: strong headwinds (consumer moderation, share loss, distributor and retailer power), partial tailwind from service fixes. Industry: strong headwinds in US spirits (gluts, price deflation, new entrants in unaged spirits and cans); tailwinds in India and stout. Sector: the discount rate is a headwind to valuation, but the sector is rated Overweight in the house's Fitness for Fighting tilts and Diageo trades at the 2nd percentile of its own ten-year P/E range, so valuation is now a cushion rather than a risk.

Internal change forces/risks

C+6872nd percentile

Memo topic 2, Internal change forces: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

Diageo has the strongest set of internal change forces among the European spirits houses: a decisive board, an external heavyweight chief executive who moved from diagnosis to a live new operating model in seven months, $1bn of quantified savings that build on an over-delivered programme, a dividend reset that funds deleveraging, and disposals at 17x and 84x EBITDA. These make the earnings floor credible: guided low-to-mid-single-digit organic profit growth in FY2027 (about $115-285m) plus about $70m of net tariffs needs roughly 45-90% of the year's gross savings (about $380-440m) to reach the operating line, before price investment and ordinary cost inflation (our arithmetic), so the guidance is realistic rather than heavily cushioned. What the internal forces have not yet shown is the mechanism that turns cost into share: A&P was cut, prices were cut, the organisation is tired, and the North American share loss continues. The second-set Analyst should model the cost and balance-sheet effects with confidence and the volume effects with a wide range, and should treat a gross-margin expansion without volume acceleration as evidence against, not for, the case.

Internal risks

Judgement38C-1465C-8635C-5945C-8145D-0355
Group judgement

Risks

D3837th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The core risk is not a single event but the continuation of the present: a growth engine that has reversed (US premium tequila and spirits), compounded by price competition into gluts and by trade-policy volatility, against a balance sheet that only reaches comfort in FY2028. Tail risks are mostly policy-driven (North American trade terms, Indian customs) and a structural brown-spirits demand break that would expose $7.8bn of maturing stock. Sentiment risk is elevated after a 45% rally from the low, with monthly US data and peer prints able to move the shares sharply. The cushion is valuation: the stock trades at the bottom of its ten-year range, so a fair-trough fall is about 20-30% rather than the 40% of the deepest bear case. For the model, run explicit scenarios for (1) a US price war, (2) tariff escalation and (3) an India customs adverse outcome, and keep the base case free of any of them.

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

B6580th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Are standards improving in execution, operating measures, governance and cost discipline?

Judgement: Standards are improving clearly in cost discipline, capital discipline and accountability, but execution standards in the market are still weak and are the part that matters most for the top line.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Cost: Accelerate delivered $540m in FY2026 against $300m guided (80% over); non-working and development costs were cut from 21% to 15% of A&P in FY2025, with about 10% targeted; virtual content studios and AI tools cut agency fees; headcount fell 6.4% to 27,938.
  • Capital: capex cut from $1,549m (FY2025) to about $1.2bn (FY2026); maturing-stock investment managed down; the dividend rebased; disposals pursued.
  • Governance and incentives: the long-term plan now pays on free cash flow, EPS and ROIC (from eight metrics to three); the short-term plan weights net sales growth, operating profit growth and absolute free cash flow equally; country heads report monthly.
  • Execution in market (still weak): share losses in segments making up 65% of the North America portfolio; Casamigos ready-to-drink packaging quality problems in Q1 FY2027; out-of-stocks and order fulfilment below 80% (Bernstein, 2025); on-time-in-full at 84%; Diageo ranks in the bottom seventeenth percentile of peers for organic growth momentum.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

D3532nd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: The negatives are significant and cluster around one theme: a cost-led turnaround that may erode brand and organisational capability faster than it rebuilds competitiveness. This is the "false friend" pattern the Risks report and the Wisdom Library both warn about, where reported margins improve while the franchise weakens.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Marketing: A&P -13% organically in FY2026; on the company's own figures the A&P reduction (about $480m) was roughly four times the organic operating profit growth (about $110m), so without it organic profit would have fallen (our arithmetic); Deutsche Bank's study shows drinks companies that raise brand spend outperform those that cut it, and Campari is raising A&P (17.9% of sales).
  • Organisation: employee engagement fell 4 points to 79% and pride 2 points to 88%; staff turnover rose to 20.2% from 16.6% (involuntary 11.5%); former directors warn that loss of experienced staff has damaged customer and distributor relationships and that remaining staff are stretched; Lewis is seen by some as a cost-cutter with limited on-trade knowledge.

  • Commercial: Casamigos price cuts have not yet lifted volume; Casamigos ready-to-drink packaging problems; the Murphy's attack on Guinness in Great Britain; FY2027 free cash flow down to about $2bn on restructuring cash.
  • Governance perception: the removal of separately weighted environmental and social measures from pay drew criticism at the 2026 AGM.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

D4528th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture?

Judgement: Not yet congruent, but moving in the right direction: goals, incentives and structure have been aligned (free cash flow, EPS and ROIC; five regions with clear accountability; three simple behaviours), while the lived culture still carries siloes, fatigue and the damage of high turnover. Congruence of the Lifco or Ryanair kind is several years away.

Reasoning and evidence. The old value set was replaced with "One team, Competitive, Decisive"; purpose restated as "crafting iconic drinks chosen for life's moments"; monthly country reporting; employee motivation rose 2 points to 81% (4 points above the consumer-goods benchmark) and staff report more optimism since Lewis arrived; but engagement fell 4 points to 79%, pride fell to 88%, involuntary turnover rose to 11.5%, and former managers describe damaged distributor relationships. Incentives and strategy now point the same way, which is the most important structural fix.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

C-4554th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading Sudden, high-magnitude, low-probability fundamental risks

Judgement: The largest tail risks are trade-policy shocks in the US and India and a structural demand break that turns aged inventory from an asset into a write-down; each is plausible at low probability and several could each remove 5-10% or more of EBIT.

Risk by risk (probabilities are our qualitative judgement).

  • Removal of US-Mexico-Canada trade exemptions or broader US tariffs (low-to-medium plausibility, high impact): Fitch cites up to $600m if tequila exemptions go (about 10% of EBIT); 25% or higher tariffs on EU imports would hit Guinness and Baileys; a 20% tariff on American whiskies was once estimated at $80m.
  • India customs and state shocks (medium plausibility, medium impact): the 162.5% effective duty on bulk Scotch, held containers, a possible back-tax claim of the kind Pernod faces ($314m), further state excise changes like Maharashtra's.
  • Aged-inventory write-down (low plausibility, high impact): $7.8bn of maturing stock (39% of sales); industry gluts in cognac and bourbon; a structural decline in brown spirits would force impairments and price cuts.
  • US price war (medium plausibility, high impact): a broad reset across tequila and whisky would take North America margins down far more than the 600bps Deutsche Bank models for FY2027.
  • Key-person risk (low plausibility, high impact): the restructuring and $1bn of savings depend heavily on Lewis.
  • Moët Hennessy associate (low-to-medium): a further collapse in Hennessy or a change in LVMH's strategy toward the joint business.
  • Health regulation (low near term): EU-wide warning labels following the European Parliament's November 2026 text; tobacco-style restrictions.
  • Litigation (low impact): New York class actions alleging non-agave content in Casamigos and Don Julio (damages sought $5m; Mexico's tequila council certifies the products).
D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

C5563rd percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading How diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers of the differences between the forecasts and the last four to six years?

Judgement. Highly concentrated. Two drivers, the share of the $1bn of savings kept in profit and the fall in net finance charges as debt is repaid, account for essentially all of the net EPS growth to FY2029; everything else is a set of known, mechanical gains and losses that cancel. This is the reverse of FY2020-26, when price/mix, volume and then interest drove the numbers. Concentration is normally a risk, but here its character matters more than its degree: both drivers are internal, dated and visible in advance, and the main subtractions (East African Breweries, the roll-off of currency hedges) are already known. The concentration that should worry the Consumer is elsewhere: every external pressure (price investment, tariffs, canned-cocktail mix, lost share) lands on one profit pool, North America, which earns about 40-45% of group profit; and the growth pockets that offset it are many and small.

The EPS bridge, my base (cents per share).

Driver FY2026 to FY2028 (165.3c to about 175c) FY2026 to FY2029 (to about 185c)
Savings retained in EBIT (about $360m, then $400m) +12.3 +13.7
Lower net finance charges (about $850m to $705m, then $680m) +5.0 +5.8
Other organic profit growth (volume, mix, price, net of inflation) +2.3 +8.3
Associates (Moët Hennessy; $227m to $270m, then $290m) +1.9 +2.8
Buybacks ($0.5bn, then $1.5bn) +0.1 +2.5
Minorities +0.9 +0.3
East African Breweries and other disposals (EBIT -$225m) -7.7 -7.7
Transactional currency (hedge gains rolling off) -2.6 -3.6
Tax rate (24.3% to about 25% of profit before associates) -2.2 -2.4
Total +10.0 +19.7

Reading the bridge.

  • To FY2028 the two controllable drivers add 17.3 cents; two already-known subtractions take back 10.3 cents; organic trading, net of everything else, adds 2.3 cents. The forecast for the next 18 months barely needs the market.
  • The margin tells the same story: the EBIT margin rises from 29.0% to 31.5% by FY2029, of which the ratio of overheads and other costs to sales (14.6% to 11.8%) contributes about +280 basis points, gross margin about -20 and advertising about -10; about 50 of the 250 comes mechanically from the exit of lower-margin East African Breweries. One cost line explains more than the whole change.
  • The sensitivities are of similar size and partly offsetting: 10 points of retention on the $910m of gross savings due by FY2028 is about $90m of EBIT (3 cents); one point of North American organic sales is about $40m of EBIT (1.4 cents), so the plausible North American range (about -6% to -2% in FY2027) is worth about ±3 cents. Management holds the first lever and can use it to absorb the second, which narrows the range of EPS and widens the range of franchise health.
  • Revenue is asymmetric: the positives are diffuse (Guinness, India, canned cocktails and non-alcoholic, Latin America, travel retail each add 0.3 to 1 point of growth), the negative is single and large (United States spirits subtracts 1 to 2 points). A diffuse set of small positives cannot surprise much; a single large negative can.
  • After FY2029 the concentration becomes a weakness: the savings are spent, finance charges stop falling (my figures are flat at about $680-700m from FY2029 as buybacks absorb the cash), and growth then depends on volume, mix and buybacks. This is why the second set's margin flattens at 31.5-31.8% and why consensus margins of 31.8% and 32.3% for FY2029-30 are the most exposed numbers in the forecast period.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

A7894th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Changes in the chief executive, other key leaders and key actors, and what they signal

Judgement: The change is near-total and it signals a board that has stopped defending the old model: an external retail-turnaround chief executive, a new finance director to come, new heads of the two problem regions (North America and Asia Pacific), a new Chair, and a refreshed board. The signal is positive for the probability of decisive self-help and cost delivery; it is mixed for top-line execution, because the new leaders are strongest in off-trade retail and finance, not spirits or the on-trade.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Chief executive: Debra Crew left by mutual agreement on 16 July 2025 after regional profit warnings, the third chief executive in three years; Nik Jhangiani (finance director) was interim; Sir Dave Lewis started on 1 January 2026, chosen unanimously after a global search. He ran Tesco 2014-2020 (rebuilt profitability and supplier relations, simplified supplier income streams from 24 to 5) after nearly 30 years at Unilever, and chaired Haleon. Caveat: Tesco's share price was broadly range-bound during his tenure; he may be a stabiliser rather than a growth accelerator.
  • Finance director: on 23 September 2026 Diageo announced Jhangiani will step down in 2027, replaced by Joanne Wilson (WPP finance director, previously Britvic finance director and about ten years at Tesco overlapping with Lewis). Analysts were surprised, because Jhangiani had just built the cost plan; she is seen as a "credible successor" but has no spirits experience.
  • Regions: North America president Sally Grimes stepped aside in March 2026; John O'Keeffe (30 years at Diageo, ex-Asia Pacific head) took over. Sujay Wasan (28 years at Procter & Gamble) became Asia Pacific president in August 2026. The executive team is streamlined to five regional presidents and a few global functions; Natalie Bickford joined as Chief People Officer; Africa and HR heads left.
  • Board: Sir John Manzoni became Chair in February 2025; John Rishton (Rolls-Royce, British Airways) joined in November 2025 and bought shares in the market; Alex Keith (30 years at Procter & Gamble) joined in November 2026; Melissa Bethell replaced Susan Kilsby as remuneration chair.
  • Key actors: no activist and no controlling shareholder; the board itself was the change agent.

For the model: leadership change raises the probability that the $1bn savings and the $1.2bn restructuring are delivered on time; it does not by itself raise the probability of US share recovery. The finance-director handover in 2027 is a modest execution risk in the year the restructuring cash goes out.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

B+7289th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Changes in strategy and strategic priorities, and how well articulated and resourced

Judgement: The strategy has changed substantially and is clearly articulated and well resourced: from brand-first premiumisation to a category-led, full-price-ladder model run through a simpler operating framework, funded by $1.2bn of restructuring that is meant to release $1bn of savings for reinvestment. The weak point is internal consistency on marketing: the plan holds A&P at about 16% of sales after a 13% organic cut, while seeking share gains.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Choices: (1) compete across the full price and format ladder, adding accessible tiers below $25, small packs and ready-to-drink tied to core brands (Smirnoff Ice, Casamigos and Bulleit cocktails) while keeping luxury lines; (2) two battlegrounds, core spirits and premium beer (Guinness), with resources moved from small local brands to global trademarks; (3) category-led selling with distributors and retailers instead of transactional pushing, with US distributor incentives moved from shipments to sales velocity; (4) customer service: on-time-in-full delivery from 84% to a 98% target, against a starting point where 60-64% of customer orders were entered manually and the chief executive called service "frankly very poor"; (5) five regions and 23 market organisations with clear accountability and monthly country reporting; (6) balance-sheet repair before buybacks.
  • Articulation: the August 2026 Capital Markets Day used two slides of bullet points; the plan carries dated, numbered targets (C-72).
  • Resourcing: $1.2bn of restructuring ($1.1bn operating framework, $0.1bn supply chain), $752m charged in FY2026, about $850m of cash out in FY2027; capex about $1.25bn a year (about 7% of sales against about 5% historically), prioritising Guinness capacity (over 50% more by FY2029, about $920m) and European ready-to-drink lines.
  • Inconsistency to watch: A&P fell 13.1% organically to $3.18bn in FY2026 (from 18.1% to 16.2% of sales, mostly procurement and production efficiencies); Morgan Stanley and Deutsche Bank argue that cutting marketing is unlikely to reverse share losses. Some sources describe the purpose as an "Adult Beverages Company" broadening into ready-to-drink and low-alcohol, others say the old total-beverage-alcohol framework was retired in favour of two battlegrounds; both point to the same practical choice: core spirits plus Guinness, with formats as extensions.

For the model: treat the strategy as volume-led and margin-percentage-dilutive at the gross line in North America, offset by overheads at the operating line.

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

D4739th percentile

The Investment Analyst's heading Has guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets been set or raised?

Judgement: Top-line guidance has been rebased lower and set at a deliberately reachable level, while cost, cash-return-on-capital and leverage targets have been set or raised. The new algorithm is the first formal three-year financial framework under Lewis; it is a floor-setting exercise, not an upgrade.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

A7894th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Is the company reducing its perimeter or composition?

Judgement: Yes, materially and at good prices. The disposals simplify the group, cut volatile emerging-market beer exposure, reduce leverage and are modestly margin-accretive, but they also remove growth and cash flow that the model must strip out.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Completed or agreed: the 65% stake in EABL to Asahi for $2.3bn net (17x EBITDA; leverage -0.25x; Kenyan approval granted subject to shelf-space undertakings; completion expected by the end of calendar 2026; a possible indemnity of up to $120m); United Spirits' sale of the Royal Challengers Bengaluru cricket team for INR 166.6bn (about $1.9bn gross, 84x EBITDA; about $800m returned via a special dividend; leverage -0.1x; regulatory clearance pending, long-stop date mid-December 2026); Guinness Ghana ($81m, a $49m loss), Seychelles Breweries ($89m, a $62m gain), Guinness Nigeria ($53m), Cameroon restructured; brands Pampero ($55m), Cacique ($67m), Safari ($16m), Sheridan's ($48m), Starward; the Santa Vittoria plant in Italy ($120m); exit from Distill Ventures ($308m impairment in FY2025); ending of distribution joint ventures with Moët Hennessy in France ($145m charge) and in Japan, Singapore and Thailand ($75m).
  • Under review: a possible sale of the 63% stake in Shui Jing Fang (about 5.1% of group operating profit in FY2025 per Bernstein; high-margin, so EPS-dilutive).
  • Ruled out: Guinness and the 34% stake in Moët Hennessy.
  • Effect: EABL's beer margins of about 22% sit well below the group's 29%, so its exit lifts the group margin mechanically (C-53); Morgan Stanley estimates about $900m of lost free cash flow over FY2027-29.

For the model: deconsolidate EABL from about the end of calendar 2026 (consensus "Corporate and other" revenue falls from $884m in FY2027 to $356m in FY2028, consistent with roughly half a year of EABL leaving each year); reduce net debt by the proceeds; remove the associated minority interest.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

F5220th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly?

Judgement: Diageo is not entering new geographies; it is entering new price tiers and formats within its existing categories, which is the sensible kind of adjacency (close to the core, using the same brands, customers and distributors). The risk is gross-margin dilution and late entry into a crowded ready-to-drink market, not strategic distance.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Price tiers: accessible premium below $25, pack-price architecture in Mexico (smaller affordable packs), three-tier Johnnie Walker architecture in Latin America (+28% volume, +18% gross profit in H2 FY2026).
  • Formats: ready-to-drink tied to core brands; non-alcoholic in 17 markets (Guinness 0.0 in 12); small packs.
  • Geography within existing markets: Guinness outside its home markets (double-digit growth in Germany, France, Southeast Asia, Australia, Africa); a new Emerging Markets focus inside the regional structure; Shui Jing Fang's national lottery-shop distribution deal in China.
  • Sense check: by the core-versus-adjacency test in the Wisdom Library, these moves share customers, channels and brands with the core, so their failure rate should be far lower than for distant moves; but Diageo holds only a 3% share in prepared cocktails, where Gallo and brewers are entrenched.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F182nd percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Is the company raising prices in real terms?

Judgement: No. Prices are falling in nominal terms, not just real terms, across North America and in parts of Europe and Asia; management describes the price-ladder work as an investment in margins. Real pricing power is only latent, in Guinness, luxury Scotch, travel retail and Indian Prestige and Above, and management is not currently pointing to it.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Organic price/mix -1.6% in FY2026 (US spirits and Chinese white spirits); US price/mix -4.2% (Deutsche Bank); in Q1 FY2027 price/mix -2.0% in EMEA and -2.1% in Asia Pacific (targeted repositioning and promotions), +0.7% group excluding EABL (mix).
  • Retail prices cut by more than 1% on 67-71% of US SKUs; Johnnie Walker Black down 25% in US control states; Casamigos price/mix -8.5% in October 2026 (from -2.5% in February); Casamigos recommended retail price cut 10-15%; Deutsche Bank: lower prices failed to lift volume for 72% of key brands.
  • The historical algorithm of 2-3% price and 2-3% volume broke after excessive post-pandemic price rises; Diageo raised tequila prices 4.5% in the post-pandemic boom.
  • Latent: Karnataka's strength-based tax and the UK-India agreement favour premium Indian pricing; Guinness has cultural momentum; travel retail luxury Scotch (Johnnie Walker Blue Label) is strong.

For the model: price/mix of about -1% to 0% in FY2027 and about +1% from FY2028 at best; no real price increase in the base case.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

D5035th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how sensibly?

Judgement: No; acquisitions are effectively paused while Diageo deleverages and sells non-core assets, which is the sensible choice. Its past acquisition record (Casamigos, Don Papa, Distill Ventures, Ritual) argues for continued restraint.

Reasoning and evidence. Leverage of 3.1x and the stated priority of reaching about 2.0x by FY2029 leave no room for large deals; management has explicitly ruled out selling Guinness or the Moët Hennessy stake. Peers are consolidating (Campari bought Courvoisier at a cyclical peak and is now impairing brands; Pernod and Brown-Forman talked and stopped; Sazerac bid $15bn for Brown-Forman), which may create larger rivals but is not a Diageo use of capital. Past write-downs (Don Papa $287m, Distill Ventures $308m) show the cost of premium-tier deals bought late in the cycle.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

F4019th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Is the company making more use of its balance sheet or stepping up shareholder returns?

Judgement: Not yet: returns were cut, not raised. The dividend was halved and buybacks are suspended to repair the balance sheet. Management is increasingly mooting a return of capital from FY2028 (about $2bn of buybacks is cited), which is a credible, dated optionality that consensus barely models.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • The FY2026 dividend was rebased to 50c from 103.48c (a 52% cut), saving about $1bn a year, with a 30-50% payout policy and a 50c floor; FactSet consensus dividends (51.6c, 55.0c, 60.0c for FY2027-29) sit at the bottom of the range.
  • Buybacks: $2.99bn in FY2022, $1.64bn in FY2023, $0.97bn in FY2024, none since; shareholders renewed authority for up to 10% of capital.
  • Path: leverage 3.1x to about 2.75x in FY2027 (Bernstein models 2.28x) and about 2.0x in FY2029, unlocking buybacks or dividend growth from FY2028 (Jefferies).
  • Consensus EPS implies an almost flat share count to FY2029 (FY2029 net income $4,168m over EPS $1.88 implies about 2,217m shares against 2,224m today), so a $2bn buyback at today's price (about 3.5% of shares) is largely outside consensus.

For the model: no buyback in FY2027; a buyback scenario from FY2028 conditional on leverage near 2.5x; dividend at 30-35% payout.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–60

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: Several smaller positives are present and together are worth having in the model: a better customer-service and order system, an inventory buffer that unwinds, lagged agave relief, Guinness capacity, India regulatory tailwinds, and incentive realignment.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Service and systems: the SAP S/4HANA programme (Project Voyager) and the on-time-in-full target of 98% from 84%; a credible lever for share in a channel that rewards in-stock suppliers (inference from the out-of-stock evidence).
  • Working capital: about $100m of deliberate inventory built for the system cut-over and Middle East supply risk should reverse in FY2027; maturing-stock investment, which averaged about $600m a year since FY2021, can slow.
  • Costs: agave cost relief reaches tequila margins in the outer years; $150m of supply-chain savings; lightweighting.
  • Growth assets: Guinness Littleconnell phase one added 25% capacity in April 2026, with over 50% more by FY2029; Guinness 0.0 capacity doubles in Ireland by late 2028.
  • India: Karnataka-style strength-based tax spreading, the UK-India agreement, and United Spirits refocusing on Prestige and Above.
  • Incentives: pay tied to free cash flow, EPS and ROIC; US distributor incentives tied to velocity.
  • Portfolio: a possible Shui Jing Fang sale would cut net debt.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

C-5051st percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Have key controlling actors indicated scope for change in strategy?

Judgement: There is no controlling shareholder and no activist on the register, so no outside actor is pressing for change; the board has already made the change itself. Activist speculation exists because of the discount, but the usual playbook (buybacks, break-up) is constrained by leverage, and management has rejected the two obvious break-up ideas.

Reasoning and evidence. The Irish Times (March 2025) noted the 20% discount to the ten-year average might attract activists; an activist would likely push a spin-out of the 34% Moët Hennessy stake or a sale of Guinness, both explicitly ruled out by management and both of which would dilute the best assets. Proxy adviser Glass Lewis opposed the 2025 remuneration report. Analysts believe an improved performance culture is needed to keep activists away.

For the model: no change; a Moët Hennessy monetisation is a low-probability optionality (the associate is carried at about $5.3bn, about 9% of the market value).

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

B+7589th percentile

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading Taken together, how strong, broad and well resourced are the positive corporate changes?

Judgement: Strong, broad and well resourced: a new chief executive and team, a new operating framework, a rebased capital allocation policy, $1.2bn of restructuring money, $1bn of dated savings, a disposal programme executed at high multiples, and realigned incentives. The breadth is weighted toward costs, capital and organisation; the commercial changes (price ladder, service, distributor incentives, formats) are the right ones but are unproven, and the first quarter's evidence is mixed. In Wisdom Library terms this is a "massive corporate change plan" and a "transformation play" whose earnings floor is credible but whose growth leg must still be proven; turnarounds take longer than expected, and Lewis's own Tesco record showed operational repair before any re-rating.

For the model: high confidence in the cost and leverage lines (savings of about $340-400m in FY2027, net debt falling about $3bn with disposals); medium-to-low confidence in the revenue line.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

B+5687th percentile

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's heading What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead, how novel, and how well founded?

Judgement: A credible staircase exists, front-loaded with financial and portfolio events (well founded, moderately novel) and back-loaded with commercial proof points (more novel, less well founded). The step that would change the narrative most is evidence of US share stabilisation; the step most likely to surprise positively is the return of buybacks at a low multiple.

Next six months (to May 2027).

  • H1 FY2027 results, 10 February 2027: the first profit read of the new framework (savings drop-through, North America margin after price investment, A&P ratio). Well founded; moderately novel.
  • EABL completion (end of calendar 2026) and the cricket-team sale (long-stop mid-December 2026), with United Spirits' special dividend: well founded; low novelty (already known) but they lock in deleveraging.
  • India customs resolution on bulk Scotch: plausible (the government says bulk Scotch is covered by the UK-India agreement); novel; would release containers and the FTA saving.
  • A possible US tariff refund after the February 2026 court ruling (as Campari received): speculative for Diageo; novel.
  • Q3 FY2027 trading (May 2027) against the easiest North America comparison (-9.4% in Q3 FY2026): well founded on arithmetic.

Months six to eighteen (to May 2028).

  • FY2027 results (August 2027): leverage at about 2.75x, free cash flow about $2bn after restructuring cash, FY2028 guidance; the finance-director handover to Joanne Wilson.
  • Agave cost relief and supply-chain savings reaching tequila margins: well founded, low novelty.
  • US share "holding" in FY2028 and evidence that price-ladder changes (Casamigos, accessible tiers, small packs) drive volume: the most important and least proven step.
  • The restart of buybacks (about $2bn mooted) from FY2028: novel, conditional on leverage, largely outside consensus.
  • Littleconnell brewery completion (early 2028), easing Guinness and Guinness 0.0 constraints: well founded.
  • A possible Shui Jing Fang disposal: plausible, moderately novel.

Months eighteen to thirty-six (to late 2029).

  • US share gains in FY2029, 2.5% organic growth, leverage about 2.0x, ROIC +130bps, full $1bn savings, free cash flow of about $3bn a year: the algorithm's completion; novel if delivered, moderately founded.
  • Guinness over 50% larger capacity; India premiumisation with lower Scotch duties (75%, falling to 40% by 2035): well founded.

Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D5746th percentile

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D5746th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Diageo leads an industry whose structure is still fundamentally attractive (time-based barriers, brand-driven demand, regulation that protects incumbents, negligible private label, 60-70% gross margins), but whose discipline has weakened at exactly the point where Diageo is most exposed: US spirits, unaged categories and the mid-tier. Falling demand, gluts of whiskey, cognac and agave, consolidated distributors and retailers, and a price-cutting leader form the textbook setup for margin erosion; the offsets are that the most leveraged competitors cannot sustain a price war and that Diageo's best assets (Guinness, luxury Scotch, Indian Prestige and Above, non-alcoholic beer) sit in the most protected or fastest-growing pools. For the model: assume the industry does not reward price in FY2027-28; assume Diageo's share in US spirits stabilises no earlier than FY2028 (the company's own date); give Guinness and India above-group growth; treat a broad US price war as the main downside scenario rather than the base. The precedent analysis says Diageo is a late-cycle stabilisation and re-rating candidate, not yet a compounding share-gainer.

Value chain dynamics

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F322nd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Is the route to market helping or hindering, and how fragile is that?

Judgement: The route to market is currently a hindrance, partly self-inflicted (poor service, transactional selling) and partly structural (consolidated US distributors and retailers holding lean stock). The self-inflicted part is being fixed and is the most tractable lever in the plan; the structural part is permanent.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • US three-tier: control states hold 25% of the population and 23% of spirits sales and restrict pricing; Southern Glazer's operates exclusive regional rights and a per-case fee into local marketing banks; franchise laws make changing distributors costly; distributors carry stock at 7-9% cost; the inventory-to-sales ratio sits near 1.57x against 1.50x; O'Keeffe is moving distributor incentives from stock accumulation to sell-out velocity.
  • Self-inflicted: 60-64% of orders entered manually; on-time-in-full of 84% (target 98%); out-of-stocks; Bernstein flagged order fulfilment below 80% in 2025.
  • Elsewhere: direct teams replace hubs in Southern Europe; the Moët Hennessy joint distribution exits in France and Asia cost $220m in charges but give direct control; African beer moves to an asset-light third-party model (control lost, capital released); Diageo One digital ordering.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

F3814th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How are existing competitors behaving, and how fragile is Diageo's position against them?

Judgement: Competitors' behaviour is hurting Diageo in the US and in Indian mainstream and is a new threat to Guinness in Great Britain; Diageo's position is fragile in US tequila, vodka and Canadian whisky, and robust in Scotch, stout (globally) and Indian premium. The leverage of Pernod and Rémy limits how hard they can push.

Reasoning and evidence. Campari wins with on-trade activation, 300,000 trained bartenders and price discipline; Pernod gains in India (+19%) and leads Turkish Scotch by 10 points; Hennessy's price cuts shape cognac; Heineken's Murphy's is priced at 80% of Guinness; Radico Khaitan and Allied Blenders expand premium Indian whisky. In September 2026, Campari and Rémy outperformed while Diageo and Pernod underperformed the US market (which grew 1.9% in value). Diageo's 37% North America operating margin (calendar 2025) gives it room to reinvest that peers lack.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F3516th percentilefull answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F3816th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting, how, and how fragile do they leave Diageo?

Judgement: Disruption acts on every link: consumers (moderation, GLP-1, Gen Z habits), competitors (cheap entrants, ready-to-drink), regulators (tariffs, health labelling, excise), suppliers (glass consolidation, agave glut), distributors and retailers (consolidation, lean stock, data power) and channels (digital B2B, e-commerce). Together they leave Diageo moderately fragile in the US and robust elsewhere; none is existential, but several compound on the same profit pool (US spirits, about 40-50% of profit).

Reasoning and evidence. Consumers: fewer drinkers and fewer drinks per occasion (Gen Z goes out as often but has one drink fewer). Competitors: glut-driven price cuts and new entrants in unaged spirits. Regulators: $70m net US tariffs, India customs, EU health warnings. Suppliers: glass capacity cuts in Europe; agave deflation is a cost help but a price threat. Distributors and retailers: hold less stock and demand promotion funding; the risk of rent moving permanently downstream (Business Foundations "other side of the story"). Channels: digital B2B lowers sales administration costs (a help). The compounding: price cuts plus margin-dilutive formats plus tariffs all hit the same North American gross margin.
