C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B+8284th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Where does Diageo rank by share or size, and how far from number one or two?

Judgement: Diageo is number one: in international spirits (about 21% value share against Pernod Ricard's 6%), in global spirits revenue (about 9% against 6%), in US spirits (about 15-19% of value), and in most of its categories (Scotch, vodka, Canadian whisky, stout, Indian whisky, non-alcoholic spirits). Leadership is not the issue; the issue is that leadership has not prevented share loss in the US.

Reasoning and evidence. Diageo's spirits business is about €15bn against a pro-forma Pernod-plus-Brown-Forman of €12.8bn; it holds over 50% of the world's aged Scotch; Johnnie Walker is the leading Scotch, Smirnoff the leading vodka, Crown Royal the leading Canadian whisky, Guinness the world's leading stout; it is a top-two tequila owner (Don Julio, Casamigos) against Becle (Jose Cuervo) and Bacardi (Patrón); United Spirits leads Indian whisky in Prestige and Above. It is weak only in prepared cocktails (3%, seventh) and aperitifs (Campari's Aperol dominates). US share has fallen from 17.2% (2014) to 15.1% (2025, Jefferies).

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C-6851st percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How concentrated is the market, and how steep is the share distribution?

Judgement: It depends on the market definition, and the definition that matters for Diageo's pricing is the category and country. Global spirits is fragmented because of local spirits (baijiu, Indian whisky); international premium spirits and most of Diageo's categories are concentrated and steep.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Global spirits: the top five distillers hold about 18% of volume and 31% of value; top players about 25% of value. Diageo 9%, Pernod 6%, Suntory 3%, Thai Beverage 1%, Mark Anthony 1% (revenue shares). The top-five threshold of 30% is just met on value; the top five probably hold four to five times the share of numbers six to ten (our inference from the stated shares).
  • International premium spirits: Diageo 21% against 6% for Pernod, so the leader holds 3.5 times the number two.
  • US spirits: Diageo about 15-19% of value, Pernod 5-11%, Brown-Forman about 4-5%; the top five likely exceed 40% (inference).
  • Categories: cognac's top four hold about 90%; Diageo holds over half of aged Scotch inventory; stout is dominated by Guinness; tequila is moderately concentrated but being fragmented by cheap entrants; prepared cocktails are led by Gallo (27%).
  • Value chain: premium-and-above products are about 10% of spirits volume but 40% of value; private label is only 0.7% of global spirits value.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F4020th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable are market shares, and what share have new entrants taken?

Judgement: Shares are stable in aged categories (Scotch, cognac, stout), where time and inventory protect incumbents, and unstable in unaged and new-format categories (tequila, vodka, ready-to-drink), where new entrants have taken meaningful share over the last three to five years. Diageo's share loss is slow-burning but has accelerated recently.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Diageo's US share fell about 210bps over 11 years (about 20bps a year), then faster: Diageo lost 111bps of US retail share in one recent month-long read; it is losing share in seven of nine US categories.
  • Campari compounded organic sales at 8.4% a year from 2019 to 2025, about 3.5 points faster than Diageo and 5 points faster than Pernod; Espolòn rose from 19th to 7th in US tequila.
  • New entrants: US private label grew 7.6% and smaller players 23.3% in 2025 in a soft market; celebrity and craft tequilas; Gallo and brewers in prepared cocktails (from near zero to 9% of US spirits volume); Murphy's took 4.4% of Great Britain stout in two years.
  • Over half of global billion-dollar beer brands gained share over the last decade (Bernstein), showing large brands can hold share when they reinvest.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

F4219th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Does share consolidate toward the largest or the most advantaged competitors, and is Diageo among them?

Judgement: In spirits, share is currently consolidating toward the most advantaged (focused, disciplined, well-priced brands such as Aperol, Espolòn, and in India Pernod's and Radico's premium whiskies), not toward the largest. Diageo is among the most advantaged in Guinness, luxury Scotch and Indian Prestige and Above, but not in US tequila, vodka or Canadian whisky, where it is the share donor. Corporate consolidation is happening by acquisition, not organically.

Reasoning and evidence. Campari gained in US control states (+8% volume in H1 2026 against an industry -4%) while Diageo and Pernod lost; Guinness keeps gaining on-trade share in Great Britain and Ireland; Rémy Martin regained 0.6 points of US share; Pernod-Brown-Forman (aborted) and Sazerac-Brown-Forman show the consolidation impulse; Diageo itself is shrinking its perimeter. The Wisdom Library's "scale needs a bounded market" applies: Diageo's scale gives procurement and distribution advantages, but in a fragmented consumer market with low switching costs on mid-tier brands, scale does not stop share loss.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

F359th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Competitors' attitude to competition: margin or volume first, benign or loss-tolerant, and do prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: The industry has historically been benign and margin-first (premiumisation, price leadership, low private label). That has broken in the US and in mainstream tiers: prices are now falling as volume falls, led by Diageo itself. Most competitors remain margin-first in intent but are being forced into promotion by stock gluts; only Campari is clearly holding price.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Prices falling with volume: US spirits price/mix -4.5%; 72% of top SKUs cut; control-state premium brands cut 3-4% and still losing volume.
  • By competitor: Campari refuses "reductive price wars" and accepted delistings in Germany and Italy to hold price; Pernod defends margins through its €1bn programme and cut prices on 18-40% of US SKUs; Rémy pivoted from "arrogant" pricing to value (prices cut on 92% of SKUs) and is launching an affordable cognac; LVMH cut Hennessy VS 12.7% and must reinvest for two years; Bacardi cut Patrón; Sazerac and Brown-Forman sit on bourbon stocks; smaller tequila houses discount on cheap agave.
  • Diageo: now volume-first in North America by choice ("trading some price/mix for volumes", Jefferies), but committed to avoiding a broad price war.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

D4235th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and would competitors attack industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: The majors are not erratic, reactive price-setters in the "Michael O'Leary" sense; they are large, mostly family-influenced, long-horizon companies. But the attitude has shifted over 18 months to three years from benign to defensive-aggressive, and rivals are already exploiting Diageo's weakness in specific places (Campari in US aperitifs and tequila, Heineken's Murphy's against Guinness, Pernod in India). The biggest "shark" in the water is Diageo: as the largest, best-funded player, its price resets set the floor for everyone else.

Reasoning and evidence. Heineken priced Murphy's at an 80% index to Guinness using its draught system; Pernod's India growth (+19%) outran Diageo's (+3.4%) while Diageo suffered state excise disruption; cheap tequila producers flood lower tiers as agave collapses. Diageo's scale lets it absorb a reset of up to 600bps on North America margin (Deutsche Bank), which competitors with 3-4x leverage cannot match. Stability is lowest in tequila and US whiskey (gluts) and highest in Scotch luxury, cognac prestige and stout.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5742nd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The structure remains supportive at its core (time-based barriers, brands, regulation, three-tier distribution, almost no private label) but the support has become fragile: falling demand plus gluts plus a price-cutting leader plus consolidated distributors is the combination that historically breaks oligopoly discipline. The fragility is concentrated in the US and in unaged categories.

Reasoning and evidence. Barriers: aged inventory cannot be made quickly (premium Scotch needs up to 12 years); US franchise laws and advertising bans protect incumbents; punitive excise limits cheap entrants; distillers' gross margins of 60-70% cushion cost shocks. Fragility: wholesaler consolidation shifts rent downstream; retailers use data to test which brands are irreplaceable; gluts make liquid cheap for new brands; the Wisdom Library's "competitive intensity is entry minus exit" fits: exit barriers in aged spirits are high (stock cannot be liquidated), so oversupply depresses price for years rather than leaving the market.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?D4530th percentilefull answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F3810th percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading Are substitutes a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement: A moderate and slowly growing threat. Spirits gained share of throat from beer and wine for two decades; among younger drinkers that is now reversing toward lower-strength, lower-cost and non-alcoholic choices, and moderation itself is the largest "substitute".

Reasoning and evidence. Beer is more affordable than premium spirits in a downturn (Boston Beer: beer and beyond-beer volumes -3% in Q3 2026); younger generations drink six beverage categories a week against five for older cohorts; non-alcoholic beer grows three times faster than beer; US spirits growth is driven 80% by small packs under 375ml (Pernod data); cannabis is a possible but unproven substitute. Diageo is positioned in several substitutes (Guinness, Guinness 0.0, ready-to-drink, non-alcoholic spirits), which turns part of the threat into mix dilution rather than lost revenue.

Strong business SRCA and BTE; management; financial profile

C6266th percentile

Memo topic 3, Value chain, structure, model, financials, management: the Rater's score

4a

Business quality

D5546th percentile8 answers
Judgement55C-4162C-4276C-4340C-4430C-4528C-4645C-4742
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5546th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Diageo's advantages are wide and mostly durable on the supply side (aged stock, scale, regulation) and in its best brands (Guinness, luxury Scotch, Indian premium), but its demand-side advantage in the US mid-tier and tequila has been shown to be shallower than the old premiumisation model assumed, and its execution advantage was allowed to decay. The current plan is a repair of non-inferiority (price ladder, service, formats) rather than an extension of leadership. Pricing power is currently negative in North America and should not be modelled as a lever until share stabilises. For the model: hold gross margin flat to slightly down, give Guinness and India above-group growth and margin, and treat any return of US price/mix above zero as a FY2029 upside rather than a base-case input.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

C6265th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Which sustainable advantages does Diageo hold, how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement: Diageo holds a wide moat made of several layers, but it is narrowing in US spirits and widening in beer and India. The supply-side moats (aged inventory, scale, regulation) are intact; the demand-side moat (brand-driven willingness to pay in the US mid-tier and tequila) has proved weaker than assumed; the execution moat (route to market, service) has been eroded by Diageo itself and is repairable.

Layer by layer.

  • Production and time (strong, stable): over 50% of the world's aged Scotch; liquid cannot be made quickly; barrel ageing is a mechanical value-adder. The counterweight: maturing stock of $7.8bn ties up cash and could become a write-down risk if demand for aged brown spirits declines structurally.
  • Scale (strong, stable): the largest A&P budget in spirits ($3.18bn), global distribution in about 180 countries, procurement leverage, first claim on distributor attention, and the balance sheet to fund price resets (C-42).
  • Brands and consumer mind (mixed, narrowing in the US): Johnnie Walker, Guinness, Don Julio, Smirnoff and Crown Royal are category leaders, but the US evidence (share loss in seven of nine categories; double-digit volume falls in Casamigos and Don Julio after price cuts) shows that much of the tequila premium was fashion rather than a durable mental monopoly; Guinness is the counter-example, widening (record on-trade share, 13% compound growth, 77% incremental 0.0 shoppers).
  • Regulatory (strong, stable): US franchise laws, advertising bans and excise protect incumbents; appellation rules protect Scotch.
  • Route to market and service (eroded, repairable): on-time-in-full 84%, manual orders, out-of-stocks; the plan targets 98%.
  • India (strong, widening): United Spirits' leadership in Prestige and Above, with regulation shifting in favour of premium.
  • Culture and knowledge (weakened): siloed, bureaucratic, high turnover; being reset.
C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

B+7689th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading How large is Diageo's scale relative to competitors, and what does it confer?

Judgement: Diageo is roughly twice Pernod Ricard in spirits revenue and A&P, six times Campari and twenty times Rémy Cointreau. Scale confers lower unit costs, the deepest marketing budget, distributor priority, aged-stock depth and the capacity to absorb a margin reset no rival can match. It has not conferred share protection in the US, because the advantage was spent maintaining a premium-heavy, slow, complex organisation.

Reasoning and evidence. Net sales $19.6bn against Pernod about €11bn, Campari €3.05bn, Rémy €0.94bn, Brown-Forman about $4bn; A&P $3.18bn against Pernod's €1.68bn; EBIT about $5.7bn; 27,938 employees; FY2026 organic operating margin 29.4% against Pernod's 25.8% and Campari's 20.9%; North America operating margin 37% (calendar 2025). Scale downsides are visible: 200-plus brands, 60-64% manual order entry, overheads above 14% of sales (the target of 10.5% shows how much fat scale had accumulated), and the Wisdom Library's "big company disease".

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F301st percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading How high is Diageo's surplus customer utility against alternatives; is it leading, or at least not inferior?

Judgement: For consumers, product quality is not inferior, but in the US mid and upper tiers the price-value proposition became inferior (overpriced tequila, a 131% ready-to-drink price premium, too little in the $20-30 band), and for trade customers service became inferior. Diageo is leading only in Guinness, luxury Scotch, non-alcoholic beer and Indian premium; elsewhere the evidence currently disproves "not inferior", which is why prices are being cut and service rebuilt.

Reasoning and evidence. 82% of US tequila sales above $45 (market 39%); 31% of US spirits sales above $45 (market 20%); only 17.5% of Scotch sales in France below $20 (market 58%); retailers alienated by a lack of balanced price bands and out-of-stocks; Campari's bartender mindshare and on-trade activation give it superior utility to the on-trade; Guinness has no real substitute in its serve (Murphy's is priced 20% lower to compete). The fixes (accessible tiers, Casamigos price cut, service to 98%) are moves to restore non-inferiority, not to lead.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

D2827th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of utility, shown by application?

Judgement: Diageo's pricing power has been demonstrated to be weaker than its history implied: the 2021-23 price rises were followed by volume and share loss, and the company is now handing price back. Applied evidence of current pricing power exists only in narrow pockets.

Reasoning and evidence. History: a 2-3% a year price/mix algorithm; tequila prices +4.5% in the post-pandemic boom; then organic price/mix -1.6% in FY2026, US -4.2%, 67-71% of US SKUs cut, Johnnie Walker Black -25% in control states, Casamigos -10-15% recommended retail price, Deutsche Bank's finding that price cuts did not lift volume for 72% of key brands. Applied counter-evidence: Latin America Johnnie Walker repositioning lifted gross profit 18% on 28% more volume (price elasticity, not pricing power); Guinness keeps growing at premium price points; luxury Scotch in travel retail; Indian Prestige and Above. Compare Campari, which held price and accepted €11m of lost German shipments rather than cut.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

C4563rd percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Does Diageo share its scale economies with customers?

Judgement: Historically it did not (it took price above inflation and kept the margin); it is now beginning to share, partly by choice under Lewis (simplified, fairer commercial terms and category-led joint planning, as he did with suppliers at Tesco) and partly by necessity (price cuts, reinvestment of savings). Sharing is becoming de facto strategy, but it is not yet in the culture or values.

Reasoning and evidence. Lewis's stated aim is to invest the $1bn of savings "back into brand competitiveness without reducing operating profits"; management describes price repositioning as an investment in margins; the North America plan moves from transactional selling to category partnerships and rebuilt retailer margins; the Wisdom Library's "scale economies shared" pattern would predict durable share gains if this is sustained, but it requires the margin percentage to stop rising, which conflicts with consensus margins reaching 31.8% in FY2029.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

F4224th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading Do mechanical forces, rather than heroic dynamism, improve Diageo's advantage and outputs each period?

Judgement: Some mechanical forces improve the financial outputs (deleveraging lowers interest, the dividend cut retains cash, savings run-rate builds, agave costs fall, ageing liquid gains value, Guinness capacity comes on stream), but the mechanical forces on demand in developed markets run the other way (moderation, demographics, downstream power). The competitive advantage itself is not improving mechanically in US spirits; the turnaround there depends on heroic execution, the pattern the Business Foundations report flags as "mechanical moats versus heroic turnaround action".

Reasoning and evidence. Positive mechanics: net debt falls about $3bn by FY2027 with disposals and free cash flow; restructuring cash ends after FY2027, lifting free cash flow from about $2bn to about $3bn; overhead savings accrue to FY2029; Indian premiumisation and middle-class growth; Guinness 0.0 recruits new drinkers each year. Negative mechanics: each year fewer young adults drink; distributors hold structurally less stock; retailers gain data power. Net: the earnings line has a mechanical tailwind of roughly 2-3% a year from finance and cost (our estimate); the revenue line has a mechanical headwind in the US.

Diageo now has a decisive, credible management team whose early actions are the strongest evidence in the case: speed, clarity, cost delivery, capital-allocation reset and a rebuilt top team, under a chief executive with a turnaround record and a board that acted when it had to. D5542nd percentile

The Investment Analyst's heading How strong is the strategy: right and bold priorities, moat-widening, growth-oriented and dynamic?

The Investment Analyst's heading Is the company an enlightened capital allocator and dynamic repurchaser of its shares?

Judgement: Historically not: Diageo bought back stock at high multiples, paid up for premium brands late in their cycles, and over-invested in maturing stock, with ROIC falling as a result. 4c

Financial profile

D4537th percentile8 answers
Judgement45C-4922C-5045C-5155C-5245C-5378C-5428C-7633
Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

D4537th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Diageo's economic engine is high-margin and cash-generative but currently not growing: EPS has fallen about 18% from FY2022 and consensus only regains growth through overhead savings, deleveraging and the mechanical lift of disposing of low-margin EABL. The strengths are the peer-leading margin (about 29-30% EBIT), returns above the cost of capital (ROIC 13.4%), and a clearly cuttable overhead base; the weaknesses are mediocre cash conversion (maturing stock and capex above depreciation), a noisy earnings base with recurring exceptional items, a still-heavy debt load, and two-sided operating leverage that is currently working against the company in North America. The most important modelling choice for the second set is how much of the $1bn of savings is retained: the company's algorithm requires about half, peer precedent (Heineken) suggests much less, and Diageo's own FY2026 shows that profit growth came from cutting A&P rather than from sales. A balanced base case is EBIT margin up about 150-250bps by FY2029 (below consensus's about 280bps), EPS growth of about 4-7% a year from FY2028, and free cash flow of about $3bn a year from FY2028.

Judgement: Conversion is mediocre and volatile, structurally held down by maturing stock and elevated capex, and FY2027 is a trough year because of restructuring cash. Reasoning and evidence.

  • Gross margin: 60.7% (FY2020-21), 61.6% (FY2022 peak), 60.0%, 60.5%, 60.5%, 59.8% (FY2026); strongly seasonal (H1 about 61%, H2 about 58%); consensus 59.6% (FY2027) on only three brokers. Pressures: price investment in North America, ready-to-drink mix, tariffs (about 35bps on our arithmetic), negative geographic mix; offsets: agave relief from FY2028, supply savings, the exit of lower-margin EABL beer.
  • EBIT margin (FactSet adjusted): 29.6%, 29.6%, 31.1%, 31.2% (FY2023 peak), 29.4%, 28.3%, 29.0% (FY2026); consensus 30.0%, 31.3%, 31.8%, 32.3% (FY2027-30). Organic operating margin 29.4% (or 28.9%; data conflict) in FY2026, up 116bps from cost savings and the A&P cut.
  • Mechanical perimeter effect: EABL's roughly 22% margin against the group's about 29% means its exit alone adds roughly 30-40bps to the group margin (our estimate, assuming EABL revenue near $1bn).
  • Peers: Pernod 26%, Campari 20-21%, Rémy 16-18%, Heineken 15%.
C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

D2834th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How clean is the earnings base?

Judgement: Not very clean. The gap between reported and adjusted earnings is large and recurring, capex has run well above depreciation for years, free cash flow definitions exclude leases, and several below-the-line items (associate income from Moët Hennessy, minorities, a 25% tax rate, rising interest) leak earnings. None is fraudulent; together they mean adjusted EPS overstates economic earnings by a meaningful margin.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Exceptional items: reported basic EPS 78.1c against pre-exceptional 165.3c in FY2026 (106c against 164.2c in FY2025); FY2026 carried about $1.5bn of impairments (including $287m on Don Papa) and about $0.75-0.9bn of restructuring; FY2025 carried $308m (Distill Ventures) and $145m (French distribution exit, which Morgan Stanley argues was an operating cost); a further $75m in FY2026 for the Asian distribution exits; Morgan Stanley says frequent classification of operating costs as exceptional flatters earnings quality.
  • One-offs inside adjusted profit: FY2025 operating profit included $183m of fair-value gains (Morgan Stanley).
  • Depreciation versus capex: depreciation and amortisation of $745m against capex of about $1.2bn in FY2026 and about $1.25bn guided; aligning depreciation with capex would have cut the FY2024 operating margin by about 4 points (Morgan Stanley) and would cut it by about 2.5 points on FY2027 consensus (our arithmetic).
  • Below the line: tax guided at about 25% (reported 25.8% in FY2026); United Spirits' 44% minority and other minorities (consensus implies about $180m); Moët Hennessy associate profit fell sharply in FY2025 (Hennessy shipments -24.2% in 2025; LVMH wines and spirits margin at a multi-decade low); net interest about $1bn.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

F339th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How material is financial leverage, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement: Material and uncomfortable today, but on a clear, largely self-funded path to comfort. Net debt of about $20.5bn (3.1x EBITDA) makes interest about 18% of EBIT, so each 1% change in EBIT moves pre-tax profit about 1.2%; deleveraging should add roughly 2% to EPS over FY2027-28 and unlock buybacks from FY2028.

Reasoning and evidence. Net borrowings $20.48bn (June 2026), down from $21.85bn; FactSet total debt $22.2bn including leases, cash $2.0bn; net interest $1,021m (FY2026) at about 3.9%, guided about 4% in FY2027; interest cover about 5.6x EBIT; 25% of gross debt to be refinanced by December 2028 at higher rates (Deutsche Bank: -1.9% EPS by year three); Fitch affirmed A- with a negative outlook and withdrew its ratings (January 2026). Path: EABL ($2.3bn), the cricket-team proceeds (about $800m to the parent), FY2027 free cash flow of about $2bn less about $1.1bn of dividends take net debt to roughly $17.5-18bn and leverage to about 2.75x (Bernstein 2.28x); about 2.0x by FY2029. Consensus pre-tax profit already implies about $100m lower net finance costs in FY2028 than in FY2027. Peers: Pernod 3.7-3.8x, Rémy 3.2-3.5x, Campari 2.5-2.7x, Heineken 2.2-3.0x.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

D5548th percentile

Memo topic 5, Pricing power, leverage, cost control, feedback loops: the Rater's score

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
C-1718C-4528P-0433
C-17To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?F182nd percentilefull answer under 2b Internal corporate change forces
C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?D2827th percentilefull answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F3317th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Price will subtract from Diageo's margin in FY2027, be neutral to slightly positive for revenue in FY2028, and contribute only about +1 point a year to organic growth from FY2029, half the historical 2-3%. The reason is structural, not cyclical: the company is deliberately handing price back in North America to repair a price ladder that overshot (Casamigos and Don Julio above $45; 82% of US tequila sales above $45 against 39% for the market), and the gluts of agave, American whiskey and cognac mean competitors will not let it take price back. Diageo's real pricing power is confined to Guinness, Indian Prestige and Above, luxury Scotch and travel retail, which together are too small to lift the group. A hidden point matters for reading the reported numbers: hyperinflation pricing in Turkey contributed about 70bp of group organic growth in FY2026, two-thirds of it price (Morgan Stanley), so headline price/mix flatters the underlying developed-market price trend by about half a point.

The price contribution I model (organic, my base).

FY2026 actual FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030-31
Organic volume -0.4% +0.8% +0.7% +1.2% +1.3%
Organic price/mix -1.6% -0.3% +0.5% +1.0% +1.2%
Organic net sales -2.0% +0.5% +1.2% +2.2% +2.5%
Gross margin 59.8% 59.2% 59.4% 59.6% 59.7-59.8%

Evidence by pool.

  • North America (about 36% of sales, about 40-45% of profit), negative: FY2026 organic -8.4% (volume -6.7%, price/mix -1.7%); Q1 FY2027 -3.6% (volume -3.7%, price/mix +0.1%). Casamigos recommended retail price cut 10-15% and Casamigos price/mix -8.5% in October 2026; Casamigos Blanco volume back to about +4% but Reposado weak (Bernstein); Crown Royal -8.7% with price/mix -1.9% (Jefferies tracker); US spirits price/mix -4.5% (Morgan Stanley); retail prices cut on 67-71% of Diageo's US SKUs. Morgan Stanley expects North America gross margin -477bp in FY2027. My base: North America price/mix about -1.5% in FY2027, about 0% in FY2028, about +1% from FY2029, with no real price increase.
  • The price war is bounded, which is why I do not model worse: Brown-Forman sees only one major brand repositioning and 1-2% deflation on core 750ml bottles; competitor pricing across US spirits down only 0.5% (company calls, September 2026); Bernstein reports competitors held price after the Casamigos reset; Pernod Ricard (3.6-3.8x leverage) and Rémy Cointreau (near its covenant) cannot fund a long war; Campari refuses one. The glut categories (tequila, American whiskey, cognac) are where it will continue.
  • Europe (about 28% of sales after the merger with Africa), mixed: Guinness is the strongest pricing asset (Great Britain value +8.6% in FY2025 and +8.7% year to date; beer share 4.8% from 4.1%), but Murphy's at an 80% price index has taken about 5% of Great Britain stout and Great Britain off-trade Guinness growth fell from 18.9% (July) to -0.2% (October). Southern Europe and Germany are price-negative (Johnnie Walker Red at €12 against €5 private label in Spain; promotional resets with negative price/mix across Europe). Q1 FY2027 EMEA price/mix -2.0%. My base: about +1% price/mix, Guinness-led.
  • India (about 7.5% of sales), positive: United Spirits' Prestige and Above price/mix +7.5% in Q2 FY2027 (volume -1.2%), Karnataka's strength-based excise lowered shelf prices about 15% and lifted premium volume 27-28%; the UK-India agreement allowed 7-9% shelf-price cuts on bottled Scotch. Price here is mix, and part of it is given back to the consumer through lower duty. My base: +3-4% price/mix inside 7% growth.
  • Asia Pacific and Latin America, neutral to slightly positive: Q1 FY2027 Asia Pacific price/mix -2.1% (promotions, repositioning); Latin America's three-tier Johnnie Walker architecture lifted volume 28% and gross profit 18% (price elasticity, not pricing power); hyperinflation adjustments ($323m in Latin America and Caribbean reported sales in FY2026) inflate reported, not organic, figures.

Contribution to margin.

  • FY2027: price is a margin cost. The North America price investment, the shift to ready-to-drink (cans at about 30-50% gross margin against 60-70% for premium bottles; ready-to-drink grew 35% in North America in FY2026) and smaller packs take group gross margin down about 60bp despite the EABL exit and about $40m of supply savings. Every 50bp of gross margin is about $95m of EBIT, about 3c of EPS, so this is the single most important unknown inside the FY2027 profit guidance, and the company gives no gross-margin guidance.
  • FY2028-29: price stops subtracting; lagged agave relief (contracts at ₱11-13/kg against a spot of ₱4-5/kg; benefit "mainly in the outer years") and supply savings add about 20bp a year. Input-cost inflation (glass 23% of cost of goods; hedges rolling off into higher energy prices in 2027, Jefferies) offsets part of it.
  • FY2030-31: +1.2% price/mix only keeps gross margin flat-to-up about 10bp a year: in this business, price at around inflation just protects gross margin; it does not expand it.

Cone and skew. Negative skew over 18 months: a matched cut by Pernod Ricard or Bacardi on tequila and whisky would take North America price/mix to about -3% and group gross margin another 50-80bp lower (Deutsche Bank's warning of permanent gross margin contraction without volume growth). Upside is limited to Guinness and India; a US recovery in real incomes would help volume and mix before it helps price. Pricing power is the weakest foundation of the forecast, and it is the component in which my numbers sit most clearly below the company's historical algorithm.

5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7542P-0158
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F4224th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading How material is fixed-cost operating leverage, and how wide a range does it create?

Judgement: Material and two-sided. With a gross margin of about 60%, A&P of about 16% and overheads of about 14% of sales that are largely fixed, each 1% of organic sales moves EBIT by roughly 1.5-2% at constant mix; adverse mix (losing high-margin US tequila and Chinese white spirits) and price cuts can double that. A ±3% swing in organic sales therefore creates roughly a ±5-12% range in EBIT in a year.

Reasoning and evidence (the sensitivities are our arithmetic, anchored on these data).

  • History: FY2023 organic sales +6.5%, operating profit +7.0%; FY2024 -0.6% and -4.8% (Latin America mix and promotion); FY2025 +1.7% and -0.7% (overhead investment); FY2026 -2.0% and +2.0% (because A&P was cut about $480m). The FY2026 figure masks the leverage: without the A&P cut organic profit would have fallen roughly 6-7%.
  • Fixed elements: maturation warehousing and distilleries (majors rarely use more than 70% of supply capacity), overheads of more than 14% of sales, depreciation.
  • Company sensitivity: a 0.5 point change in operating margin moves basic EPS about 2% (about 3.3c).
  • Peer precedents: Heineken, a 2% volume fall cuts EPS about 5%; spirits (Morgan Stanley on Rémy), a 12% sales fall cut organic EBIT 25-30%.

For the model: run sensitivities of organic sales growth from -2% to +3% in FY2027-28 with explicit mix and A&P assumptions; the operating leverage works in Diageo's favour only once North America stops falling.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

C+5868th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Diageo's operating leverage over FY2027-31 will be driven by the cost side, not by volume. In FY2027-28 the deliberate removal of fixed overheads (the $850m programme) dominates and lifts the EBIT margin by about 210bp (including about 50bp from the EABL exit) even though reported revenue falls; from FY2029, with savings largely exhausted, the only remaining lever is volume leverage on 2-2.5% organic growth, which adds only about 15-30bp of margin a year. Volume-driven operating leverage is therefore a modest, two-sided sensitivity rather than an engine: each 1 point of organic sales growth moves EBIT by about 1.5-2% (about 2.5-3.5c of EPS), and adverse mix (losing high-margin US tequila and Chinese white spirits) can double that on the downside. Against FY2020-26, when margin was set by price/mix on the way up (FY2021-23) and by discretionary cost choices on the way down (overhead investment in FY2025, the A&P cut in FY2026), the coming years swap price for overhead as the margin driver. On my base the EBIT margin regains its FY2023 peak (31.2%) only in FY2029 and EBIT in dollars regains its FY2023 level ($6.4bn) only in FY2031.

Why operating leverage is now mostly a cost-side story.

  • The arithmetic of the cost base (FY2026, my decomposition on company and FactSet data): gross margin 59.8%; A&P 16.2% of sales (held at about 16% by policy, so it behaves as a variable cost); "overheads and other" are the residual, about 14.6% of sales (about $2.85bn, consistent with management's "way north of 14%"); EBIT 29.0%. Contribution after A&P is therefore about 43.5% of each sales dollar. One point of organic sales (about $190m) adds about $83m of EBIT (1.45%) at constant mix; because the lost volume is concentrated in the highest-gross-margin pools (US tequila, Chinese baijiu), the downside drop-through is nearer $100-110m (about 1.9%). Morgan Stanley's "85% drop-through" from revenue declines to EBIT once savings are stripped out (14-Sep-26) is the same point seen from the downside.
  • History shows margin has been set by discretionary choices since FY2024: organic sales and organic operating profit were +6.5%/+7.0% (FY2023, price-led), -0.6%/-4.8% (FY2024, Latin America destock and promotion), +1.7%/-0.7% (FY2025, overhead investment, margin -68bp), -2.0%/+2.0% (FY2026, margin +116bp because organic A&P fell 13.1%, while gross margin fell 141bp). In none of the last three years did volume set the margin; costs did.
  • Fixed elements: maturation and warehousing (aged stock of $7.8bn), distilleries running at about 65-70% utilisation across the majors (expert evidence), depreciation ($745m, rising), and overheads. These are the costs that make the downside drop-through high and that the programme attacks.

How it shapes each line (my base; FY2026 actual first).

Line FY2020-23 FY2024-26 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030 FY2031
Revenue ($m) 14,796 to 20,584 20,269, 20,245, 19,643 19,041 18,723 19,135 19,613 20,103
Organic sales growth +21.4% (FY22), +6.5% (FY23) -0.6%, +1.7%, -2.0% +0.5% +1.2% +2.2% +2.5% +2.5%
Gross margin 60.7%, 60.7%, 61.6%, 60.0% 60.5%, 60.5%, 59.8% 59.2% 59.4% 59.6% 59.7% 59.8%
A&P, % of sales (company basis) about 18% 18.1% (FY25), 16.2% (FY26) 16.3% 16.3% 16.3% 16.3% 16.3%
Overheads and other, % of sales (residual) about 14.6% (FY26) 12.9% 12.0% 11.8% 11.8% 11.7%
EBIT margin 29.6%, 29.6%, 31.1%, 31.2% 29.4%, 28.3%, 29.0% 30.0% 31.1% 31.5% 31.6% 31.8%

EBITDA margin (brokers' basis from FY26) 33.1-34.3% 32.7%, 32.0%, 32.8% 34.0% 35.3% 35.7% 36.0% 36.2%
  • FY2027 (savings against flat sales): about $380m of gross savings (40% of $850m overheads plus 25% of $150m supply chain) meets about $230m of drags (North America price investment, about $70m of net tariffs, wage and freight inflation, $25m of transactional currency) and flat-to-slightly-positive volume. Organic EBIT +3%; reported EBIT flat at $5,719m because half a year of EABL (about -$105m), other disposals (-$15m) and currency (-$25m) leave. Margin +100bp, of which about 25bp is EABL perimeter.
  • FY2028 (the peak savings year): about $530m of gross savings (55% of overheads), the full-year loss of EABL, and a transactional currency headwind as hedge gains taper (scheduled hedge gains of $88m in FY2027 fall to $14m in FY2028, company accounts). Organic EBIT +4.5%; reported EBIT +1.7%; margin +110bp (about 25bp from the second half of the EABL exit).
  • FY2029-31 (savings exhausted): only about $100m of gross savings remain in FY2029 (5% of overheads, the last supply-chain tranche). EBIT growth falls to about 3-4% on 2.2-2.5% sales: positive but small volume leverage, offset by rising depreciation (capex of about $1.25bn a year against depreciation of about $0.75bn adds about $30-35m of D&A a year, worth roughly 15-20bp of margin a year). This is consistent with Morgan Stanley's view of "de minimis" margin expansion once savings are exhausted, which I accept for FY2030-31.
  • EBITDA versus EBIT: EBITDA margin rises faster than EBIT margin (+340bp against +280bp over FY2026-31) because depreciation rises with capex; the gap is an accounting effect, not extra cash.

Where I differ from the first set and from consensus. The first set framed operating leverage as "two-sided" and volume-driven (±3% sales giving ±5-12% EBIT). That is right as a sensitivity, but the realistic range of organic growth over the next 18 months is narrower (about -1% to +2%), so volume leverage moves FY2027-28 EBIT by only about ±3-4%; the overhead programme moves it by about ±5% depending on delivery and retention. The two debates are of equal size: ±1 point of organic growth and ±10 points of savings retention (±$100m on $1bn) each move EPS by about ±3c. Consensus EBIT margins (30.0%, 31.3%, 31.8%) are close to mine; consensus EBIT is above mine from FY2028 mainly because of revenue (FY2029 consensus revenue $19,720m, +3.9%, against my $19,135m, +2.2%).

Skew and cone. For FY2027 the skew is mildly negative: a broad US price war or a slower overhead ramp would cost more margin than faster volume would add, because drop-through on lost tequila volume is higher than on regained mainstream volume (ready-to-drink and affordable tiers carry gross margins of about 30-50% against 60-70% for premium bottles). FY2027 EBIT cone: about $5.50bn to $5.85bn around $5.72bn.

Financial leverage

C-7633P-0266
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?F339th percentilefull answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

A6691st percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Financial leverage turns from Diageo's largest EPS headwind of FY2022-26 into its most predictable EPS tailwind of FY2027-31. Net debt falls by about $4bn in FY2027 (disposals plus free cash flow) and by a further $2.5bn by FY2029, cutting net finance charges by about $270m by FY2029 (worth about 9c of EPS, about 5.5%), while refinancing at higher rates claws back only about 0.4 percentage points of the effective rate. The balance sheet reaches the company's 2.5-3.0x range in FY2027 and about 2.0x in FY2029. The most important and least-noticed point is arithmetic: the company's own targets (about $8bn of cumulative free cash flow over FY2027-29, about 2.75x leverage at FY2027, about 2.0x at FY2029) leave almost no room for buybacks before FY2029; buybacks of the size mooted ($2bn) are only consistent with the 2.0x target if free cash flow beats $8bn by about $1bn or Shui Jing Fang is sold. My base, with $8.8bn of free cash flow, carries $0.5bn of buybacks in FY2028 and $1.0bn in FY2029, then $2bn a year from FY2030.

The debt and interest mechanics (company accounts unless stated).

  • Structure: gross borrowings $21.5bn at 30-Jun-26 (due within a year $2.45bn, one to three years $4.25bn, three to five years $3.77bn); fixed-rate net borrowings $18.5bn against floating $1.4bn (policy 70-90% fixed); effective interest rate 4.3% (FY2024), 4.1%, 3.9% (FY2026), guided about 4.0% for FY2027. FactSet's "net interest" of $1,021m in FY2026 also contains other finance charges (hyperinflation, pensions, lease interest) of roughly $150-200m.
  • Refinancing: 25% of gross debt must be refinanced by December 2028 (Deutsche Bank, 27-Oct-26); a 6.5% roll-over rate would cut EPS by 1.9% by year three; refinancing all debt due within a year at +100bp costs only $24m (Morgan Stanley, 20-Oct-26). The exposure is real but slow; I add about 10bp a year to the effective rate (4.0% in FY2027 to 4.4% in FY2031).

My net debt and interest path (company net borrowings basis; my base).

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030 FY2031
Net borrowings, year end ($bn) 20.5 16.5 15.0 14.0 13.9 13.7
Leverage (net borrowings / EBITDA) 3.1x about 2.55x (company basis about 2.65x) 2.3x 2.05x 1.95x 1.9x
Net finance charges incl. other finance charges ($m) -1,021 -910 -797 -749 -738 -744
Interest cover (EBIT / net finance charges) 5.6x 6.3x 7.3x 8.0x 8.4x 8.6x
Buybacks ($bn) 0 0 0.5 1.0 2.0 2.0

FY2027 bridge: $20.5bn less free cash flow $2.2bn, plus dividends $1.12bn and minority dividends $0.12bn, less EABL proceeds $2.3bn and about $0.8bn of net cash from the Royal Challengers Bengaluru (RCB) sale retained by the group after Indian tax and the minority holders' share of United Spirits' special dividend.

Effect on EPS.

  • Deleveraging: lower net finance charges add about +4c in FY2027, +7.5c by FY2028 and +9c by FY2029 against FY2026 (after tax at 24%). I disagree with the first set on two points here: it said interest would be "roughly flat in dollars as lower debt offsets higher rates" (C-07) and that deleveraging "should add roughly 2% to EPS over FY2027-28" (C-76). The debt reduction (about $5.5bn, 27%) is far larger than the rate rise (about +0.1-0.2 points by FY2028), so the gain is about 4.5% of EPS by FY2028, not 2%.
  • But the disposals that fund it are EPS-dilutive: EABL's attributable net profit is about $175m a year (Bernstein: the minorities' share is $94m), while the interest saved on $2.3bn at 4% is about $70m after tax; the net dilution is about $105m, roughly 4.5-5c (about 3% of EPS). Morgan Stanley's pro forma puts the FY2027 dilution at 3c. This is why EPS is flat in FY2027 and only +6% in FY2028 despite a sharp fall in interest: the deleveraging largely pays for the lost EABL profit.
  • Buybacks: $0.5bn in FY2028 and $1.0bn in FY2029 at about $27-29 a share remove about 53m shares (2.4%) by FY2029, worth about 1.4% of FY2029 EPS (weighted-average effect); $2bn a year from FY2030 adds about 3% a year to EPS from FY2030. Consensus implies an almost flat share count to FY2029 (about 2,217m shares), so buyback upside is largely outside consensus, but not as far outside as the $2bn-a-year narrative suggests.
  • The buyback arithmetic: starting from the company's 2.75x (about $17.7bn) at FY2027, $8bn of FY2027-29 cash less about $2.4bn of FY2028-29 dividends and minority payments takes net borrowings to about $14.3bn, about 2.07x on about $6.9bn of EBITDA, with zero buybacks. Jefferies' "c.$2bn from FY2028" would leave leverage near 2.4x at FY2029 on the company's own cash numbers. My higher free cash flow ($8.8bn: restructuring cash as guided, lower cash interest, modest working-capital build) and RCB proceeds are what create room for $1.5bn by FY2029. A Shui Jing Fang sale (Bernstein: about $1.8-2.3bn, 0.3x of leverage, 0.4% EPS dilution) would be accretive if recycled into buybacks at about 14x earnings.

Effect on the balance sheet. Shareholders' equity rebuilds quickly because the dividend payout is now about 31% of EPS (consensus equity $13.3bn at FY2027 to $18.1bn at FY2029), which lifts the ROIC denominator but lowers leverage. Maturing stock ($7.8bn) continues to grow slowly and keeps working capital heavy (net working capital about 36-37% of sales). The risk to the path is timing: the RCB sale has a long-stop date in mid-December 2026 and EABL completion could slip into early 2027; a six-month slip would leave FY2027 leverage near 2.9x and push the first buyback into FY2029, costing about 1c of FY2028 EPS.

Cone and skew. This is the narrowest cone in the model: FY2028 net finance charges of about $0.75-0.85bn in almost any operating scenario, because the debt is fixed and the disposals are signed. The skew is positive (a Shui Jing Fang sale, an earlier buyback, a tariff refund), with the main negative being a delay to the disposals or a rating-driven preference to hold leverage below 2.0x.

5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7780P-0362
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

A8099th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Overheads are highly cuttable and are the main lever (about a quarter of them, $850m, is targeted); A&P is only modestly cuttable without damage (the non-working share can still fall, but working media should rise); cost of goods offers small, steady savings ($150m) against inflation and tariffs.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Overheads (about 14%+ of sales, roughly $2.8bn): to about 10.5% of sales, a cut of about $650-700m on today's sales; levers are the five-region, 23-market framework, removal of duplication, headcount (already -6.4%; the next leg is senior roles, Jefferies), shared services and systems; 40% of the $850m lands in FY2027.
  • A&P ($3.18bn, 16.2%): already cut 13%; non-working and development costs fell from 21% to 15% of A&P with about 10% targeted, so roughly $150m more of efficiency is available (our arithmetic); held at about 16% of sales to FY2029; further cuts would accelerate share loss.
  • Cost of goods (about 40% of sales): glass, grain, agave, energy, conversion; $150m of supply savings (25% in FY2027); majors rarely use over 70% of capacity, so plant consolidation is possible but requires closing units; the group delivered $180m of supply savings in FY2026.
  • Not cuttable: tariffs, excise, maturing-stock carrying cost.
P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

B+6289th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The gross $1bn will be delivered, probably slightly early and possibly exceeded, but only about 40% of it (about $400m, about 210bp of FY2029 sales) is likely to reach EBIT, with the rest absorbed by North American price investment, tariffs, cost inflation and reinvestment in selling capability. Timing is front-loaded: about $150m net in FY2027 and about $210m net in FY2028, then about $40m in FY2029. Cash goes the other way first: about $850m of restructuring cash in FY2027 and about $150m in FY2028, so the programme is cash-negative until about FY2029. My retention assumption sits between Jefferies (about 30%) and the company's algorithm (about half), and slightly below consensus (about 45%).

What is being cut, and when (company, Capital Markets Day 6-Aug-26, and Jhangiani).

$m FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 Total
Overhead savings, gross (40% / 55% / 5% of $850m) 340 468 42 850
Supply-chain savings, gross (25% in FY2027; rest my phasing) 38 60 52 150
Total gross savings in the year about 380 about 530 about 95 about 1,000
Retained in EBIT (my base, about 40%) about 150 about 210 about 40 about 400
Restructuring cash (company: about $850m in FY2027; rest my estimate) -850 -150 -50 about -1,050 of the $1.2bn charge

The target is overheads from "way north of 14%" to about 10.5% of sales. My base takes the overhead-and-other ratio from about 14.6% to about 11.8% by FY2029, so it does not reach 10.5%: the difference is wage and systems inflation on the remaining base (about 3% a year on about $2bn), stranded central costs after the African disposals (Jefferies, 5-Nov-26), and money put back into sales capability and service (the on-time-in-full target of 98% from 84%).

Why about 40% retention, not 50% or more.

  • Peer precedent is consistent and sobering: only 25% of Heineken's more than €3bn of gross savings reached net organic profit growth (Jefferies, 28-Oct-26); Campari's 70bp of SG&A savings and 100bp of gross margin gain were matched by a 100bp step-up in A&P (Morgan Stanley, 5-Mar-26); Pernod Ricard delivered about €500m of its €1bn in FY2026 but keeps A&P at about 16% of sales, so net margin gain is modest; Rémy Cointreau's €100m must be "substantially reinvested" (Jefferies, 28-Oct-26). Carlsberg's Funding our Journey (+300bp of EBIT margin, 2015-19) is the only clear precedent of high retention, and it ran in a growing market.
  • Diageo's own recent precedent is weaker than the headline: Accelerate delivered $540m in FY2026 against $300m guided, but $230m of it came from A&P and trade investment (not repeatable as overhead), $180m from supply and only $130m from corporate overheads; stripping savings out, the underlying drop-through from falling sales was 85% (Morgan Stanley). FY2026's 116bp margin gain came from the 13% A&P cut while gross margin fell 141bp. FY2027 has to lap that A&P benefit without repeating it.
  • The drags are identifiable and sized: North America price investment (Morgan Stanley expects North America gross margin -477bp in FY2027; on about 36% of sales that alone is about 170bp of group gross margin if unmitigated; I assume about 60bp net at group level after the EABL exit, supply savings and positive mix elsewhere), net tariffs of about $70m (35bp), transactional currency of -$25m in FY2027 and about -$50m in FY2028, and overhead inflation of about $60m a year.
  • Why not lower than 40%: the company keeps A&P at about 16% of sales rather than raising it (unlike Campari), most of the drags are front-loaded into FY2027, and Lewis's incentives (free cash flow, EPS and ROIC) and the over-delivery record argue that the gross number will be at or above $1bn.

Where it lands in the line items.

  • Gross margin: supply savings (about $150m) and agave relief from FY2028 offset part of the price investment and ready-to-drink mix: 59.8% (FY2026) to 59.2% (FY2027), then back to 59.8% by FY2031.
  • A&P: flat at about 16.3% of sales; in dollars it rises with sales from FY2029 (about +$80m a year). It is not a source of further savings in my base; the non-working share (15% of A&P against a target of about 10%) offers about $150m of efficiency that I assume is reinvested in working media.
  • Overheads: the main beneficiary: about -2.8 points of sales by FY2029.
  • Depreciation: not a saving; it rises about $30-35m a year because capex runs at about $1.25bn.
  • Free cash flow: FY2027 is depressed by about $850m of restructuring cash (company guidance: about $2bn after it); the run-rate savings lift FY2028-29 free cash flow by about $0.4-0.5bn a year against an FY2027 base excluding restructuring.

Cone and skew. Gross delivery is skewed upward (Accelerate was over-delivered by 80%; Lewis's record at Tesco was cost and supplier-terms repair). Retention is skewed downward (every peer reinvested more than planned; share loss in 65% of the North America portfolio will keep the pressure to reinvest). Net EBIT effect by FY2029: about $250m to $550m around $400m. The first test is the H1 FY2027 results on 10-Feb-27: the overhead line and the North America margin will show whether the FY2027 retention is closer to 30% or 50%.

5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4742P-0548

P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

D4838th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. The loops at work sort into two clean groups, and the split is itself the key insight. The reinforcing loops that are working are financial and mechanical (deleveraging to lower interest to more free cash flow to buybacks; capacity to share in Guinness; premiumisation in India), and they act mostly below the gross line and below EBIT. The reinforcing loop that is hurting is commercial and sits at the top of the income statement in North America (less A&P, share loss, forced price investment, lower gross margin, less money for brands), and it is being slowed but not yet reversed by balancing forces (indebted competitors who cannot follow price down, the end of destocking, the surgical scope of the price cuts). For the model this means: high confidence that EPS and free cash flow compound at about 6% and 8% a year from FY2028, low confidence that revenue compounds at more than about 2%, and a quality-of-earnings gap that persists through FY2029, because growth below EBIT is not the same as growth in the franchise.

Loop by loop: status, evidence and the line it drives.

Loop Type and status now Evidence (dated) Line items affected, my base
Disposals and free cash flow to lower debt to lower interest to buybacks Reinforcing, positive, operating, high confidence EABL $2.3bn (completion by 31-Dec-26); RCB about INR 166.6bn (long-stop mid-Dec-26); dividend halved to 50c (Feb-26); leverage 3.4x to 3.1x (FY2026) Net finance -$1,021m to -$749m by FY2029; free cash flow $3.2-3.9bn from FY2028; share count -2.4% by FY2029, -8% by FY2031

Transactional currency hedges Balancing with a lag Hedges up to 36 months at about 75%; hedge gains $88m (FY2027) to $14m (FY2028) EBIT -$25m (FY2027), about -$50m (FY2028), about -$30m (FY2029)
Capex above depreciation to rising depreciation Mechanical, slow Capex about $1.25bn against depreciation about $0.75bn D&A $755m (FY2027) rising to $885m (FY2031); EBIT margin drag of about 15-20bp a year

How the loops net out in the line items.

  • Revenue: the commercial loops roughly cancel: the North America negative loop costs about 1 point of group growth a year in FY2027, falling to about 0 by FY2029, while Guinness and India add about 1-1.5 points. That is why organic growth climbs only from about 0.5% to about 2.5%.
  • Gross margin: the negative North America loop dominates in FY2027 (-60bp); the positive loops (Guinness mix, India mix, agave) only bring it back by FY2031.
  • EBIT: the cost programme is not a loop; it is a one-off step that buys time for the commercial loops to turn. If the North America loop has not turned by FY2029, the step will have been spent on defending share rather than compounding margin.
  • Below EBIT, free cash flow and EPS: the financial loop is the one compounding engine with high confidence; it supplies about half of my FY2026-29 EPS growth.

Where the evidence is weakest and what would decide it. The causal link from the A&P cut to share loss is inferred, not isolated, because demand weakness and destocking overlapped with it. The decisive indicators over the next six months are monthly US scanner and NABCA data on Diageo's share in tequila and Canadian whisky, whether Casamigos Blanco's volume gain persists beyond the reset, the North America margin in the H1 FY2027 results (10-Feb-27), and whether Pernod Ricard and Bacardi match prices after the holiday season.

Guidance has been reset to a credible, specific floor: flat FY2027 sales, low-to-mid-single-digit organic profit growth built on dated savings, about $2bn of free cash flow after restructuring cash, and a three-year algorithm whose cost, cash and leverage legs are well specified. Case simplicity

Judgement58C-7930D-0272D-0355D-0465D-0540D-0668
Group judgement

Risks

C5859th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The forecasts for the next 18 months are predictable in the narrow sense and the investment case is less predictable than the forecasts. In the narrow sense: the earlier sets are congruent on the thesis, and the errors I found in their arithmetic cancel at the EPS line (D-01); the 18-month forecast rests on strengths Diageo has demonstrated, and asks of the United States only a slower decline (D-02); the EPS growth is concentrated in two internal, dated drivers, with the main subtractions known in advance (D-03); the EPS cone is about -5% to +3% for FY2027 and about -7% to +6% for FY2028, with about a 75% chance of FY2027 landing within 3% of $1.67 (D-04); and the chain from the company's stated building blocks to EPS is clear for almost all of that change (D-06).

The case is less predictable than that for four reasons the Consumer should carry into the memo. First, the predictability is of a number, not of the franchise: management can protect profit by keeping more of the savings, so the most likely disappointment is EPS met with sales, share and gross margin missed, and nothing the company discloses will show in time whether competitiveness is improving (D-05, D-06). Second, plausibility decays with the horizon: from FY2028 the thesis needs share-holding execution, service, tequila pricing and retention discipline that Diageo has not shown, and I rate the FY2029 revenue and share targets at no better than 40% (D-02). Third, the tails are negative and mostly political or in the distribution channel (trade measures on tequila and Canadian whisky, a matched price war, a distributor shock), about 10-12% in aggregate over 18 months (D-04). Fourth, the share price is mainly a spirits-industry trade: about half its daily variance and three-quarters of its recent gain are common to peers, the multiple is about two-thirds industry-driven, and the model's return depends chiefly on the multiple (D-07).

The steer: treat FY2027 EPS of about $1.67 and FY2028 of about $1.75 as high-confidence figures, on consensus; treat the composition (North America about -4.5%, gross margin down about 60 basis points, retention about 40%) as the thing to monitor; and treat 10-Feb-27 as the test of both. The readings that would raise my confidence are an overhead ratio falling by more than 100 basis points with gross margin down by less than 100 and advertising at or above 16% of sales; the reading that would lower it is profit growth delivered with advertising cut again.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

C-7254th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does past and present delivery make the expected change plausible, and does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has?

Judgement. Plausibility depends on the horizon, and that is the most useful way to state it. Over the next 18 months the forecast relies almost entirely on strengths Diageo demonstrably has: taking out cost, selling assets well, cutting capital spending, generating cash, growing Guinness and Indian premium spirits. It asks of the weak part of the business (United States spirits) only that it declines more slowly, not that it recovers. On that horizon the expected change is plausible, and I would put the probability of the base EPS path being met within 3% at about 75%. Beyond 18 months the thesis needs four strengths Diageo does not demonstrably have: execution that holds and then gains share in the United States, customer service at the level it has promised, pricing power in tequila, and the discipline to keep savings rather than spend them. The FY2029 targets (2.5% organic growth, share gains, margins sustained after the savings end) rest on those, and I rate them at no better than 40% likely. The plan is plausible as a stabilisation and not yet as a recovery.

What the forecast needs, and whether Diageo has shown it.

What the forecast needs Demonstrated? Evidence When it matters
Gross savings of $1bn, 40% of overheads in FY2027 Largely Accelerate delivered $540m in FY2026 against $300m guided; headcount down 6.4%; the $752m charge is already taken. But only $130m of that came from corporate overheads ($230m came from advertising and trade spend, $180m from supply); the new programme needs $850m from overheads FY2027-28
Keeping about 40% of the savings in profit Not shown Morgan Stanley: more than $3.5bn of cumulative savings since 2017 did not prevent the margin from falling; FY2026's 116 basis points of margin came from a 13% cut in advertising while gross margin fell 141 basis points; peers kept little (Heineken about 25%; Pernod Ricard's margin fell 35 basis points in the year it delivered €500m) FY2027-29
Deleveraging through disposals and cash Yes East African Breweries at 17 times EBITDA, the cricket team at 84 times; dividend halved; FY2026 free cash flow $3.21bn against a $3bn floor; leverage 3.4 to 3.1 times FY2027
Growth outside the United States Yes Guinness 13% compound growth FY2023-26 with a return on capital of about 30%; United Spirits' Prestige and Above about 90% of sales and growing 6-10%; Latin America recovered Throughout
