Holding United States share in FY2028, gaining in FY2029 No Share down from 17.2% (2014) to 15.1% (2025); no sequential improvement in scanner data; Diageo's own Smirnoff price reset in the middle of the last decade failed to restore growth (Morgan Stanley) FY2028-29
Service: on-time-in-full from 84% to 98% No A target only; order fulfilment was below 80% in 2025; staff turnover of 20.2% and, by expert testimony, damaged distributor relationships; the second-largest United States distributor (RNDC) entered liquidation in July 2026 FY2028-29
Pricing power in tequila No Casamigos price/mix -5% to -6%; Becle's unit revenue -8% and margin down 16 points; agave at a fraction of its peak, so cheap entrants keep coming FY2029

Three points that shape the judgement.

  • The record is strong where the next 18 months need it and weak where the following 18 need it. Diageo's forecast errors over ten years sat in revenue and volume, not in cost (the AlphaSense work on outcome dispersion). A forecast whose 18-month growth comes from cost and finance is therefore leaning on the reliable half of the record.
  • Retention is a choice, not a capability, and that cuts both ways. Management can meet a profit target by spending less of the savings, as it did in FY2026. That makes the 18-month profit number more plausible and the three-year franchise less so: every precedent in the files in which a drinks or staples company cut brand spending and then tried to regain share through price ended with lower gross margin and no share recovery (Deutsche Bank's study: 483 basis points of cumulative gross margin lost by companies that cut brand spending; Anheuser-Busch after 2008: $2.25bn of synergies, seven years of flat revenue, profit down excluding the synergies). I found no precedent of success from a comparable starting point; Campari, the company gaining United States share, is doing the opposite (raising brand spending, holding price).
  • The chief executive's precedent supports the sequence, not the speed. At Tesco, Sir Dave Lewis restored profit in about 18 months and like-for-like growth in about the same time, reinstated the dividend after three years, and saw no re-rating during his tenure. The tools that worked there (range reduction, supplier terms, price investment against discounters) translate to the 70% of Diageo's sales made through retailers and not to brand building or the on-trade.
D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?C5563rd percentilefull answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

B+6588th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. The cone is narrow for EPS, wide for revenue and gross margin, and very wide for the share price; the three should not be confused. For EPS I put FY2027 at $1.58 to $1.72 around a base of $1.67 (about -5% to +3%), with about a 75% probability of landing within 3% of the base, and FY2028 at $1.63 to $1.85 around $1.75 (about -7% to +6%). The skew is mildly negative for FY2027 and roughly symmetric for FY2028 inside the cone, but the events that would take the outcome outside the cone (trade measures, a matched price war, a channel shock) are almost all negative and I give them about a 10-12% probability over the 18 months. So the EPS forecast is predictable by the standards of a turnaround. Two qualifications matter more than the width. First, part of the narrowness is manufactured: management can hold profit by spending less of the savings, so the most likely bad outcome is not an EPS miss but EPS delivered with sales, share and gross margin missed. Second, the investment outcome depends far more on the multiple, whose plausible range is several times wider than that of EPS.

The cone by line (my base in brackets).

FY2027 FY2028
Organic net sales growth -1.5% to +1.5% (+0.3% to +0.5%) -1% to +3% (+1.2%)
North America organic -6% to -2% (about -4.5%) -4% to +1% (-1.2%)
Gross margin 58.6% to 59.6% (59.2%) 58.6% to 59.9% (59.4%)
EBIT ($bn) 5.50 to 5.85 (5.72) 5.55 to 6.05 (5.82)
EPS ($) 1.58 to 1.72 (1.67) 1.63 to 1.85 (1.75)
Free cash flow, company definition ($bn) 1.9 to 2.4 (2.2) 2.8 to 3.5 (3.2)
Net borrowings at year end ($bn) 16.2 to 19.0 (about 16.6) 14.8 to 17.5 (about 15.4)

The upper end of net borrowings is the case in which the sale of East African Breweries slips beyond June 2027 (leverage of about 2.95 times and no buyback before FY2029); it is a timing risk, not an earnings risk, since the business would still be consolidated.

Scenarios (the second set's, restated on my figures). Bear, about 25%: North America about -6% with tequila and whisky price cuts matched by competitors, retention of savings near 25%, gross margin down about 120 basis points; EPS about $1.58 and $1.64. Base, about 55%: $1.67 and $1.75. Bull, about 20%: North America about -2%, retention near 55%, the Indian customs dispute settled; EPS about $1.72 and $1.84 with a $1bn buyback in FY2028. Outside the cone, about 10-12%: the measures in D-06 and C-81 (for example the removal of the tequila exemption under the North American trade agreement, which Fitch sizes at up to $600m, about 10% of EBIT; or the proclamation restricting certain Canadian alcoholic imports that a law firm reported on 18-Nov-26, whose effect on Crown Royal I cannot size from the files); EPS of $1.40-1.55, close to Morgan Stanley's bear case of $1.39.

Why the EPS cone is narrow.

  • About 17 of the 22 cents of gross EPS gain to FY2028 come from two drivers that are already set in motion: the restructuring charge is taken, 1,922 roles have gone, and the debt is fixed-rate (70-90% by policy) with the disposals signed.
  • The main subtractions (East African Breweries, the fall in scheduled hedge gains from $88m to $14m) are known in advance.
  • Brokers agree: the FactSet range for FY2027 EPS is $1.60 to $1.72 across 17 brokers (about ±3.5%), and $1.69 to $1.84 for FY2028. The views outside it are identifiable: Bernstein at about $1.82, Morgan Stanley's pro forma after disposals at about $1.56-1.63.
  • By 10-Feb-27 about 57% of the year's EBITDA will be reported (the first half is the larger half). The cone narrows less than that suggests, because the savings, the perimeter change and the hard and easy comparisons all fall in the second half.

What history says about the tails. In FY2026, a year with no shock, EPS of 165.3c came in 2-5% below the brokers' models of twelve months earlier (168-174c) even though cost savings beat guidance by $240m: revenue took back more than cost gave. In FY2024, a shock year, consensus EPS fell 17% in twelve months and the shares fell 11% on the day of the November 2023 warning. On every occasion the cause was revenue, through channel inventory management could not see. The destock has ended and guidance has been rebased, so the chance of a repeat is lower, but the channel is not healthy: one large United States distributor entered liquidation in July 2026, the wholesaler inventory-to-sales ratio is 1.57-1.63 against a norm of 1.50, and retailers are cutting back-room stock.

The skew. Negative events are larger and more numerous than positive ones: a matched price war (about $150-400m of EBIT), tariff escalation (up to $600m), restrictions on Canadian imports (unsized), an adverse Indian customs ruling (small at group level: India is about 4% of profit), a delay to disposals. The positives are a tariff refund (unsized and probably reported as exceptional), a Shui Jing Fang sale (cash, slightly dilutive), an earlier buyback (about 0.5% of EPS) and a faster recovery at Moët Hennessy (about 1 cent). Management's discretion over reinvestment trims the negative skew in EPS and transfers it to the franchise: I put the probability of "EPS within 3% of base, but organic sales or North America below the company's guidance" at about 20-25%. The second set's point applies with more force than it gave it: a result of that kind would be read negatively even though EPS is met.

The share-price cone. The EPS cone is about ±5%. The forward multiple has ranged from 10.9 to 25.5 times over five years; brokers' scenario values run from 1,000p (Jefferies' downside) and 1,120p (Morgan Stanley's bear case, 9.2 times) to 3,000p (Jefferies' upside), and published targets from 1,570p to 2,470p, against 1,880p. Predictable EPS does not make a predictable return here (see D-07).

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

F4016th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. Moderately helpful, and lopsided in a way the Consumer should state plainly: Diageo's disclosure is best where the uncertainty is least (costs, cash, capital, the balance sheet) and thinnest where the uncertainty is greatest (United States sell-out, price investment, gross margin, how much of the savings is kept). In the next six months the company will publish profit once (the half-year results on 10-Feb-27) and sales once more (the third-quarter statement in May 2027). The gap is partly filled from outside: the most uncertain driver in the case, United States consumer off-take, is also the most frequently measured, monthly and by brand, by third parties; and listed subsidiaries and the associate report separately on about 15% of profit. The case is therefore trackable, but mostly not through the company, and the external data map loosely onto reported results.

What will be disclosed between now and late May 2027.

When What What it tests
Four-weekly and monthly Scanner data (Nielsen, Circana) and control-state data (NABCA) by brand, with price/mix; brokers' trackers United States off-take and share; Casamigos, Don Julio, Crown Royal, Johnnie Walker, Smirnoff; whether competitors match price
December 2026 Completion of East African Breweries (expected by the end of 2026); cricket-team sale (long-stop date mid-December); Brown-Forman's quarter Leverage path; United States whiskey
Late January 2027 LVMH's full year (wines and spirits); United Spirits' and Shui Jing Fang's quarters Associate income; India (customs, excise); China
10-Feb-27 Half-year results: the only full income statement, cash flow and balance sheet in the window; the first five-region segment note Everything below
February-March 2027 Pernod Ricard (18-Feb-27), Campari, Heineken, Rémy Cointreau United States destocking at peers; Murphy's; price discipline
May 2027 Third-quarter trading statement (sales only) The second-half comparison; North America

What the company discloses well. The savings by programme and by year; the restructuring charge and cash; capital spending; the tax and interest rates; tariffs with the rates assumed; currency, including the schedule of hedge gains in the accounts; leverage; organic sales by region each quarter split into volume and price/mix; an aide-memoire before each event; and, from this half-year, operating profit for five regions with India separate.

What it does not disclose. Profit at the first and third quarters; the net amount of savings kept after reinvestment; a gross margin or North American margin outlook (the sources disagree on whether any was given); depletions against shipments and distributor stock, beyond the word "appropriate"; United States share (management declined to comment at the first quarter); net prices after trade terms; the half-year phasing of the $850m of restructuring cash; the composition of Corporate costs; the contribution of Turkish hyperinflation to organic growth (about 70 basis points in FY2026, by Morgan Stanley's estimate); and the timing of any buyback.

Why the external data help less than their frequency suggests. They cover retail sales for home consumption and miss bars, restaurants and many independent shops; they measure consumer purchases, not Diageo's shipments. In the first quarter the scanner data showed Diageo down about 5-6%, consensus expected North America down 6.7% and the company reported -3.6% because of shipment timing; Johnnie Walker fell 10% in October scanner data yet grew in reported North American sales. Each monthly reading moves the shares (C-82) while predicting the next reported number only loosely.

What to look for on 10-Feb-27 (the readings that would confirm my base). Organic net sales for the half at or above zero; North America no worse than about -5.5%; organic operating profit growth of at least 1-2% with the margin up (the year is weighted to the second half); the ratio of overheads to sales down by at least 100 basis points; gross margin down by no more than about 100 basis points and the North American margin by no more than about 200; advertising and promotion at or above 16% of sales; restructuring cash and working capital consistent with about $2bn of free cash flow; both disposals completed; and, for the corrections above, the net finance charge (I expect about $430m for the half, before the disposal proceeds reduce debt), the tax charge and its basis, and the profit attributable to minorities.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

A6891st percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into operational drivers, onto revenue, cost and margin lines, and through to EPS; and how clear is the external transmission from the value chain to EPS?

Judgement. The internal chain is clear, and quantified, for everything on the cost and financing side, and unclear for the route from commercial actions to gross profit. Roughly 85-90% of the EPS change I forecast over the next 18 months can be traced step by step from a stated company number to a named line to EPS; none of the franchise outcome can. The external transmission is clear in direction and usually measurable, but loose in timing, and several external forces act on the same North American gross margin so that their individual effects cannot be separated in reported figures. Even the part that looks simplest, from operating profit to EPS, is less transparent than it appears: the second set mis-stated finance charges and minorities by about $170m and $35-80m because the standard data do not show the company's own figures.

The company also gives two summary sensitivities that tie the chain together: half a point of operating margin is about 2% of EPS, and a 10% move in the dollar is about ±$200m of revenue, ±$66m of EBITDA and ±2.96 cents of EPS.

Where the chain breaks.

  • Net savings. The plan's central number, how much of the $1bn reaches profit, is not disclosed and cannot be observed directly: reinvestment appears as lower price, lower gross margin and trade terms, not as a line.
  • Gross margin. Price investment, canned-cocktail mix (gross margins of about 50% against more than 60% for full-strength spirits), tariffs, agave relief and supply savings all pass through one line with no bridge.
  • Below operating profit. Associates (Moët Hennessy is controlled by LVMH; Diageo booked $227m of profit and received $108m in cash in FY2026), minorities (about $220m), the classification of exceptional items, and disposals that some brokers have removed from their forecasts and others have not.

The memo can show with confidence how the plan produces EPS of about $1.75 in FY2028; it cannot show, from anything the company discloses, whether the business is becoming more competitive while it does so.

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

B3577th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading Perception- or sentiment-driven sudden risks, including events elsewhere in the sector or market

Judgement: High. Diageo's shares are exposed to frequent, visible data points (monthly US scanner and state-store data), to peer read-across, to health headlines, and to a sector rally that was multiple-led and could reverse if investors rotate out of defensives. After a 45% rise from the 52-week low, part of the recovery is priced and sentiment can swing quickly.

Reasoning and evidence. Monthly Nielsen and NABCA data move the stock (Diageo's US off-trade -6.1% against a market +0.6% in the latest read); Pernod's H1 FY2027 results on 18 February 2027, Brown-Forman and Campari prints transmit sector sentiment; GLP-1 and Gen Z abstinence headlines; Deutsche Bank warns the sector rally came from exogenous factors and multiple expansion and that, once data gaps close, structural margin pressure could trigger a rapid de-rating; the Fitness for Fighting digest judges the defensive rotation "may already be paid for" and warns that "the leveraged defensive de-rates first", which applies to Diageo at 3.1x. Offsets: short interest is very low (0.41% of float, 1.59 days to cover), so there is no squeeze risk but also no forced-buying support; options markets priced a 26-28% probability of large declines in early 2026.

Track record (guidance raise/sell-side upgrades) ~L2-3Y

C-61How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?F4013th percentilefull answer under 4b Management quality
C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

D3529th percentile

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Diageo's recent record is of cutting top-line guidance and raising cost targets; Lewis has no upgrade record at Diageo yet, and his early behaviour is to set a floor and hold it (FY2027 guidance was reiterated, not raised, after a Q1 beat). The pattern to expect is "under-promise on sales, over-deliver on cost", with upgrades, if any, coming from savings and cash rather than revenue.

Reasoning and evidence. Raised: Accelerate from $500m to $625m (August 2025) and over-delivered in FY2026 ($540m against $300m); the ROIC improvement target stepped up to about 130bps from 80bps. Cut: FY2026 guidance and the dividend at the half-year (February 2026); the 5-7% medium-term target abandoned (2025); profit warnings in late FY2024. Behaviour: Jefferies and others suspect deliberate low-balling of near-term targets to rebuild credibility through beats ("re-establishing a habit of beating consensus is highly likely to trigger multiple expansion"); Lewis at Tesco similarly prioritised credibility over ambition. The Wisdom Library's "sandbagging precision management archetype" is a plausible fit but unproven at Diageo.

C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

F3011th percentile

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

The Investment Analyst's heading What is the record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and estimates over one, three and five years?

Judgement: Over the last twelve months Diageo has beaten a lowered bar (FY2026 free cash flow, profit and EPS ahead; Q1 FY2027 sales ahead); over three and five years its record is poor, with a profit warning, guidance cuts, an abandoned medium-term target and a prolonged downgrade cycle in which actual EPS undershot the brokers' initial models. In comparable conditions (the FY2015 destock, the FY2024 Latin America destock) Diageo missed.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • One year: FY2026 operating profit about 2% and EPS about 3% ahead of expectations (Bernstein), free cash flow $3.21bn against the $3bn floor and $2.9bn consensus, organic sales -2% in line; Q1 FY2027 organic +1.4% against -0.4%, North America -3.6% against -6.7%, but India 3.4% against 6.4% and EMEA 2.4% against 3.4% missed.
  • Three years: the November 2023 profit warning (Latin America); FY2024 consensus EPS -17% over twelve months; FY2025 organic growth of 1.7% beat 1.2-1.4% and EPS of 164.2c beat 161.6c, but EPS fell 8.6%; FY2026 EPS of 165.3c fell short of brokers' August 2025 models of 168-174c after guidance and the dividend were cut in February 2026.
  • Five years: FY2022-23 strong (organic +21.4% and +6.5%); the 5-7% target for FY2023-25 then abandoned; consensus FY2027 EPS fell from a $1.96 peak to $1.68 and FY2028 from $2.09 to $1.77.
P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

D4842nd percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Weight the forecasts by line, not by year, because Diageo's record splits cleanly. The lines management controls (overheads, capex, cash, disposals, leverage, dividend) have been delivered or beaten in the last 18 months and deserve high weight, leaned slightly favourable. The lines the market controls (North America sales, price/mix, gross margin) have been missed repeatedly for three years and deserve low weight, leaned below the company. Concretely: the FY2027 guidance deserves high weight (about 75-80% likely to be met, profit at the low-to-middle of the range); the FY2027-29 algorithm deserves about 70% weight on its cash, cost and leverage legs (lean free cash flow above $8bn) and about 40-50% weight on its revenue leg (lean FY2029 organic growth to about 2.2% against 2.5%); consensus deserves about 65% weight for FY2027-28 EPS (lean FY2027 about 2% lower) and lower weight on FY2029 revenue (lean about 3% lower) and on FY2029 buybacks (lean higher than consensus's flat share count, lower than Jefferies' $2bn a year).

The record, by type of line.

Line Record Evidence (dated) Weight and lean
Cost programmes Over-delivered Accelerate raised from $500m to $625m (August 2025) and delivered $540m in FY2026 against $300m guided; supply savings $180m in FY2026; headcount -6.4% High; gross $1bn lean above; retention lean below (P-03)
Free cash flow Beaten FY2026 $3.21bn against a $3bn floor and $2.9bn consensus; capex cut from $1,549m to about $1.2bn High; FY2027 lean slightly above $2bn; FY2027-29 lean above $8bn
Disposals and leverage Delivered at good prices EABL at 17x EBITDA; RCB at 84x; leverage 3.4x to 3.1x High for value; medium for timing (RCB long-stop mid-December 2026)
Organic sales Missed 5-7% medium-term target abandoned (2025); FY2026 guidance cut (February 2026); profit warning (November 2023); missed in the FY2015 and FY2024 destocks; US share 17.2% (2014) to 15.1% (2025) Low beyond FY2027; lean below the company
EPS Mixed, missed on a three-year view FY2025 164.2c beat 161.6c; FY2026 165.3c below August 2025 broker models of 168-174c; FY2027 consensus fell from $1.96 to $1.68 Medium; lean FY2027 slightly below consensus
Quarterly sales (new management) Beaten once, partly on phasing Q1 FY2027 +1.4% against -0.4% consensus; North America -3.6% against -6.7% but helped by shipments; India +3.4% against +6.4% and EMEA +2.4% against +3.4% missed Medium; do not extrapolate

Why the record should be read this way.

  • New management changes the cost and cash record, not the market record. Lewis has replaced the operating model, the finance and regional leadership, the incentives (free cash flow, EPS and ROIC) and the capital policy within eleven months; the over-delivery on cost and cash is his team's record and is likely to persist. His Tesco record (operational repair delivered, share price range-bound) is the same split. Nothing in the new team's record yet shows the North America share loss turning.
  • The guidance is set as a floor, but only on the cost side. The FY2027 profit guidance (low-to-mid-single-digit organic growth, about $115-285m) needs roughly 45-90% of the year's gross savings to reach EBIT after about $70m of tariffs (first set, C-71); with my 40% retention plus some volume and mix, organic EBIT +3% is the middle-low of the range. The cash guidance ($2bn after about $850m of restructuring) looks conservative by about $0.1-0.2bn, in line with the FY2026 habit.
  • The downgrade cycle is late. Jefferies' history: Diageo's downgrade cycles lasted 9-24 months with peak-to-trough EPS cuts of 20-27%; this one began in November 2023 (36 months) with consensus FY2027 EPS -14% from its peak, and revisions have turned positive (FY2027 EPS +1% over one month, +2% over three, +4% over six; sales flat to -1%). The pattern is consistent with a cycle that has ended for profit and not yet for sales.
  • Consensus has already converged on guidance, so the leans are small for FY2027 and larger for FY2029, where consensus revenue growth of +3.9% (FactSet median, 16-Nov-26) sits above the company's own 2.5% organic target with no stated reason (possibly translation assumptions on a weaker dollar; the broker figures range from $19,162m to $20,521m).

Lean, numerically (my base against company and consensus).

  • FY2027: organic sales +0.5% (company broadly flat; Bloomberg consensus +0.5%); organic EBIT +3% (company low-to-mid-single-digit; consensus +2.5%); EPS $1.65 (consensus $1.68); free cash flow $2.2bn (company about $2bn; consensus $2.2bn).
  • FY2028: reported revenue $18.7bn (consensus $19.0bn); EBIT $5.82bn (consensus $5.95bn); EPS $1.75 (consensus $1.76).
  • FY2029: revenue $19.1bn (consensus $19.7bn); EBIT $6.02bn (consensus $6.28bn); EPS $1.86 (consensus $1.88); free cash flow FY2027-29 $8.8bn (company about $8bn; consensus about $8.9bn).

Confidence bands I would attach (one standard error, my judgement). FY2027 EPS ±3%; FY2028 ±6%; FY2029-31 ±10%. Free cash flow FY2027 ±$0.25bn (timing of restructuring cash and working capital); net debt FY2027 ±$0.8bn (mostly disposal timing).

Forecasts and upgrades

7

Forecasts with good growth rates and higher growth/margins than past

D4620th percentile

Memo topic 6, Forecast financials: the Rater's score

7a

Growth rate

4 answers
Judgement35C-4922P-0870P-1035
Group judgement

Financial forecasts

F3524th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Diageo's next four years are forecastable in an unusual way: the lines that management controls (overheads, capex, disposals, leverage, dividend, buybacks) are well specified, dated and backed by a recent record of over-delivery, and they carry almost all of the earnings growth; the lines the market controls (North America volume, price/mix and gross margin) carry almost all of the uncertainty and have been missed for three years. The base case is therefore a high-floor, low-ceiling forecast: EPS flat in FY2027 at about $1.65 and then compounding at about 6% a year to about $2.09 in FY2031; free cash flow rising from about $2.2bn to about $3.9bn; leverage falling from 3.1x to about 2.0x by FY2029 and buybacks returning at about 14-15x earnings; but organic revenue growth of only about 2% and an EBIT margin that stops rising once the savings are spent. Two numbers decide whether the case beats or misses: savings retention (each 10 points is about 3c of EPS) and North America organic growth (each point of group growth is about 3c). Consensus already sits close to this EPS path, so the differences that matter for the fourth set are in composition: consensus revenue for FY2029 looks about 3% too high against the company's own 2.5% target, consensus capital returns look too low against my cash flow, and the tax line may carry about 1% of EPS of hidden upside. For the third set: the 18-month cone is narrow on EPS (about -5% to +5%) because costs and finance are bounded, but wide on revenue and gross margin, and the first dated test is the H1 FY2027 result on 10-Feb-27 (overhead line, North America margin, cash).

C-49What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?F2217th percentilefull answer under 4c Financial profile
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

B+7088th percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. Below-EBIT items reverse from the main reason EPS fell 18% over FY2022-26 to the source of about half of the EPS growth over FY2026-29. Between FY2022 and FY2026, net finance charges rose by about $390m (about 13c of EPS) and buybacks stopped; between FY2026 and FY2029, net finance charges fall by about $270m (about +9c) and buybacks return (about +2.5c), while tax is flat and minorities fall with EABL. On my base, EPS rises from 165.3c to 186c by FY2029 (+21c): about +11c from EBIT (organic EBIT adds about +23c, but the EABL and other disposals take away about -8c and transactional currency about -4c), about +9c from lower net finance charges, about +2.5c from buybacks, and about -2c net from tax, minorities and associates. Net income before exceptional items grows more slowly than EPS (about $3.7bn to $4.1bn by FY2029). Free cash flow troughs at about $2.2bn in FY2027 on restructuring cash and then runs at about $3.2-3.9bn, well above the FY2024-26 average of $2.85bn, because interest, restructuring and capex intensity all fall while EBITDA rises.

Item by item (company, FactSet and broker data; my base where stated).

  • Net finance charges: $633m (FY2022), $924m, $1,084m, $1,031m, $1,021m (FY2026); my base $910m (FY2027), $797m, $749m, $738m, $744m (FY2031). Guided effective interest rate about 4.0% for FY2027 on a heavily fixed book; I add about 10bp a year for refinancing. Other finance charges (hyperinflation, pensions, lease interest) of about $140-170m are kept in the line.
  • Share of associates (mainly the 34% of Moët Hennessy): the weakest-visibility input. LVMH Wines and Spirits operating margin fell to 19.0% in 2025 from 23.1% and Hennessy shipments fell 24.2%; the stake contributed €177m of net profit in FY2025 (about $195m) and Bernstein says associates have historically been up to 11% of Diageo's net earnings. My base: about $250m in FY2027 rising to about $310m by FY2031 (modest Hennessy recovery; Deutsche Bank saw improving Q3 shipments, 12-Oct-26). Each ±10% on associate income is about ±1c of EPS. The put option at 80% of fair value is not in the base (Morgan Stanley: selling now would be "highly value destructive").
  • Tax: guided about 25% for FY2027 against 24.3% in FY2026, with a "revision to exclude associate income from the baseline calculation" (company accounts). I read that as 25% of pre-tax profit excluding associates' (already post-tax) income, which equals about 23.8% of pre-tax profit including associates. I use 24.0% of pre-tax profit including associates throughout. If brokers apply 25% to total pre-tax profit, they carry about $50m (about 2c, 1.3% of EPS) more tax than the company's guidance implies; this is a small but real source of consensus upside, and an ambiguity the Consumer should check against the H1 FY2027 accounts.
  • Minority interests: United Spirits (44% public), EABL (35%, until completion; the minorities' share was $94m a year, Bernstein), Shui Jing Fang (37%) and smaller holdings; consensus implies about $180m in FY2027. My base: about $170m (FY2027), $140m (FY2028), then rising about $10m a year with United Spirits' growth. The model form has no minorities line, so my EPS deducts them separately (Part 4).
  • Exceptional items and one-offs: $1.2bn restructuring ($752m charged in FY2026, the rest in FY2027), about $1.5bn of impairments in FY2026, and a long habit of classifying operating costs as exceptional (the $145m French distribution exit in FY2025; $75m for the Asian distribution exits in FY2026; Morgan Stanley's critique). Restructuring cash: about $850m (FY2027), about $150m (FY2028), about $50m (FY2029). I also carry about $100m a year of "other" cash outflows (share-scheme purchases, pensions, recurring exceptional cash) indefinitely, because the habit is likely to recur. On disposal, EABL may recycle cumulative currency translation losses through the income statement, as the Guinness Nigeria sale did (a $125m exceptional loss that included $175m of recycled translation losses, September 2024); this affects reported, not pre-exceptional, EPS.
  • Buybacks and issuance: $2.99bn (FY2022), $1.64bn, $0.97bn (FY2024) at roughly 20-25x earnings, none since; employee schemes add about 2m shares a year. My base: $0.5bn (FY2028), $1.0bn (FY2029), $2.0bn a year (FY2030-31), at about 14-15x earnings, removing about 53m shares by FY2029 and about 178m (8%) by FY2031. The same dollars buy almost twice the earnings they bought in FY2022-24.
  • Dividends: rebased to 50c (from 103.48c) with a 30-50% payout and a 50c floor; my base 51c, 54c, 58c, 62c, 66c (about 31-32% payout), against consensus 51.6c, 55.0c, 60.0c.

What drives the free cash flow step-up. From FY2026's $3.21bn: restructuring cash (-$850m in FY2027, then almost nil), lower cash interest (+about $200m by FY2029), EABL's lost cash flow (about -$300m a year from FY2028, Jefferies/Redburn), EBITDA growth (+about $400m by FY2029), cash tax rising with profit (-about $150m), and a working-capital build of about $100-150m a year (maturing stock grows with the aged-spirits business; the $100m safety stock reverses). The company definition excludes lease payments (about $170m a year, my estimate) and minority dividends; after both, FY2029 free cash flow is about $3.15bn.

Cone and skew. Below-EBIT items are the most predictable part of the EPS bridge (net finance ±$40m, tax ±1 point, minorities ±$20m in any FY2027-28 scenario), with two exceptions: associates (Hennessy) and the buyback start date. The skew is positive: a tariff refund after the February 2026 Supreme Court ruling (Campari has collected one; Diageo's files do not mention one), a Shui Jing Fang sale recycled into buybacks, and a lower tax charge than consensus assumes.

Where I differ from the first set (consolidated). (1) Interest falls materially rather than staying "roughly flat" (C-07), and deleveraging adds about 4.5% to EPS by FY2028 rather than about 2% (C-76), but the EABL dilution offsets about half of it. (3) The company's own cash and leverage targets leave little room for the $2bn of buybacks mooted for FY2028 (C-19); my base has $1.5bn over FY2028-29. (4) I agree with the first set's balanced case of EBIT margin +150-250bp by FY2029 (C-group judgement, Economic strength), landing at the top (+250bp) because about 50bp of it is the mechanical EABL exit.

7b

Margins/growth vs. past

6 answers
C-5378C-7857C-8020C-6742P-0433P-0933
C-53How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?A7891st percentilefull answer under 4c Financial profile
C-78

Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

F5718th percentile

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

The Investment Analyst's heading Beyond costs, how much scope is there to improve financial outputs, do peer precedents show it is achievable, and is it congruent with strategy?

Judgement: Meaningful scope exists, concentrated in a few big areas (US share and price ladder, Guinness capacity, India premium, service levels, cash and working capital), and it is congruent with the strategy. Peer precedents show cost programmes are reliably delivered but mostly reinvested; Diageo's algorithm implies keeping a larger share of its savings than Heineken did, which is the main credibility gap.

Reasoning and evidence.

  • Big areas: (1) North America: holding share from FY2028 instead of losing about 1-2 points of growth a year would add about 1 point to group growth (our arithmetic); (2) Guinness: over 50% more capacity by FY2029 supports high-single-digit growth on about 10% of sales; (3) India: 4-6% guided against 10% compound growth expected by Jefferies; (4) service from 84% to 98% on-time-in-full; (5) ready-to-drink fair share (+3% to US sales per Deutsche Bank, at lower margins); (6) working capital and maturing-stock release; (7) disposals (Shui Jing Fang).
  • Precedents: Pernod's €900m programme (FY2023-25) expanded organic margin 64bps to 26.9% while sales fell; Heineken delivered over €3.5bn of gross savings since 2020 but its margin rose only 41bps in FY2025 because savings funded brands; Campari targets 200bps of overhead benefit and reinvests it; Rémy's €100m is back-loaded; Lewis rebuilt Tesco's margin but not its share price during his tenure.
  • Retention test: $1bn of gross savings is about 5 points of sales; mid-single-digit profit growth on low-single-digit sales implies 80-100bps of margin expansion a year (Redburn), about 2.5-3 points over three years, so Diageo must keep roughly half of its savings (our arithmetic) against peers that kept much less.
C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

F206th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading Has the core growth engine decelerated, and is the input change force itself slowing?

Judgement: Yes, decisively. Organic growth has run well below Diageo's normal rate for three years, and the input force that powered the last decade (US premiumisation led by tequila) has not just slowed but reversed for Diageo.

Reasoning and evidence. Organic net sales: +21.4% (FY2022), +6.5% (FY2023), -0.6% (FY2024), +1.7% (FY2025), -2.0% (FY2026), guided about flat (FY2027), against about 4% a year from 1980 to 2019 and a 9% compound rate over FY2019-23. US tequila category growth fell from +10% (2023) to +3%; Diageo's US tequila -21% in FY2026, North America Tequila -23.1% in H1 FY2026 (Casamigos -30.9%, Don Julio -20.9%); Don Julio went from +42% growth to -12%; North America organic -8.5% (FY2026); price/mix turned from the engine (+2-3% a year) to a drag (-1.6%). The replacement engines (Guinness, India premium, ready-to-drink) are real but smaller.

C-67

How do the sell-side's forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five years, for the main financial statement line items in particular and the granular working up to them, and how credible is the gap given the change forces and foundations found above?

D4234th percentile

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

The Investment Analyst's heading How do sell-side forecasts for the next one to three years compare with the last one to five, and how credible is the gap?

Judgement: Consensus forecasts essentially no revenue growth over three years (reported revenue $19.6bn in FY2026 to $19.7bn in FY2029, about 1.5-2% a year organic once disposals and currency are stripped out), but a return to record operating margins (EBIT 31.8% in FY2029 against a 31.2% peak in FY2023 and 29.0% now) and EBITDA margins (35.7% against 32.8%), all from below the gross line. EPS rises about 4.5% a year (FY2026 $1.65 to FY2029 $1.88, brokers' basis) yet stays below the FY2022-23 level because interest costs have risen by about $400m. The revenue path is credible; the margin path is plausible but demanding; FY2029 revenue growth of +3.9% is above the company's own 2.5% target.

Reasoning and evidence (our arithmetic on FactSet).

  • Revenue: FY2020-26 history $14.8bn to $19.6bn (+4.8% a year including the pandemic rebound); FY2022-26 -1.1% a year; consensus FY2027-29 -2.7%, -0.6%, +3.9%.
  • Gross margin: consensus about 59.6% in FY2027-29 against 59.8% in FY2026 (only three brokers): a modest give-back despite North America price investment, helped by the EABL exit and agave.
  • EBIT margin: history 29.6% (FY2020-21), 31.1%, 31.2%, 29.4%, 28.3%, 29.0%; consensus 30.0%, 31.3%, 31.8%, 32.3% (FY2030, three brokers), about 280bps over three years, consistent with $1bn of savings of which roughly half is retained, plus about 30-40bps from the EABL exit.
  • Below the line: consensus pre-tax implies net finance costs plus associates of about -$540m in FY2027 and about -$430m in FY2028; tax about 25%; minorities about $180m; share count almost flat (buybacks barely modelled).
  • Cash: free cash flow $2.2bn, $3.2bn, $3.5bn; dividend 31-32% of EPS; gross debt falling to $16.4bn by FY2029.
  • Credibility against the forces and foundations: the cost leg is credible (C-14, C-77); the gross-margin assumption is exposed to price investment and ready-to-drink mix (C-17, C-03); FY2029 revenue growth needs US share gains (C-26, C-34). The base-case risk is that savings are reinvested more than consensus assumes (the Heineken precedent), which would leave EBIT margin nearer 30.5-31% in FY2029 (our judgement).

P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F3323rd percentile

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement. The net effect is a business that, over FY2026-31, earns its FY2022 EPS back (209c against 201.9c) on a smaller perimeter, a lower EBIT (FY2031 $6.4bn, back to FY2023's level only at the end) and a cheaper capital base, by swapping the drivers of the last decade: price/mix and volume growth are replaced by overhead removal, deleveraging and low-multiple buybacks. Over the next 18 months the net impact is almost nil at the EPS line (flat FY2027) because the gains and the losses cancel: retained savings (about $150m in FY2027 and $210m in FY2028, inside organic EBIT growth of about $170m and $250m) and lower net finance charges (about +$110m in FY2027, +$225m by FY2028 against FY2026) are absorbed by the loss of EABL's profit (about $105m of EBIT in FY2027 and $210m a year from FY2028, net of interest saved about 3% of EPS), other disposals, and transactional currency (about -$75m of EBIT over FY2027-28). Over FY2028-31, EPS compounds at about 6% a year, free cash flow at about 7% a year from FY2028, and the dividend at about 7%, while reported revenue grows only about 2% a year from FY2029. Earnings quality improves in cash terms (conversion from 59% to about 90%) but weakens in growth terms (organic EBIT growth of about 3-4.5% a year is below the company's mid-single-digit algorithm).

The three insights the Consumer should carry into the third set.

  1. The forecast is concentrated in two drivers, both inside the company's control, and one driver outside it. Overhead savings (retention) and deleveraging (disposal completion plus free cash flow) together explain about 80% of the difference between FY2026 and FY2029 EPS; North America organic growth explains most of the remaining uncertainty and almost all of the difference between my revenue and consensus.
  2. The disposals are EPS-neutral to dilutive in the near term and only become accretive through buybacks. EABL costs about 3% of EPS net of interest saved; the proceeds pay down 4% debt. The accretion comes only when cash is recycled into shares at about 14x earnings (an earnings yield of about 7%), which the leverage targets delay to FY2029-30.
  3. The gap to consensus is in revenue and capital returns, not in EPS. My EPS is within about 2% of consensus every year, but my revenue is about 1.5% lower in FY2028 and 3% lower in FY2029, my EBIT about 2-4% lower, and my net debt lower with more buybacks. A consensus that has the right EPS for the wrong reasons is vulnerable to the composition of results: a quarter in which North America misses while costs and cash beat would leave EPS intact but would likely be read negatively.

Memo topic 7, Scope for upgrades over 6 to 12 months: the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

C-4457th percentile

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Our assessment is, to a first approximation, already what guidance, estimates and the narrative reflect. Guidance for FY2027 is our base case and is most likely to be met and repeated, with cash the only element likely to beat (I-01). Consensus EPS is our EPS for FY2027-28, and we are below consensus on revenue and operating profit further out, with the difference made up below operating profit in ways that are partly uncertain (I-02). The narrative contains every main dynamic on both sides of the argument (I-03). The gap between what we think and what is expected is therefore small, and what there is of it is two-sided and, in composition, slightly unfavourable.

On the favourable side, none of it large: the cost and cash legs are conservative and likely to beat; published estimates contain no buyback; Moët Hennessy is modelled cautiously while LVMH's figures improve; a tariff refund or a sale of Shui Jing Fang is possible; and the downgrade cycle has ended, with about a 60% chance that no guidance is reduced over the next six events. On the unfavourable side: mechanical cuts to consensus revenue and EBITDA when East African Breweries leaves; outer-year margins and depreciation; the chance (about 35%) of cautious FY2028 guidance from a new finance director; a first buyback smaller than the constructive narrative expects; and trade measures the narrative has barely noticed.

Three steers for the memo. First, the case cannot rest on upgrades to guidance or estimates over the next 6 to 12 months; on our forecasts there are none of substance, and the Consumer should say so plainly under the topics on scope for and momentum of upgrades. What the case has instead is a low probability of downgrades after three years of them, which has value but is a different claim. Second, with earnings in line, the return over 18 months is almost entirely a question of the multiple, and the multiple is mostly a spirits-industry variable (D-07). The narrative's range for it runs from about 9 times to above 20. On our assessment, delivery of the financial steps supports a move towards the stock's own five-year median of 16.75 times; applied to two-year-forward EPS on the brokers' basis at the end of 2027 (about $1.90), that is about 2,420p, a return of about 25-27% with dividends from 1,934p, against the 71% the model reports at 21.6 times on its own EPS. A multiple above the high teens needs evidence that United States share has stopped falling, which the company itself does not expect before FY2028 and which I rate at no better than 40%. Third, the dates that matter are 10-Feb-27, for whether savings reach profit without a further cut in advertising, and August 2027, for FY2028 guidance and the size of the first buyback; the monthly United States data in between will move the shares more than they will change the forecasts.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

F4225th percentile

Layer 4 · Guidance

The Investment Analyst's heading How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events, and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, the same for the targets 18 months to three years out.

Judgement. The most likely outcome at each of the six events in the next 18 months is that guidance is met and repeated: I put the probability that no element of annual guidance is reduced at any of them at about 60%, the probability that profit guidance for either year is raised at about 15-20%, and the probability that some element is raised (most likely cash or the savings figure) at about 35%. Guidance is a floor on the cost and cash side and has no cushion on the profit side, so "met, not raised" is the base. The event with the widest range is the full-year result in August 2027, because three things are set at once: guidance for FY2028, the first decision on a buyback, and the first statement by a new finance director. For the three-year framework, the cash, savings and EPS legs are likely to be met or beaten; the revenue leg (2.5% organic growth in FY2029, United States share held in FY2028 and gained in FY2029) is the least likely to be met but is unlikely to be withdrawn inside 18 months; and the leverage target and the buyback narrative cannot both be satisfied on the company's own numbers.

The events.

August 2027 in detail.

  • Delivery of FY2027 against guidance: organic sales within "broadly flat" (my range -0.5% to +1%): about 80%. Organic operating profit within the range, in its lower-middle part (my +2.5% to +3.5%): about 80%; below the range about 15%; above it under 5%. Free cash flow at or above $2.0bn about 85%, at or above $2.2bn about 55%. Leverage at or below about 2.8 times about 80%, which is mostly the probability that both disposals have completed.
  • FY2028 guidance: in line with the framework (low-single-digit sales growth, mid-single-digit profit growth) about 55%; more cautious than the framework (for example a repeat of "low-to-mid single digit" for profit) about 35%; above it about 10%. I weight the cautious outcome heavily for a specific reason: the finance director who built the plan leaves in 2027, and an incoming finance director has every reason to set her first year's guidance low. Morgan Stanley points out that her previous company issued several profit warnings during her tenure, which argues for caution on her side as much as for risk on ours.
  • Buyback: some programme announced in August 2027 about 55%, most likely $0.5-1.0bn; deferred to February 2028 or later about 45%.

Why "met" and not "raised" for profit.

  • The guided range of about $115-285m of organic profit growth, plus about $70m of net tariffs, needs most of the year's roughly $380m of gross savings before price investment and inflation; my 40% retention reaches the lower middle of the range. There is no hidden cushion.
  • The year is weighted to the second half (Deutsche Bank reports the company as expecting "a pronounced back-half weighting" of margin), so the half-year result cannot by itself justify a raise.
  • The chief executive's observed habit is to set a floor and hold it: sales beat consensus in the first quarter (+1.4% against -0.4%) and guidance was repeated.

Why cash is the leg most likely to beat. FY2026 free cash flow was $3.21bn against a floor of $3bn. For FY2027 the company guides to about $2bn and I have $2.2bn; for FY2027-29 it guides to about $8bn and I have about $8.8bn. The sources of the difference are identifiable: the company targets inventory days falling from 110 to 90 in FY2027 and below 85 in FY2028; the stock built for the systems change (about $100-125m) reverses; investment in maturing stock falls to about $235m a year (Morgan Stanley's reading of the plan) from about $600m; and my interest bill is lower. The risks to it are an overrun of restructuring cash and reliance on supplier finance ($1,092m of payables).

The three-year framework (secondary).

The leverage target and the buyback cannot both be met in full. The second set's arithmetic holds on my figures: starting from about 2.75 times at FY2027, $8bn of cash over three years less dividends and payments to minorities reaches about 2.0 times at FY2029 with no buyback; each $1bn of buybacks adds about 0.15 times. A buyback of $2bn in total would leave leverage near 2.35 times at FY2029; $2bn a year from FY2028, which Jefferies describes as plausible, would leave it nearer 2.65 times. Unless cash beats by $1bn or more, or Shui Jing Fang is sold (Bernstein: $1.8-2.3bn, about 0.3 times of leverage), I expect the company to keep the target and size the buyback to fit, as the peers did: AB InBev restarted at $1bn and stepped up over two years; Carlsberg reached its target and still deferred the decision.

Against our assessment. Guidance is our base. On FY2027 profit there is no gap; on cash a small favourable one; on the FY2028-29 revenue and profit framework our figures are slightly below the company's. For an investment case this is a floor, not a springboard.

I-02

How likely are sell-side consensus forecasts, and the net breadth of revisions, to move higher or lower over the next few earnings events (over the next 18 months): to be upgraded, cut or held, and why? Secondarily, how likely are the consensus forecasts for 18 months to three years out to be upgraded, cut or held?

C+4667th percentile

Layer 4 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement. Consensus EPS for FY2027 and FY2028 is most likely to be held, within about 1.5% either way, through the next three events; our EPS is on it. The upward revisions of the last six months (FY2027 EPS +4%, target prices +9%, the share of buy ratings up 6 points to 46%) came from the savings programme and currency and are now in the numbers; I expect that momentum to fade to flat rather than continue. Net breadth will look worse than it is: revisions to sales and EBITDA will be negative around the half-year results for a mechanical reason, because several brokers have not yet taken East African Breweries out of their published forecasts. Further out, consensus revenue and EBIT for FY2029-30 are more likely to be cut than raised (about 65%), by about 2% and 3-4%, with EPS cut by less because lower finance charges and buybacks offset. Nothing here amounts to an upgrade cycle; what it amounts to is a downgrade cycle that has ended.

Where consensus sits against my base.

FY2027 FY2028 FY2029
Revenue: consensus / mine 19,109 / 19,034 (-0.4%) 18,988 / 18,711 (-1.5%) 19,720 / 19,122 (-3.0%)
EBIT: consensus / mine 5,730 / 5,717 (-0.2%) 5,947 / 5,820 (-2.1%) 6,276 / 6,007 (-4.3%)
Pre-tax profit: consensus / mine 5,190 / 5,157 (-0.6%) 5,514 / 5,385 (-2.3%) 5,849 / 5,617 (-4.0%)
EPS: consensus / mine 1.68 / 1.67 (-0.6%) 1.76 / 1.75 (-0.6%) 1.88 / 1.85 (-1.6%)
Free cash flow: consensus / mine 2,222 / 2,180 3,187 / 3,160 3,495 / 3,450

With finance charges corrected, my pre-tax profit for FY2027 matches consensus, which it did not on the second set's inputs (-2.6%); that is further evidence for the correction. In FY2028-29 my EPS stays close to consensus although my pre-tax profit is 2-4% lower, for two reasons, and the Consumer should know that both are uncertain. First, consensus deducts about 28.5% of pre-tax profit for tax and minorities together and I deduct about 27.8%; I cannot tell from the medians whether the difference is the tax basis (the second set's suggestion that some brokers apply 25% to profit including associates) or higher minorities. It is worth about 1% of EPS. Second, consensus has an almost flat share count and I have $1.5bn of buybacks by FY2029, worth about 1%.

Three features of consensus that the earlier sets did not bring out.

  1. Published consensus mixes two bases. For FY2028, individual brokers' revenue runs from $18,767m to $20,001m. Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank, Goldman Sachs and RBC ($19.7-20.0bn) evidently still include East African Breweries; Jefferies, UBS, Goodbody and Oddo ($18.8-18.9bn) do not. Morgan Stanley's own pro forma for FY2027 is net sales of $18,603m against $19,688m as published and operating profit of $5,444m against $5,654m. When the sale completes, those brokers will cut revenue by about 5%, operating profit by about 3.5% and EPS by 1-3%. The revision counts for sales and EBITDA will turn negative for that reason alone, and the EPS median will slip by perhaps 1%, to about where my figure is. This is not information about trading.
  2. Consensus revenue growth of +3.9% in FY2029 is an artefact. It compares medians drawn from different sets of brokers. Broker by broker, growth in FY2029 is +1.4% to +3.8%, with a median of about +2.5%, which is the company's target. I therefore disagree with the second set's reading that FY2029 consensus revenue is about 3% too high against the company's own target. About half of our 3% shortfall is brokers that still include East African Breweries; the like-for-like gap is about 1.5-2%, from my lower FY2028 base and 2.2% growth against 2.5%.
  3. The dispersion of EPS is tight and the rating base is cautious. FY2027 EPS ranges from $1.60 to $1.72 on FactSet, and fewer than half of brokers rate the shares a buy on that count. Tight dispersion means large swings in breadth are unlikely in either direction; a cautious rating base means rating upgrades are a more likely source of support than estimate upgrades.

Upside and TSR

11d

Current multiple

B7980th percentileMemo topic 11, The fair multiple: the Rater's score1 answer
D-0747
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

D4747th percentile

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement. On a rolling six-month basis about half of the daily variance in Diageo's share price, and about three-quarters of its return over the last six months, is common to its beverage peers; the common part is mainly a spirits factor, not a general beverages one. The multiple is, on my judgement, about two-thirds industry-driven: every alcohol company in the dashboard sits in the bottom tenth of its own ten-year range, and most of Diageo's recovery from its low has been a rise in the multiple that its peers shared. Two features make this more than a statistic. Co-movement is at the top of its two-year range now, in the very months when the company-specific story (new chief executive, the Capital Markets Day, the first-quarter beat) was at its most active: the market is paying for the group, not yet for the plan. And over the last two years the stock has tended to move on its own when its own news was bad and with the group when prices rose. Both mean that the re-rating on which most of the model's return depends is not in the company's gift.

The evidence (co-movement table, to 24-Nov-26; equal-weighted basket of ten dashboard peers).

  • Latest six months: correlation with the basket 0.73, beta 1.09, R-squared 54%, so 46% of daily variance is the stock's own. Diageo rose 24.4% against the basket's 17.3%; beta times the basket's move is about 19 points, leaving about 5.5 points (under a quarter of the gain) as Diageo's own.
  • Over 24 months: R-squared has ranged from 36% to 57% and averaged 46%. The two latest readings (57% and 54%) are the highest in the series. The lowest readings (36-41%, for the windows ending October 2025 to January 2026) cover the months of the previous chief executive's departure and replacement; the highest betas (1.34-1.38, February and March 2026) follow the February 2026 cut to guidance and halving of the dividend.
  • By peer: Pernod Ricard 0.83 (R-squared 70%); Campari 0.67 and Rémy Cointreau 0.66; Heineken 0.62 and AB InBev 0.57; the soft-drink companies 0.18 to 0.44. Diageo trades as one half of a pair with Pernod Ricard, then with spirits, then with brewers, and hardly at all with soft drinks.
  • The multiple: Diageo's price-to-earnings ratio is at the 2nd percentile of its ten-year history; Pernod Ricard is at the 1st, Campari the 6th, Carlsberg the 9th, Heineken the 11th; Coca-Cola Europacific Partners, a soft-drink bottler, is at the 99th. The low multiple is shared across the alcohol industry and is not particular to Diageo.
  • Price against earnings: over six months consensus FY2027 EPS rose about 4% and the share price 16-24%; the forward multiple has risen from about 12 to about 14 times. The first set's sector work found the same for the group: the 2026 recovery of European beverage shares was a rise in multiples and a rotation into defensive shares rather than a rise in earnings.
  • Where Diageo has differed: over twelve months it has returned +6.4% against -6.0% for Pernod Ricard, but +20% to +36% for Campari, Heineken, Carlsberg, AB InBev and Rémy Cointreau. Its own news has kept it ahead of its closest twin and behind the rest.

What follows for predictability and for the memo.

  • A forecast of EPS, however accurate, explains the smaller part of what the shares do over six months. The larger part is the industry's multiple, which turns on United States spirits data, interest rates and sector rotation. Deutsche Bank's outlook for 2027 (25-Nov-26) describes the sector's rally as "more froth than fizz", and the house's own digest warns that the leveraged defensive de-rates first; a reversal would take Diageo with it whatever it delivers.
  • Of the 71% return the model reports to its 18-month column, 54 points come from the multiple rising from 14.1 to 21.6 times. On the evidence here, the company-driven part of a re-rating is the minority of it. Delivery of the financial steps can plausibly close the gap to the stock's own five-year median (16.75 times); anything beyond needs the whole spirits group to re-rate, which needs United States spirits volumes to stop falling.
  • The favourable reading is that company-specific credit for the plan has not yet been given, so it remains to be earned: the idiosyncratic share of the last six months' gain was small. The unfavourable reading is that the gain so far can be withdrawn for reasons that have nothing to do with Diageo.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C-5052nd percentile

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What do precedents and evidence show about how companies in this sector have out- or underperformed, and does Diageo fit?

Judgement: The precedents point to four routes to outperformance and three to underperformance. Diageo fits two of the positive routes (a destock ending first, a large cost programme at a trough multiple) and one of the negative ones (premiumisation overshoot and channel loading), while missing the most reliable positive route (share gains from disciplined brand investment). That makes it a "stabilisation and re-rating from trough" case, not a "compounding share-gainer" case.

Routes to outperformance.

  • A destock that ends lifts reported sales without consumption growing; restocking is a single step; own each spirits house only until shipments reach consumption (sector digest). Diageo is the first to reach it.
  • Cost programmes and buybacks at a multiple near its low: large drinks companies compounding EPS about 12% a year at the 4th percentile of their ranges pay without a re-rating (sector digest); Pernod's Transform & Accelerate after Elliott (2018) closed part of a 500bp margin gap to Diageo and took the multiple to 21.4x.
  • Share gains by focused brand houses with pricing discipline and reinvestment: Campari (8.4% organic CAGR 2019-25; share price -8% in 2025 against Diageo and Rémy -37%).
  • New-chief-executive resets that reinvest savings: Carlsberg under Cees 't Hart (from Unilever, SAIL'22) overhauled culture and reinvested efficiencies; the market rewarded it over years.

Routes to underperformance.

  • Premiumisation overshoot and channel loading: Diageo's Latin America (50% sales growth 2019-22, 120 days of stock, a 23% fall and 1,000bp regional margin collapse in H1 FY2024); Rémy's US VSOP prices raised over 30% then volume -28%.
  • Aged-inventory gluts and leverage into a downturn: Rémy (ROIC 4%, 3.2x leverage); Pernod (3.8x, buybacks suspended).
  • Price-led recoveries: "in spirits, price is not the lever" (sector digest); Deutsche Bank: price cuts failed to lift volume for 72% of key brands.

Diageo's own cycle: Jefferies finds Diageo's downgrade cycles lasted 9-24 months with peak-to-trough EPS cuts of 20-27% before inflecting. This cycle began with the November 2023 Latin America warning (about 36 months ago); pre-exceptional EPS fell from 201.9c (FY2022) to 164-165c (FY2025-26), about -18%; FY2027 consensus EPS fell from a peak of $1.96 to $1.68 (-14%). The magnitude matches history and the duration exceeds it, consistent with a late-stage cycle (and with the Wisdom Library's "worser, odder, longer"). Margin-led EPS growth "typically commands a lower multiple" (Redburn) and "slow growth is punished twice".

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

C+5568th percentile

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: About 20-30% in a realistic bad case, with the Morgan Stanley bear case (about 40%) as the extreme. Downside is cushioned because the multiple is already near the bottom of its own history and the stock tested a trough at about 11x forward earnings within the last year.

Reasoning and evidence (our arithmetic on the dashboard and financials-block data).

  • Where it is: 1,880p; 24-month-forward P/E 14.0x (23rd percentile of five years; five-year range 10.9x to 25.5x, median 16.75x); current P/E 13.8x at the 2nd percentile of its ten-year history; the house's ten-year bands put 15.6x at the 10th percentile ("crash"), so the stock already trades below its ten-year crash band; 52-week low 1,295p (31% below today); peers Pernod 11.9x (1st percentile) and Heineken 13.5x.
  • Trough scenarios: (1) 11x on unchanged forward EPS (about 138p) gives about 1,515p (-19%); (2) 11x on forward EPS 10% lower (US price war, savings reinvested) gives about 1,365p (-27%); (3) Morgan Stanley's bear cases, 1,190p (10.7x calendar 2026 earnings, a 30% discount to European staples) and 1,120p (prolonged destocking and recession), are -37% and -40%.
  • Support: the 34% Moët Hennessy stake (associates about $5.3bn, about 9% of the market value); aged Scotch inventory; a 50c dividend floor (about 2% yield); disposal values well above the group multiple (EABL at 17x EBITDA against the group's about 10.8x enterprise value to FY2027 EBITDA).

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

B+5584th percentile

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The narrative can move from "broken premiumisation and a stretched balance sheet" to "a cost-led stabilisation with returning cash" over the next eighteen months on events that are largely in management's control and well dated (H1 results in February 2027, disposals closing, leverage at about 2.75x, buybacks from FY2028). The move to "a share-gaining compounder", which is what a multiple above the ten-year median would need, depends on US evidence that is not due until FY2028-29 and is the least well founded step. With the shares 45% above their 52-week low and the valuation still at the 2nd percentile of ten years, the narrative staircase supports a re-rating toward the mid-teens on delivery of the financial steps alone; anything beyond that requires the commercial steps.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–68

Layer 3 · Risks

The Investment Analyst's heading How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets?

Judgement. The two sets are highly congruent on the thesis and only moderately congruent in the arithmetic beneath it. They agree, question after question, on one organising idea: the lines management controls (overheads, capital spending, disposals, debt, the dividend) are specified, dated and recently over-delivered, and they carry the earnings; the lines the market controls (North American volume, price/mix and gross margin) carry the uncertainty and have been missed for three years. I found nothing in either set that contradicts that idea, and the first set's scores draw the same picture (external forces 36, internal forces 62, management 57, risks 36). Where the sets jar, they jar in detail, and the errors I found mostly offset at the EPS line. That is reassuring about the EPS forecast and a warning about its composition: the EPS is robust because it is over-determined by a few large, bounded items, not because every line is right.

Where the answers reinforce each other.

  • Cost and cash: the first set's evidence of cost delivery (Accelerate: $540m against $300m guided) and of a clearly cuttable overhead base feeds the second set's central assumption (gross savings of $1bn delivered, about 40% retained), and the retention assumption sits where the first set's precedent work pointed (Heineken about 25%, the company's algorithm about half).
  • No market recovery in the base: the first set's instruction (no North American recovery before FY2028, no real price increases, gross margin flat at best) is carried into the second set's lines (North America -3.0% and -1.2%, price/mix -0.3%, gross margin down 60 basis points).
  • The plan's own logic: the company says savings will be invested "back into brand competitiveness without reducing operating profits" while holding advertising and promotion at about 16% of sales and growing profit at a mid-single-digit rate. Those three aims are jointly possible only if the reinvestment is made in price and trade terms, above the net sales line, rather than in advertising. Both sets model it that way (negative price/mix and a lower gross margin, an unchanged advertising ratio), so the model is congruent with what management is actually doing.
  • Quality of earnings: the first set's "false friend" warning (margins rising while the franchise weakens) and the second set's conclusion (EPS growth with organic growth of about 2%) are the same judgement seen from two sides.

Where they jar, and what follows.

  1. The below-operating-profit bridge does not reproduce the base year. Finance charges were taken from a FactSet line that overstates the company's charge by about $170m, and minorities were set about $35-80m too low (points 1 and 2 above). Consequences beyond the input change: the first set's "interest is about 18% of EBIT, cover 5.6 times" (C-76) should read about 15% and about 6.7 times; and the EPS gain from deleveraging to FY2029 is about 6 cents, not the 9 cents the second set gives (P-02, P-08), because the starting charge is lower.
  2. The dilution from selling East African Breweries. The second set says the sale costs about 3% of EPS net of interest saved, on an attributable profit of about $175m. Diageo's announcement (December 2025) gives East African Breweries' net income as $94m in the year to June 2025; Diageo's 65% share is about $60m, and Morgan Stanley puts the dilution at about 2% before interest savings and Bernstein at about 1% after them. Against about $70m of after-tax interest saved on $2.3bn, the sale is roughly neutral to 1% dilutive. I disagree with the second set's text; its lines, however, already behave like the brokers' figures, so no further input changes. The second set's larger point stands: disposals fund deleveraging, they do not add earnings until the cash buys back shares.
  3. North America in FY2027. The second set has -3.0% for the year. Its own first half implies about -5% (first quarter -3.6%; a second quarter near -7%, which is also Morgan Stanley's reading), so its second half must be about -0.5%. The first-quarter statement, as AlphaSense reports it, guides North America to a mid-single-digit decline for the year, and Jefferies models the same. I would put North America at about -4.5%, with Asia Pacific, Latin America and Europe running slightly above the second set (their first-quarter growth was 4.1%, 4.8% and 2.4% against assumptions of 2.0%, 3.5% and 2.0%). Group organic growth of +0.3% to +0.5% still holds, so I do not change the revenue input; the mix costs about $20-40m of FY2027 EBIT, which is inside the rounding of the retention assumption.
  4. The second-half comparison. Both sets rest the "better second half" on the easy North American comparison of the third quarter of FY2026 (-9.4%). Morgan Stanley (10-Nov-26) says the second half of FY2026 also contained pre-loading ahead of the football World Cup and a $35m Canadian settlement, a hard comparison. One of the two second-half quarters is easy and one is hard; the second half is less safe than the earlier sets assumed.
  5. Gross margin guidance. The first set says the company gives none (C-72); one AlphaSense report says the company guided to stable gross margins at the Capital Markets Day and that Deutsche Bank's and consensus forecasts assume flat gross margins. If so, our gross margin (down 60 basis points) is below the company's own indication, with the offset in overheads; the half-year results will show which is right.
  6. The fair multiple. The first set judged that reinvestment opportunities support "a fair multiple in the mid-teens" (C-50) and that delivery of the financial steps alone supports "a re-rating toward the mid-teens" (narrative group). The model summary applies a guideline fair multiple of 21.6 times at the 18-month column, between the 75th percentile (21.0 times) and the 90th (23.0 times) of the stock's ten-year range, and draws 54 points of its 71% return from it. The multiple is the memo Analyst's topic; I record only that it is the least congruent element between the foundations the earlier sets describe and the return the model reports, and that my answers to D-07 and I-03 bear on it.

Net. Congruent thesis; arithmetic errors that I have corrected and that net to about +2 cents in FY2027 and nothing thereafter; one unresolved tension (the multiple) outside the forecasts themselves.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

D4249th percentile

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

The Investment Analyst's heading How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of sell-side commentary and of investor commentary, and how well known is each?

Judgement. The sell-side narrative is comprehensive and unusually explicit, because it is a two-sided argument conducted in public: Jefferies and Bernstein on the constructive side, Morgan Stanley and Deutsche Bank on the cautious side, Redburn between them. Every dynamic that drives our forecasts for the next 18 months is articulated by at least one of them, and the central ones by all. The same is true of the main risk beyond 18 months, that margins stop rising once the savings are spent, which is Morgan Stanley's core argument. What we see that the narrative does not is limited to three things, none large: the arithmetic that sets the company's own cash and leverage targets against the buyback that the constructive brokers describe; the mixed basis of published consensus; and trade measures on Canadian whisky, which appear in the files only in a law-firm note and channel checks. On investor commentary I have no direct evidence: the AlphaSense work found none distinct from the sell-side, so what follows on investors is inferred from positioning and price.

The dynamics, who articulates them, and where we differ.

The sector's multiple Deutsche Bank ("more froth than fizz"), Bernstein Known at sector level See D-07

Where the narrative's centre is, and where we sit. The constructive case is a self-help recovery that needs no cyclical help, with a re-rating to above 20 times earnings on execution and an upside scenario of 3,000p (Jefferies), and EPS growth of about 10% in FY2027 (Bernstein). The cautious case is that growth below the consumer-staples average deserves a discount, that margins fall once the savings are spent, and that 14 times "appropriately reflects a lower growth outlook" (Deutsche Bank), with bear cases of 1,000-1,120p. Our assessment (a high floor and a low ceiling; EPS on consensus; revenue and quality below) is closest to the neutral camp. At 1,880p the shares stand above three of the five targets the first set cites (1,570p, 1,740p, 1,800p) and below the other two (2,200p, 2,470p); the FactSet mean target is about 9% above the price.

Investors. The indirect evidence is: a share price 45% above its 52-week low and within 4% of its high; relative strength at the 64th percentile; short interest of 0.41% of the free float; a mean broker rating of Overweight; and a national newspaper headline on the first-quarter statement, "Investors toast Drastic Dave's Diageo recovery" (6-Nov-26). I read these as showing that the floor (cost, cash, disposals, a credible chief executive) is known and owned by generalist investors, and D-07 shows that most of what they have paid for is the recovery of the spirits group as a whole. I cannot say how far investors on social media or elsewhere have absorbed the narrower points in the table; my third research request addresses positioning.

By horizon. For the next 18 months the narrative is complete: I can find no driver of our FY2027-28 forecasts that is not already argued in print. For 18 months to three years it is nearly as complete on the risks (the margin plateau, the credibility of 2.5% growth) and looser on the upside, where "buybacks from FY2028" stands in for analysis of how much cash there will be.
