Each layer's own material

What each layer was asked, what its scores mean, and the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 23 November 2026, 14:13 to 14:34 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Diageo (DGE-GB): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-11-23 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Before the answers: source check, anchor facts and data conflicts

Source check. The latest Earnings Review covers the full year to 30 June 2026 (FY2026, reported 6 August 2026, with the Capital Markets Day the same day) and the first-quarter FY2027 trading statement of 5 November 2026. That matches the last period FactSet shows as reported (06/2026), so the review is current and is relied on throughout. Diageo reports in US dollars; the share price is in pence (1,880p on 20 November 2026).

Anchor facts for the model (company or FactSet unless stated).

  • FY2026: net sales $19,643m (reported -3.0%, organic -2.0%: volume -0.4%, price/mix -1.6%); organic operating profit +2%; organic operating margin 29.4% (+116bps); FactSet adjusted EBIT $5,693m (29.0%); reported operating profit $3,156m after about $0.75bn of restructuring charges and about $1.5bn of impairments; pre-exceptional EPS 165.3c (+0.7%); free cash flow (company definition) $3.21bn; net borrowings $20.48bn; net debt to adjusted EBITDA 3.1x; ROIC 13.4%; dividend rebased to 50c (30-50% payout policy, 50c floor).
  • Q1 FY2027: net sales $4,931m; organic +1.4% (excluding East African Breweries, EABL, +0.6%: volume -0.1%, price/mix +0.7%) against consensus -0.4%. North America -3.6% (consensus -6.7%; helped by shipment phasing), EMEA +2.4%, Asia Pacific +4.1%, Latin America +4.8%, India +3.4%.
  • FY2027 guidance: broadly flat organic net sales; low-to-mid-single-digit organic operating profit growth; free cash flow about $2bn after about $850m of restructuring cash; capex about $1.25bn a year; effective tax rate about 25%; effective interest rate about 4%; US tariffs $100m gross, about $70m after mitigation; currency -$100m on net sales and -$25m on operating profit; leverage to the midpoint of 2.5-3.0x (about 2.75x).
  • FY2027-29 algorithm: low-single-digit organic sales growth (2.5% in FY2029), mid-single-digit organic operating profit growth, EPS growth ahead of operating profit, about $8bn cumulative free cash flow, ROIC up about 130bps, leverage about 2.0x by FY2029; $1bn of savings ($850m overheads, $150m supply chain), about 40% of the overhead savings in FY2027; overheads from "way north of 14%" of sales to about 10.5%; advertising and promotion (A&P) held at about 16% of sales; US market share: donor in FY2027, holding in FY2028, gaining in FY2029; buybacks mooted from FY2028.
  • FactSet consensus (median, 16 November 2026), FY2027/FY2028/FY2029: revenue $19,109m / $18,988m / $19,720m; EBIT $5,730m / $5,947m / $6,276m (margins 30.0% / 31.3% / 31.8% by our arithmetic); EPS $1.68 / $1.76 / $1.88; free cash flow $2.2bn / $3.2bn / $3.5bn; dividend 51.6c / 55.0c / 60.0c.

Data conflicts the Consumer must resolve when modelling (each is flagged again where it matters).

  1. FY2026 organic operating margin is given as both 29.4% and 28.9% (both attributed to the annual report).
  2. North America's weight: 39% of sales and 50% of operating profit (older segmentation, Deutsche Bank) against about 31% of sales and 40% of profit (Jefferies); FactSet consensus FY2027 regional revenue implies about 36%. The restated five-region segmentation (India now standalone) explains part of the gap.
  3. FY2026 A&P ratio: 16.2% of sales (annual report) against 15.2% (Redburn).
  4. FY2027 framework savings: "40% of $850m" (about $340m) against "about $400m"; supply-chain savings add 25% of $150m (about $38m).
  5. Accelerate savings delivered in FY2026: $540m (against $300m guided) or $514m.
  6. Whether the about $8bn FY2027-29 free cash flow target is struck after the about $850m of restructuring cash (one source says it incorporates it, another that it excludes restructuring and the EABL sale). The FY2027 $2bn is explicitly after restructuring.
  7. Buy ratings: 46% (FactSet universe) against 63% Overweight (Refinitiv, 10 November 2026). Different broker sets.
  8. Brokers' "consensus" in research (Bloomberg FY2027 organic sales +0.5%, organic operating profit +2.5%) is organic; FactSet's lines are reported and include disposals and currency. Never compute growth from FactSet history to FactSet consensus; use its like-for-like line (FY2027 revenue -2.7%, EBITDA +0.6%, EPS +1.6%).

The Investment Analyst's group headings, as written Group: External change forces · Group: Internal change forces · Group: Industry and Value Chain · Group: Company - Competitive advantage · Group: Company - Economic strength · Group: Company - Management · Group: Risks · Group: Guidance · Group: Sell-side estimates · Group: Stock market narrative

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Diageo plc (DGE-GB), the world's largest international spirits company and the owner of Guinness (Johnnie Walker, Smirnoff, Don Julio, Casamigos, Crown Royal, Baileys, Tanqueray; 56% of United Spirits in India; a 34% stake in Moët Hennessy), reporting from fiscal 2027 in five regions (North America, Europe including Africa, Asia Pacific, Latin America and Caribbean, India) plus a Corporate segment; fiscal year ends 30 June.

Request: T-P1

Peers: Pernod Ricard (closest listed spirits peer; Fit for Future programme of EUR 1bn for FY2026-29 after a EUR 900m programme for FY2023-25); Davide Campari-Milano (spirits peer with a 200bp SG&A programme to FY2027 and a recent capacity build, under a new chief executive); Rémy Cointreau (aged-inventory spirits house with high fixed costs; RC Forward, EUR 100m by FY2029); Brown-Forman (American whiskey owner; cost and capacity record through the bourbon downturn); Heineken (beer peer for Guinness; EverGreen gross savings of over EUR 3.5bn since 2020 and how much reached margin); Carlsberg (cost transformation under a chief executive from Unilever, SAIL'22); LVMH Moët Hennessy (Diageo's associate; Hennessy margin compression in 2025-26); Bacardi, Suntory Global Spirits and William Grant & Sons (private competitors with disclosed or reported cost programmes); Tesco 2014-2020 (not a peer: the cost and supplier-terms programme led by Diageo's current chief executive, Sir Dave Lewis). Inputs: What share of Diageo's cost base is fixed (maturation and warehousing, distilleries and breweries, depreciation, regional and central overheads) and what share is variable (raw materials, glass and packaging, freight, A&P)? How did Diageo's organic operating profit move with organic net sales in FY2015 (shipments behind depletions), FY2020-21, FY2023, FY2024 (Latin America destock), FY2025 and FY2026 (when organic A&P fell 13% to about USD 3.2bn, company, August 2026), and how much of each year's profit change came from A&P versus gross profit and overheads? How is the USD 1bn FY2027-29 savings target (USD 850m overheads, USD 150m supply chain; Capital Markets Day, 6 August 2026) phased and split by bucket, and how much is management planning to reinvest in price and brand support rather than retain? What did the Accelerate programme (USD 540m delivered in FY2026 against USD 300m guided, company) deliver by bucket, and how much reached operating profit? Which overhead lines sit behind the target of about 10.5% of net sales (from above 14%), and what overhead ratios do the named peers run? What did comparable programmes at the named peers deliver against promise, over what time, and how much was retained in margin versus reinvested? How cuttable is A&P beyond the cut in non-working and development costs from 21% to 15% of A&P (company, FY2025), and what happened to share and margin at peers that cut brand spend? What is the utilisation of Diageo's distilling and brewing capacity, including the Littleconnell brewery and La Barca distillery, and how will capex guided at about USD 1.25bn a year (against depreciation and amortisation of about USD 0.75bn in FY2026, FactSet) affect future depreciation and margins? Do stranded overheads remain after the disposals of East African Breweries, Guinness Ghana, Guinness Nigeria, Seychelles Breweries, the Royal Challengers Bengaluru cricket team and tail brands, and is there a plan to remove them? How do North America price repositioning (Casamigos recommended retail price cut of 10-15%), ready-to-drink growth and accessible price tiers affect gross margin, and how does the East African Breweries exit change the group margin mix? Focus: How much of Diageo's USD 1bn of savings is likely to reach operating profit by FY2029 rather than be reinvested, judged against what peers' programmes actually retained, and how sensitive operating profit is to continued declines, or a recovery, in North American sales given the fixed cost base.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Davide Campari-Milano (holds price and accepts delistings; gaining US share with Aperol and Espolòn); Pernod Ricard (cut prices on a smaller share of US SKUs; Jameson and Indian whisky pricing); Brown-Forman (American whiskey pricing in an oversupplied category); Rémy Cointreau (cognac pricing after pandemic-era increases and later cuts); LVMH Moët Hennessy (Hennessy VS price cuts); Becle/Jose Cuervo (tequila pricing and agave integration); Heineken (Murphy's stout priced against Guinness in Great Britain); Radico Khaitan and Allied Blenders and Distillers (premium Indian whisky competitors to United Spirits); Bacardi and Sazerac (private; Patrón price cuts, active US whiskey and tequila competitors); E&J Gallo (private; leader in US prepared cocktails). Operating conditions: US spirits price deflation, with most tracked SKUs cutting price in 2026; distributor and retailer destocking under high interest rates; down-trading by squeezed US middle-income consumers; tequila deflation driven by the agave glut; European retailer negotiations, buying alliances and private label (Southern Europe, Germany); input-cost inflation in glass, energy and freight in 2026-27; US import tariffs (10% on EU imports, 50% on certain Canadian imports, the Scotch tariff removed in April 2026, and the US Supreme Court ruling of 20 February 2026); Indian state excise changes (Maharashtra, Karnataka), the UK-India trade agreement and the customs dispute over bulk Scotch; currency devaluation in Mexico, Turkey, India and Brazil; China anti-extravagance rules for baijiu; the travel-retail recovery; a recovery scenario of lower interest rates and rising real incomes. Inputs: What was the split of Diageo's organic growth between volume and price/mix, by region and by category (Scotch, tequila, vodka, Canadian whisky, beer, ready-to-drink), in each year from FY2019 to FY2026 and in Q1 FY2027? Which of Diageo's recent price cuts held or regained volume, by brand and market: Casamigos and Don Julio in the US, Johnnie Walker Black in US control states, Crown Royal, and the three-tier Johnnie Walker architecture in Latin America (company: volume up 28% and gross profit up 18% in H2 FY2026)? What price increases has Guinness achieved over the last three years in Great Britain, Ireland, the US and Africa, and how did volume respond, including against Murphy's? How has pricing behaved in Indian Prestige and Above against the popular segment after the Maharashtra and Karnataka changes, and how much excise was passed through? How quickly, and how fully, has Diageo passed through tariffs and excise in the past, and what are the lags created by US franchise laws, control-state price posting and distributor contracts? What price increases or cuts have Diageo and the named peers announced or are expected to make for 2027 in the US, Great Britain, Europe and India, and how do they compare with expected glass, energy and freight inflation? What do distributors, retailers, bartenders and competitors say about whether Diageo's price resets will trigger a broader price war in tequila and whisky? What do ready-to-drink cans and small packs earn per litre and per serve against 700ml and 750ml bottles, and are they additive or cannibalising? Focus: Whether Diageo's price investment in North America will restore volume and share without setting off a broad industry price war, and in which brands and regions Diageo can still raise prices in 2027-28 to cover tariffs and cost inflation without losing volume.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Diageo's outcome over FY2027-29 depends on whether its cost-funded reset sets off self-reinforcing dynamics (share recovery, deleveraging into buybacks, Guinness capacity and recruitment) or balancing ones (lower marketing intensity, price resets and staff cuts feeding further share loss and competitor retaliation). Peers: Pernod Ricard (cutting A&P while deleveraging; destocking that ends last); Davide Campari-Milano (raising brand spend while gaining share); Rémy Cointreau (price rises then cuts, aged-inventory cycles); Brown-Forman (American whiskey oversupply); Heineken (draught and non-alcoholic beer loops; Murphy's against Guinness); LVMH Moët Hennessy (Hennessy price resets and volume); Becle/Jose Cuervo (tequila category and agave cycle); Carlsberg and AB InBev (deleveraging followed by buybacks and re-rating); Bacardi (private; Patrón and premium tequila pricing). Inputs: Does a lower marketing intensity lead to share loss and then forced promotion? Diageo cut organic A&P by 13% in FY2026 (company) while Campari raised its A&P ratio, and Deutsche Bank (7 October 2026) found that drinks companies that increase brand spend outperform; what is the evidence of the effect on Diageo's share so far? In US tequila, does a price cut lead to volume recovery, or to competitor cuts and category deflation, given the agave glut (Casamigos recommended retail price cut of 10-15% in 2026; Patrón and Hennessy cuts)? Do high distributor carrying costs create a loop of lean trade stocks, shipments below depletions and supplier promotion, and has it ended? Does better service (company target of on-time-in-full delivery of 98% from 84%) lead to fewer out-of-stocks, more shelf space and share, and what are precedents? Does Guinness's draught installation, Guinness 0.0 recruitment (company: 77% of Guinness 0.0 shoppers incremental) and new capacity at Littleconnell compound share, and is Murphy's a balancing loop? Do disposals and the dividend rebase lead to lower leverage, buybacks from FY2028 (mooted by management) and a re-rating, as at Carlsberg and AB InBev? Do headcount cuts and higher staff turnover (company: 20.2% in FY2026 against 16.6%) lead to execution errors and lost distributor relationships? Does premiumisation in India, helped by Karnataka's move to strength-based excise and the UK-India agreement, reinforce United Spirits' Prestige and Above growth? Focus: Which of these loops are operating now, with dated evidence, and which are most likely to strengthen or stall in 2027; above all, whether lower marketing intensity and price resets in North America are producing a reinforcing share-loss loop or a stabilising one, and whether deleveraging will lead to buybacks.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Diageo's EPS over FY2027-30 is affected by several items below operating profit and by perimeter changes (interest and refinancing, tax, United Spirits minorities, Moët Hennessy associate income, the East African Breweries deconsolidation, possible further disposals, hedged currency and buybacks), so these inputs need evidence of their own. Peers: Pernod Ricard (similar leverage, refinancing and currency exposure); Davide Campari-Milano (deleveraging and dividend policy); Rémy Cointreau (leverage near covenants, hedging policy); Heineken and Carlsberg (buyback restarts after deleveraging); LVMH (Moët Hennessy, the source of Diageo's associate income); Brown-Forman (US dollar earnings, capital returns). Inputs: What is Diageo's debt maturity profile to 2029, its fixed and floating mix, and the likely path of net interest given an effective interest rate guided at about 4% for FY2027 (company, November 2026) and the refinancing needs Deutsche Bank highlighted (27 October 2026)? What drives the effective tax rate guided at about 25% and how might it move? What is the outlook for Diageo's share of Moët Hennessy profit, given Hennessy shipments fell 24.2% in 2025 (LVMH) and LVMH wines and spirits margins fell to multi-decade lows? How large are minority interests, mainly United Spirits, and how will the special dividend from the Royal Challengers Bengaluru sale be split between Diageo and minorities? When does East African Breweries deconsolidate, and what revenue, operating profit, minority interest and free cash flow leave with it? What would a sale of the 63% stake in Shui Jing Fang do to EPS and net debt? How do Diageo's transactional hedges (up to 36 months, a 75% coverage target) phase currency effects into FY2028-29, beyond the FY2027 guidance of -USD 100m on net sales and -USD 25m on operating profit? When and at what leverage would buybacks restart, and how large are they likely to be? How much of the USD 1.2bn restructuring charge and other exceptional items is cash, when, and which items are likely to recur? How does capex above depreciation affect future depreciation? Where do sell-side models differ from company guidance on each input? Focus: How net interest, tax, minorities, Moët Hennessy associate income, the East African Breweries deconsolidation and the timing and size of buybacks will bridge Diageo's operating profit to EPS for FY2027 to FY2030, and where sell-side assumptions differ from company guidance.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 24 November 2026, 09:14 to 09:31 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Diageo (DGE-GB): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Written 2026-11-24 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets (predictability; guidance, consensus and narrative).

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Before the answers: bases, conventions and the shape of the model

Bases used throughout (read these before any figure).

  • Money in US dollars (millions unless stated); Diageo's fiscal year ends 30 June. "FY2027" is the year to 30-Jun-27.
  • Revenue is reported net sales (after excise), the FactSet line. EBIT is operating profit before exceptional items (FactSet adjusted EBIT: FY2026 $5,693m, 29.0%). EBITDA is the brokers' basis (FY2026 $6,434m, 32.8%). EPS is pre-exceptional basic EPS, the brokers' basis (FY2026 165.3c; FactSet $1.65).
  • "Organic" is the company's like-for-like basis (excludes currency, acquisitions and disposals). Company guidance is organic; FactSet consensus is reported. I never compute growth from FactSet history to consensus; I use FactSet's like-for-like lines.
  • Where the first set's data conflict (organic margin 29.4% or 28.9%; A&P 16.2% or 15.2% of sales), I use FactSet's 29.0% adjusted EBIT margin and the company's 16.2% A&P ratio.
  • Figures labelled "my base" are my forecasts; everything else carries its source.

The shape of my base case in one paragraph. Reported revenue falls in FY2027 (-3.1%) and FY2028 (-1.7%) because East African Breweries (EABL, about $1.08bn of annual sales) leaves at about 31-Dec-26, then grows 2.2-2.5% a year from FY2029. Organic sales growth is +0.5%, +1.2%, +2.2%, +2.5%, +2.5% (FY2027-31), slightly below the company's path (flat, low-single-digit, 2.5% in FY2029). EBIT margin rises from 29.0% to 30.0%, 31.1%, 31.5%, 31.6%, 31.8%: almost all of the gain comes from overheads (about 40% of the $1bn savings retained) plus about 50bp from the mechanical exit of lower-margin EABL; gross margin falls about 60bp in FY2027 and only recovers to about 59.8% by FY2031. Below EBIT, net finance charges fall from $1,021m to about $750m by FY2029 as net debt falls from $20.5bn to about $14bn. EPS is flat in FY2027 ($1.65) and then compounds about 6% a year ($1.75, $1.86, $1.97, $2.09), close to consensus ($1.68, $1.76, $1.88, $2.01) but built differently: lower revenue and EBIT than consensus from FY2028, offset by more deleveraging, modest buybacks from FY2028 and a lower tax charge on my reading of the tax guidance. Free cash flow (company definition) is $2.2bn, $3.2bn, $3.45bn, $3.7bn, $3.9bn.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

  • Revenue: group organic growth +0.5% (FY2027), +1.2%, +2.2%, +2.5%, +2.5%; volume about +0.8% to +1.3% a year; price/mix -0.3% (FY2027), +0.5% (FY2028), about +1.0-1.2% from FY2029 (no real price increase; North America price investment in FY2027). Perimeter: EABL (about $1.08bn a year of sales) leaves at about 31-Dec-26, removing about $540m in H2 FY2027 and about $540m more in FY2028 (against the H1 FY2027 base); other disposals about -0.3 points in FY2027. Currency -$100m on FY2027 sales (company guidance), neutral afterwards. Shui Jing Fang is kept in the base (a sale is a scenario).
  • EBITDA margin: gross margin 59.8% (FY2026) to 59.2% (FY2027) on North America price investment, ready-to-drink mix and about $70m of net tariffs, recovering to about 59.8% by FY2031 with supply savings and lagged agave relief; A&P held at about 16.3% of sales; overheads and other from about 14.6% of sales to about 11.8% by FY2029 (the $1bn programme: gross $380m, $530m, $95m in FY2027-29, of which about 40% retained in EBIT); organic EBIT +3.0%, +4.5%, +4.0%, +3.0%, +3.0%; transactional currency -$25m, -$50m, -$30m on EBIT (FY2027-29) as hedge gains taper; EABL removes about $105m of EBIT in FY2027 and $210m a year from FY2028. EBIT margin 30.0%, 31.1%, 31.5%, 31.6%, 31.8%. EBITDA margin rises faster than EBIT margin because depreciation rises.
  • Depreciation: rises about $30-35m a year because capex (about $1.25bn) runs well above depreciation (about $0.75bn), partly offset by EABL's exit; Littleconnell (Guinness) capacity comes on stream from 2028.
  • Net financial items: net finance charges (interest at the guided effective rate of about 4.0% in FY2027, rising about 10bp a year with refinancing, on average net borrowings of about $19.0bn, $15.8bn, $14.5bn, $13.9bn, $13.8bn, plus about $140-170m a year of other finance charges for hyperinflation, pensions and lease interest): -$910m, -$797m, -$749m, -$738m, -$744m; plus the share of associates' post-tax profit (mainly the 34% of Moët Hennessy): +$250m, +$270m, +$290m, +$300m, +$310m. The line includes associates because Diageo's own pre-tax profit includes them.
  • Tax rate: 24.0% of pre-tax profit including associates. This equals about 25% of pre-tax profit excluding associates' (already taxed) income, which is how I read the company's guidance of about 25% "excluding associate income from the baseline calculation". If the guidance is instead read on total pre-tax profit, use 25.0% (about -2c of EPS a year).
  • Minorities (not a line in this form): about -$170m (FY2027), -$140m (FY2028), -$150m, -$160m, -$170m: United Spirits (44% public), EABL (35%, until completion; about $94m a year, Bernstein) and Shui Jing Fang (37%). The model's EPS will exceed my cross-check by about these amounts (about 7-8c a share) unless a minorities line is added.
  • Dividend: 30-50% payout policy with a 50c floor; my base holds the payout at about 31-32% while leverage falls and buybacks restart: 51c, 54c, 58c, 62c, 66c.
  • Share count: employee schemes add about 2m shares a year (FactSet basic shares rose 2m a year in FY2024-26). Weighted average shares in my cross-check: 2,225m, 2,221m, 2,193m, 2,146m, 2,085m.
  • Capex: about $1.25bn a year to FY2029 (company guidance; Guinness capacity programme of about $920m over FY2026-30), easing to about $1.2bn.
  • Working capital: net working capital (FactSet basis: receivables plus inventories less payables) builds by about $100m a year in FY2027-28 and about $150m a year from FY2029 (maturing stock grows with the aged-spirits business; the $100m safety stock built for the systems cut-over reverses). EABL's working capital was already classified as held for sale at 30-Jun-26, so it is not in the $6,700m starting balance; the ratio rises from 34.1% to 35.7% in FY2027 and 36.9% in FY2028 mainly because EABL's revenue leaves the denominator, not because of a cash outflow.
  • Lease payments: my estimate from lease liabilities of about $0.7bn (FactSet total debt of $22.2bn less gross borrowings of $21.5bn).
  • Acquisitions less disposals: FY2027 +$3,100m = EABL net proceeds of $2,300m plus about $800m of net cash retained by the group from the sale of the Royal Challengers Bengaluru cricket team (about INR 166.6bn gross to United Spirits, less Indian tax and the 44% minority holders' share of United Spirits' special dividend). The minorities' share is netted here because the form has no minority dividend line. Small bolt-on purchases of $100-200m a year from FY2029.
  • Buybacks: none in FY2027; $0.5bn in FY2028 (after FY2027 leverage is confirmed at or below about 2.75x), $1.0bn in FY2029, $2.0bn a year from FY2030 once leverage is about 2.0x (company target for FY2029).
  • Restructuring cash (not a line in this form): about -$850m in FY2027, -$150m in FY2028 and -$50m in FY2029, plus about -$100m a year of other cash items (share-scheme purchases, pensions, recurring exceptional cash). These are in my free cash flow cross-check but not in the model's lines, so the model's FY2027 free cash flow will exceed mine by roughly $1bn.
  • Half-years: H1 FY2027 organic about +0.3% (Q1 +1.4% including EABL, a weaker Q2 on US distributor stock), EBITDA margin 35.5% (H1 FY2026 35.1%) as the operating framework's savings start; H2 FY2027 organic about +0.8% against the easiest North America comparison, with EABL gone, EBITDA margin 32.2% (H2 FY2026 30.0%) as savings build; H1 FY2028 organic about +1.0% and EABL fully gone, EBITDA margin 37.0% (the 55% overhead tranche).

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

  • Revenue: company "reported net sales" equals FactSet revenue (FY2026 $19,643m). The company guides on organic growth, which excludes currency, acquisitions and disposals (EABL from completion) and caps hyperinflationary price increases; consensus in FactSet is reported. FY2027 bridge: organic +0.5% less EABL (-2.75 points), other disposals (-0.3 points) and currency (-0.5 points) gives reported -3.1%.
  • Operating profit and EBIT: company "operating profit before exceptional items" is close to FactSet adjusted EBIT ($5,693m in FY2026). Reported operating profit ($3,156m in FY2026) is after about $2.5bn of exceptional items (restructuring $752m, impairments about $1.5bn including Don Papa $287m, and other items). FY2027 bridge: organic EBIT +3% (+$171m) less EABL (-$105m), other disposals (-$15m) and currency (-$25m) gives reported EBIT $5,719m (+0.5%).
  • EBITDA: the brokers' basis is pre-exceptional operating profit plus depreciation and amortisation excluding exceptional impairments ($6,434m in FY2026, 32.8%); FactSet's operating EBITDA ($6,438m) is almost identical for Diageo.
  • EPS: the brokers' basis is the company's EPS before exceptional items (165.3c in FY2026; FactSet $1.65). Reported basic EPS was 78.1c. Exceptional items in FY2027 (the rest of the $1.2bn restructuring charge, EABL and cricket-team disposal accounting, including possible recycling of currency translation losses) will keep reported EPS below the pre-exceptional figure.
  • Net finance and associates: Diageo's pre-tax profit is operating profit plus the share of associates' post-tax profit less net finance charges; FactSet's "net interest" ($1,021m in FY2026) includes other finance charges (hyperinflation, pensions, lease interest).
  • Free cash flow: the company definition is net cash from operating activities (after interest, tax and restructuring cash) less net capex, before lease payments, dividends to minorities and disposals; FactSet consensus free cash flow ($2,222m for FY2027) is on a similar basis. FactSet's history line (operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex: $1,349m in FY2026 against the company's $3,211m) does not reconcile and should not be used.
  • Leverage: the company uses adjusted net borrowings over adjusted EBITDA (3.1x at 30-Jun-26 on net borrowings of $20.48bn); FactSet's total debt ($22.2bn) includes leases.

Your cross-check: your own earnings per share and free cash flow for each of the five fiscal years

Cross-check FY2027 (year to 30-Jun-27) FY2028 (year to 30-Jun-28) FY2029 (year to 30-Jun-29) FY2030 (year to 30-Jun-30) FY2031 (year to 30-Jun-31)
Earnings per share 1.65 1.75 1.86 1.97 2.09
Free cash flow 2,180 3,160 3,450 3,730 3,910

Earnings per share is pre-exceptional basic EPS (the brokers' basis), after minorities of about $170m, $140m, $150m, $160m and $170m; the model will show EPS about 7-8c higher each year if it has no minorities line. Free cash flow is the company's definition: after interest, tax, restructuring cash (about $850m in FY2027, $150m in FY2028, $50m in FY2029) and about $100m a year of other cash items, and counting only associates' dividends (about $160-200m a year) rather than their profit; it is before lease payments (about $170m a year), dividends to minorities, disposals and buybacks. The model's free cash flow will therefore differ: about $1bn higher than mine in FY2027 and about $0.1-0.3bn higher or lower thereafter, depending on its treatment of leases and associates.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Diageo plc (DGE-GB), the world's largest international spirits company and the owner of Guinness (Johnnie Walker, Smirnoff, Don Julio, Casamigos, Crown Royal, Baileys, Tanqueray; 56% of United Spirits in India; 63% of Shui Jing Fang in China; a 34% stake in Moët Hennessy); from fiscal 2027 it reports five regions (North America; Europe including Africa; Asia Pacific; Latin America and Caribbean; India) plus a Corporate segment; fiscal year ends 30 June; reports in US dollars.

Request: T-D1

Peers: Pernod Ricard (closest listed spirits peer, running its own EUR 1bn efficiency programme while deleveraging); Davide Campari-Milano (gaining US share while raising A&P and holding price); Rémy Cointreau (aged-inventory house that cut prices after pandemic-era increases, RC Forward programme); Brown-Forman (American whiskey owner managing an oversupplied category); Heineken (EverGreen savings, and owner of Murphy's, the challenger to Guinness in Great Britain); Carlsberg (cost transformation under a chief executive from Unilever, then deleveraging and buybacks); AB InBev (deleveraging from 5.5x followed by buybacks); LVMH Moët Hennessy (Diageo's associate; Hennessy price resets); Becle/Jose Cuervo (tequila pricing through the agave cycle); Bacardi, Sazerac and E&J Gallo (private competitors in premium tequila, American whiskey and US prepared cocktails); Tesco 2014-2020 (not a peer: the cost and supplier-terms turnaround led by Diageo's current chief executive, Sir Dave Lewis). Operating conditions: US spirits price deflation (most tracked SKUs cutting price in 2026); US distributor and retailer destocking under high interest rates; down-trading by squeezed US middle-income consumers; the agave glut and tequila deflation; gluts of American whiskey and cognac; European retailer buying alliances and private label; US import tariffs and the 20 February 2026 Supreme Court ruling; Indian state excise changes and the UK-India trade agreement, including the bulk Scotch customs dispute; emerging-market currency devaluation; a recovery scenario of lower interest rates and rising real incomes. Inputs: Can a company with Diageo's starting point (organic net sales -2.0% in FY2026, North America -8.4%, organic A&P cut 13.1%, share lost in segments covering 65% of the North America portfolio according to Morgan Stanley) cut overheads from above 14% to about 10.5% of net sales (company, Capital Markets Day, 6 August 2026) while moving its US market share from donor in FY2027 to holder in FY2028 and gainer in FY2029 (company targets)? What did Diageo's own record show: Accelerate savings of USD 540m in FY2026 against USD 300m guided, of which USD 230m from A&P and trade investment (company, 6 August 2026); the FY2015 and FY2024 destock cycles; the abandoned 5-7% medium-term target; US share falling from 17.2% (2014) to 15.1% (2025, Jefferies)? Which strengths does Diageo demonstrably have (scale, aged Scotch inventory, Guinness with 13% compound growth FY2023-26, United Spirits' Prestige and Above leadership, cost delivery) and which would it need to acquire (US share-gaining execution, distributor relationships after 20.2% staff turnover, on-time-in-full delivery from 84% to a 98% target, pricing power in tequila)? How plausible is 2.5% organic growth in FY2029 with A&P held at about 16% of sales? What retention of gross savings in operating margin did comparable programmes achieve (Heineken, Pernod Ricard, Campari, Carlsberg Funding our Journey, Rémy Cointreau), and over what time? What happened to share at drinks and staples companies that cut brand spend and then tried to regain share through price? What did Sir Dave Lewis's Tesco turnaround deliver, in what order, and over how long? Focus: Whether a cost-led turnaround that holds A&P at about 16% of sales can stop and reverse Diageo's US share loss by FY2028-29, judged against precedents of large consumer companies attempting the same from a similar starting point, and how much of the USD 1bn of savings such companies kept in their margins.

Layer 3 and Layer 4: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months / Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

Written 25 November 2026, 07:51 to 08:21 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
Diageo (DGE-GB): third and fourth sets of questions

Written 2026-11-25 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo. The third set tests how predictable the forecasts are over the next 18 months; the fourth judges how far our assessment is already reflected in company guidance, sell-side estimates and the market narrative.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Before the answers: bases, what I changed, and the two gaps the model summary flags

Bases. US dollars, millions unless stated; fiscal year to 30 June. "Organic" is the company's like-for-like basis. "EPS" without qualification is pre-exceptional EPS on the brokers' basis, after minority interests (FY2026: 165.3c). "The model" is the Financial Analyst's model, update 1; its EPS is on the Viewforth definition, before minority interests (FY2026: $1.84), so it cannot be read against consensus EPS. "My base" is the second set's base case as amended here. Probabilities are my judgement.

1. One forecast input was wrong and I have changed it: net finance charges (inside "Net financial items"). The second set took FactSet's "net interest expense" of $1,021m for FY2026 as Diageo's net finance charge and projected $910m for FY2027. Five independent checks say the company's own FY2026 charge was about $850m (range $830-900m):

  • the company's effective interest rate of 3.9% on average net borrowings of about $21.2bn is about $827m;
  • with operating profit of $5,693m, associates of about $227m, the company's 24.3% tax rate and EPS of 165.3c, a charge of $1,021m leaves room for only about $90m of minority interests, against the $221m Bernstein reports for FY2026; a charge of about $850m reconciles exactly;
  • the model's own FY2026 actual shows pre-tax profit of $5,107m against EBITA of $5,693m, that is $586m for finance charges, associates and other items together;
  • FactSet consensus FY2027 pre-tax profit less EBIT is -$540m, which with about $245m of associates is a finance charge of about $785m;
  • on my reading of FactSet's definition, its line is interest expense net of capitalised interest, before interest income (the quirks digest warns that FactSet's lines are not the company's headline).

On the second set's input the model's net financial items worsen from -$586m (FY2026 actual) to -$660m (FY2027) in a year in which net debt falls by about $4bn; that cannot be right. My net finance charges are $810m, $705m, $680m, $680m, $700m (FY2027-31), built as the second set built them (the guided effective rate of about 4%, rising 10 basis points a year, on average net borrowings) but with about $50m of other finance charges rather than $150m. With the second set's associates unchanged ($250m rising to $310m), "Net financial items" becomes -560, -435, -390, -380, -390 (was -660, -527, -459, -438, -434). This should be verified against the FY2026 income statement; it is in my first research request.

2. Minority interests are larger than the second set assumed. It used $170m (FY2027) and $140m (FY2028). Bernstein has $221m for FY2026 and forecasts $207m, $221m and $237m for FY2027-29. I use $205m, $200m, $215m, $230m, $245m (United Spirits' 43-44% public holding growing with Indian profit; East African Breweries leaving after the first half of FY2027; a small recovery at Shui Jing Fang). The model has no minorities line (Richard, 16-Oct-26), so this changes the cross-check, not an input.

3. The two corrections largely cancel in EPS, which is itself a finding. My cross-check of EPS on the brokers' basis is:

FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030 FY2031
EBIT (unchanged) 5,717 5,820 6,007 6,201 6,388
Net finance charges -810 -705 -680 -680 -700
Associates +250 +270 +290 +300 +310
Pre-tax profit 5,157 5,385 5,617 5,821 5,998
Net income before minorities (tax 24.0%) 3,919 4,092 4,269 4,424 4,558
Minorities -205 -200 -215 -230 -245
EPS, brokers' basis ($), my base 1.67 1.75 1.85 1.95 2.07
Second set's cross-check 1.65 1.75 1.86 1.97 2.09
FactSet consensus (16-Nov-26) 1.68 1.76 1.88 2.01 none
Model EPS after this update, before minorities (my estimate) 1.76 1.85 1.95 2.09 2.24

FY2027 EPS is $1.67 (growth of about 1%), not flat at $1.65; from FY2028 the figures are within two cents of the second set's. My EPS is on consensus in FY2027-28 and 1.5-3% below it in FY2029-30. I leave the free cash flow cross-check unchanged (about $2.2bn, $3.2bn, $3.5bn, $3.7bn, $3.9bn, company definition): lower cash interest adds about $85m a year, and associates' dividends nearer $110-130m than the $160-200m the second set assumed (the company received $116m in FY2026 against $269m in FY2024) take away about $50-70m.

4. The first flagged gap: FY2027 free cash flow, the Analyst's 2,180 against the model's 3,083. No input is wrong; the model form has no line for three cash items. The model's "equity free cash flow" is net income before minorities, plus depreciation, less capital spending, the change in working capital and lease payments. Against the company's definition it omits about $850m of restructuring cash and about $100m of other recurring cash items, and it counts associates' profit ($250m) rather than their dividends (about $120m); it deducts lease payments ($170m), which the company's definition does not. 2,180 + 850 + 100 + 130 - 170 = 3,090. The gap is definitional and will remain flagged; use the cross-check for free cash flow. What the flag does not show is the consequence for the model's net debt, which has no cross-check beside it: the model's FY2027 net debt of -15,131 is about $1.2bn too low (it would imply leverage of about 2.3 times against the company's own guidance of about 2.75 times), and the understatement grows to about $3bn by FY2031. I have therefore netted the omitted cash items (restructuring cash, the other recurring items and the part of associates' profit not received in cash, plus dividends paid to minority shareholders, about $155m rising to $190m) in "Acquisitions less disposals", as the second set netted the minorities' share of the cricket-team proceeds there: 1,865, -540, -550, -600, -600 (was 3,100, 0, -100, -200, -200). After the update the model's net debt should be about -16.3bn, -15.2bn, -14.5bn, -14.6bn, -14.7bn (FY2027-31), that is leverage of about 2.5, 2.3 and 2.1 times at FY2027-29 on the model's EBITDA. This is slightly more debt than the second set's text (16.5bn, 15.0bn, 14.0bn on the company's basis, which starts $0.3bn higher), because its bridge left out new leases of about $0.15bn a year.

5. The second flagged gap: FY2031 EPS, the Analyst's 2.09 against the model's 2.22. Two causes, neither an input error. (a) Minorities, which the model does not deduct: $170m in the second set's figures is 8 cents; this is the whole gap in FY2027-29. (b) The share count: the model's FY2031 EPS implies about 2,038m shares against the second set's 2,085m. The same $5.5bn of buybacks retires about 195m shares in the model and about 150m in the cross-check, so the model appears to buy at a price in the low-to-mid $20s, near today's, whereas the second set assumed $27-32 (14-15 times earnings). That is worth 5 cents in FY2031 and 2-3 cents in FY2030. The cross-check is the more coherent: a model that re-rates the shares to 21.6 times within 18 months cannot also retire shares at today's price until FY2031.

6. What this does to the return in the model summary (the memo's topic 12). The arithmetic of the summary indicates that the return's "from earnings" part compares consensus EPS today (two-year-forward, about $1.81, after minorities) with the model's own EPS at each horizon (about $2.00 at 31-Dec-27 and $2.22 at 30-Jun-29, before minorities and with the low buyback price). On a like-for-like brokers' basis the earnings part is about +5% to the 18-month column and about +14% to the three-year column, not +10.1% and +22.1%; the rest is the change of basis. At the guideline multiple of 21.6 times, value per share is correspondingly about 5% lower at the 18-month column and about 7% lower at the three-year column than the summary shows. The "Viewforth against consensus" EPS line (+3%) is the same artefact: on the brokers' basis we are in line with consensus, not above it.

7. Requests to the Financial Analyst.

  • The Financial Analyst should deduct minority interests before EPS for this stock, or show EPS after them beside the Viewforth figure, using $205m, $200m, $215m, $230m and $245m for FY2027-31: they are about 5% of net income, consensus EPS and the ten-year multiple bands from which the fair multiple is drawn are after minorities, and without the deduction the return of record is overstated by about 5 points at the 18-month column.
  • The Financial Analyst should add a line for restructuring and other cash items the form does not carry (restructuring cash of 850, 150, 50, 0, 0; other recurring cash items of 100 a year; dividends to minorities of 155, 160, 170, 180, 190; associates' profit not received as dividends of 130, 130, 130, 120, 110, for FY2027-31); if that line is added, the same amounts should be taken back out of "Acquisitions less disposals", restoring 3,100, 0, -100, -200, -200.
  • The Financial Analyst should state the price at which the model retires shares in a buyback and, for this stock, use about 14-15 times forward EPS (about $27-32 over FY2028-31), or the model's own value per share if the re-rating is assumed.
  • The Financial Analyst should confirm whether the return's earnings part compares consensus EPS today with model EPS at the horizon, and if so put both on one basis.

8. Cautions on the AlphaSense reports. They conflict with each other and with the earlier sets in places; where it matters I say so. The restated regional table (the report on the five-region restatement) does not add: its regional operating profits sum to $6.88bn against a group $5.69bn, which is coherent only if Corporate's net cost is about $1.2bn, not the $319m the report prints; the regional margins it gives (North America 38.5%, Europe 36.2%, Asia Pacific 37.3%, Latin America 32.3%, India 19.8% for FY2026) may therefore be right and the second set's regional estimates (Europe 32.5%, Corporate about -$420m) mis-allocated between regions and Corporate, with no effect on the group. One sentence gives the cumulative cash target as $9bn; every other source says about $8bn. The last three rows of the broker table in the financials block are misaligned (Bernstein's row repeats Jefferies' figures) and should not be used.

Appendix The share-price co-movement table the Investment Analyst was given

Co-movement of DGE-GB with its dashboard peers (computed 2026-11-24 from the master dashboard's daily prices)

What this is: daily close-to-close returns over rolling windows of 126 trading days (about six months). Correlation: how closely the daily moves line up (1 = always together, 0 = unrelated). Beta: how far the stock moves for a 1% move in the basket. R-squared: the share of the stock's daily price variance the peer basket explains; the remainder is the stock's own (idiosyncratic) movement. Basket: the peers below, equal-weighted. Prices only; the multiple's own history is in the financials block.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for the memo

Research requests for the next set

Company: Diageo plc (DGE-GB), the world's largest international spirits company and the owner of Guinness (Johnnie Walker, Smirnoff, Don Julio, Casamigos, Crown Royal, Baileys, Tanqueray; 56% of United Spirits in India; 63% of Shui Jing Fang in China; a 34% stake in Moët Hennessy); from fiscal 2027 it reports five regions (North America; Europe including Africa; Asia Pacific; Latin America and Caribbean; India) plus a Corporate segment; fiscal year ends 30 June; reports in US dollars.

Request: OWN-1

Question: For Diageo plc (DGE-GB), what do the company-compiled consensus and individual brokers expect for the half-year results to 31 December 2026, due on 10 February 2027, and on what basis are published forecasts prepared: what are the expectations for organic net sales growth and organic operating profit growth for the group and for each of the five regions, gross margin, advertising and promotion, overheads, operating margin by region, pre-exceptional earnings per share, free cash flow, restructuring cash paid, and net borrowings and leverage at 31 December 2026; which brokers' published FY2027-29 forecasts still include East African Breweries for the full period and which remove it from completion, and what are each broker's pro-forma FY2027-29 net sales, operating profit and earnings per share after the East African Breweries and Royal Challengers Bengaluru disposals (Morgan Stanley published pro-forma FY2027 net sales of USD 18,603m against USD 19,688m as published, and operating profit of USD 5,444m against USD 5,654m, on 14 September 2026); what is the latest status and expected completion date of each disposal, including the Kenyan court and regulatory steps, the approval of the Board of Control for Cricket in India, the tax on the cricket-team sale and United Spirits' decision on a special dividend; and what did Diageo report for FY2026, before exceptional items, for net finance charges (net interest and other finance charges shown separately, with interest income), the share of after-tax results of associates, the tax charge and its basis, and the profit attributable to non-controlling interests (by subsidiary where disclosed), and what does each broker forecast for each of those lines for FY2027-29?

Request: OWN-2

Question: What is Diageo plc's (DGE-GB) exposure to United States trade measures on Canadian, Mexican, European Union and British spirits and beer as they stand in late 2026 and as they may change during 2027: what are the scope, legal basis, effective date and current status of the presidential proclamation under Section 338 of the Tariff Act of 1930 restricting certain Canadian alcoholic imports (reported by McDermott Will & Schulte on 18 November 2026), and what does it mean for Crown Royal (where it is distilled, matured and bottled, how much is shipped in bulk, what stock is held in the United States, and what Diageo, distributors and brokers have said about the effect and about mitigation); which tariffs replaced those struck down by the Supreme Court on 20 February 2026, including the tariffs imposed under Section 122 of the Trade Act of 1974 and their expiry, and how do they compare with the rates assumed in Diageo's FY2027 guidance (USD 100m gross, about USD 70m after mitigation, assuming 10% on European Union imports and 50% on certain Canadian imports; company, 5 November 2026); has Diageo claimed or received refunds of tariffs paid before the ruling, as Campari and Rémy Cointreau have reported, how large could a claim be, and how would it be accounted for; what is the status of the review of the United States-Mexico-Canada Agreement and the treatment of tequila and Canadian whisky under it; and how do brokers and rating agencies size the effect on Diageo's North America operating profit under each outcome, compared with the effect on Pernod Ricard, Campari, Brown-Forman, Becle and Rémy Cointreau?

Request: OWN-3

Question: How are investors positioned in Diageo plc (DGE-GB) and in European spirits and brewing shares in late 2026, and what have comparable recoveries been worth to shareholders: what do brokers' positioning surveys, fund-ownership and fund-flow data, changes in the largest shareholders, short-interest and options data, activist reports, and published investor letters and commentary say about who has bought and sold Diageo since its 2026 share-price low, their reasons, and what they expect from the half-year results on 10 February 2027; what do strategists expect for the valuation of European beverage shares in 2027 after the 2026 re-rating (for example Deutsche Bank's "Beverages 2027 outlook: More froth than fizz", 25 November 2026); and in earlier recoveries led by cost savings, debt reduction and a return to share buybacks rather than by sales growth (Diageo after its 2014-15 destocking, Pernod Ricard after 2018, AB InBev in 2023-26, Heineken in 2024-26, Carlsberg in 2015-19, Tesco in 2014-20, and consumer-staples companies such as Unilever, Reckitt, Kraft Heinz and Molson Coors), what forward price-to-earnings multiple did the shares trade on at the low, when guidance was first met, when buybacks restarted, and two to three years later, how long did any re-rating take, how much organic sales growth was being delivered when the multiple returned to its long-run median, and where does each broker's target multiple for Diageo sit against those precedents?
